Foreword

Lao She (1899-1966), originally named Shu Qingchun, courtesy name She Yu, was from Beijing. He lost his father at a young age and grew up in poverty, deeply influenced by the life, ideas, and aesthetic tastes of the lower-class common people in old Beijing. In 1924, he went to England to teach and began his literary career, attracting attention with his humorous style. After returning to China in 1930, he wrote works such as "Camel Xiangzi" and "Divorce," establishing his position as the foremost writer of urban common people's literature in modern literary history. During the Anti-Japanese War, he presided over the work of the All-China Literary and Art Circles Anti-Enemy Association and wrote the million-word novel "Four Generations Under One Roof." After the founding of New China, he produced powerful plays such as "Dragon Beard Ditch" and "Teahouse," earning him the title of "People's Artist."

The novel tells the tragic story of Xiangzi, a rickshaw puller in old Beijing.

Xiangzi drifted from the countryside to the city and became a rickshaw puller. He was young and strong, had no bad habits, and was full of confidence in the future. His only wish was to buy his own rickshaw and become an "independent" and "free" puller. However, the setbacks of buying a rickshaw three times, only to lose it each time, shattered his life's ideals and plunged him into despair.

In terms of relationships, Xiangzi also lost his autonomy. Huniu, the daughter of Liu Siye, the rickshaw depot owner, liked Xiangzi. Although Xiangzi disliked her for being old and ugly, he couldn't resist her seduction and married her. After marriage, Xiangzi used Huniu's private money to buy an old rickshaw from Erqiangzi. But not long after, Huniu died in childbirth, and Xiangzi had to sell the rickshaw to bury her. This family tragedy left Xiangzi heartbroken. After Huniu's death, Xiaofuzi's meticulous care made him genuinely fall in love with this unfortunate girl. He seemed to regain the courage to live, but fate still enjoyed playing tricks on him. Forced by circumstances, Xiaofuzi became a prostitute and hanged herself before Xiangzi could "make good." This shattered Xiangzi's last glimmer of hope.

From then on, Xiangzi completely collapsed and degenerated. He indulged in drinking, gambling, and whoring, engaged in petty theft and trickery, and even committed murder, until he finally became a social outcast, a walking corpse, waiting for death.

"Camel Xiangzi" uses Beijing colloquialisms and dialects, along with descriptions of old Beijing's local customs and traditions, making it feel intimate, natural, and easy to read. It is a classic work of modern vernacular fiction.

One

We are going to talk about Xiangzi, not the camel. "Camel" is just Xiangzi's nickname. So, let's talk about Xiangzi first. As for the connection between "Camel" and Xiangzi, we'll just mention it briefly here and leave it at that.

There were many types of rickshaw pullers in Beiping: the young and strong, with nimble legs, who preferred to rent beautiful rickshaws and pull "all day," free to start and stop whenever they wished. They would park their rickshaws at designated "rickshaw stands"[1]

 or outside mansions, waiting specifically for customers who wanted a fast ride. If things went well, they might earn one or two yuan in one go; if they were unlucky, they might waste a whole day and not even cover the "rickshaw rental fee," but they didn't care. These fellows generally had two hopes: either to be hired for a long-term contract or to buy their own rickshaw. Once they owned their rickshaw, it didn't matter much whether they pulled for a monthly contract or individual fares, as the rickshaw was theirs.

Those a bit older than this group, or those who ran a bit slower due to physical reasons, or those who dared not waste a day due to family reasons, mostly pulled rickshaws that were 80% new. Both the person and the rickshaw were quite presentable, so they could maintain a certain dignity when quoting prices. These pullers might work "all day" or "half day." In the latter case, because they still had considerable energy, they would always "pull at night"[2]

 regardless of winter or summer. At night, of course, more caution and skill were required than during the day; naturally, they also earned more money.

Those aged over forty or under twenty probably found it difficult to find a place in the first two groups. Their rickshaws were dilapidated, and they dared not "pull at night," so they could only go out early, hoping to earn enough for the "rickshaw rental fee" and their own food from early morning until three or four in the afternoon. Their rickshaws were old and slow, so they had to walk more and charge less. They were the ones who went to the melon market, fruit market, and vegetable market to pull goods; the money was little, but they didn't need to run fast.

Here, those under twenty—some started this line of work as early as eleven or twelve—rarely managed to become handsome rickshaw pullers after twenty, because they were injured in their youth and found it hard to become strong. They might pull rickshaws their whole lives, and never once make a name for themselves even in rickshaw pulling. As for those over forty, some had been pulling rickshaws for ten or eight years, and their muscle deterioration made them content to lag behind others. They gradually realized that sooner or later, one fall would kill them on the road. Their pulling posture, their quick wit when bargaining, their shortcuts and detours when walking, all reminded them of past glories, and they would fan their nostrils at the younger generation. But this bit of glory could not diminish the darkness of the future in the slightest, and they themselves often sighed softly while wiping sweat. However, compared to some other rickshaw pullers in their forties, they didn't seem to have suffered to the extreme. These were people who had never imagined they would have anything to do with rickshaws, and only when the line between life and death became blurred did they pick up the rickshaw shafts. Discharged policemen or school janitors, small vendors who had squandered their capital, or unemployed artisans, when they had nothing left to sell or pawn, gritted their teeth and, with tears in their eyes, embarked on this road to death. These people had already sold the most vibrant period of their lives, and now they were dripping the sweat, transformed from steamed buns, onto the road. They had no strength, no experience, no friends, and even among their peers, they received no kindness. They pulled the most dilapidated rickshaws, their tires deflating countless times a day; while pulling people, they also had to beg for forgiveness, even though fifteen large copper coins was considered a sweet deal.

In addition, due to unique circumstances and knowledge, some rickshaw pullers formed separate factions. Those born in Xiyuan Haidian naturally found it more convenient to go to Xishan, Yanjing, and Tsinghua; similarly, those outside Andingmen went to Qinghe and Beiyuan; those outside Yongdingmen went to Nanyuan... These were long-distance pullers who were unwilling to take short fares; because one trip was one trip, they disdained accumulating three or five copper coins. However, they were not as enduring as the rickshaw pullers of Dongjiaomin Lane. These specialized in pulling foreign customers and prided themselves on pulling all the way from Jiaomin Lane to Yuquan Mountain, the Summer Palace, or Xishan in one go. Endurance was a minor matter; the main reason why ordinary rickshaw pullers could not compete for this business was largely because these "foreign-fed" pullers had some unique knowledge—they could speak foreign languages. They knew about the Summer Palace, Yonghegong, and the "Eight Great Hutongs" that British and French soldiers talked about. They had their own set of foreign words, which they did not teach to others. Their pulling style was also special: a steady, unhurried pace, heads down, eyes straight ahead, walking close to the roadside, exuding an air of being detached from the world yet possessing specialized skills. Because they pulled foreigners, they could wear long-sleeved white jackets instead of vests, with white or black trousers, extra wide trouser legs tied at the ankles with thin straps; on their feet were wide, double-layered, thousand-layered cloth shoes; clean, neat, and spirited. Upon seeing such attire, other rickshaw pullers would not come over to compete for customers or race, as they seemed to belong to a different profession.

With this simple analysis, our description of Xiangzi's position will be as precise as describing—we hope—a certain nail on a machine. Xiangzi, before he became associated with the nickname "Camel," was a relatively free rickshaw puller. This means he belonged to the category of young, strong pullers who owned their own rickshaws: his own rickshaw, his own life, all in his own hands, a high-class rickshaw puller. This was by no means an easy feat. One, two, at least three or four years; one drop of sweat, two drops of sweat, countless tens of thousands of drops of sweat, just to earn that rickshaw. Through gritting his teeth in wind and rain, through self-deprivation in food and drink, he earned that rickshaw. That rickshaw was the culmination and reward of all his struggles and hardships, like a medal on a battle-hardened warrior. When he rented someone else's rickshaw, from morning till night, from east to west, from south to north, he was like a spinning top whipped by others; he had no self. But in this rotation, his eyes did not blur, his mind did not wander; he always thought of a distant rickshaw, one that could make him free, independent, a rickshaw like his own hands and feet. With his own rickshaw, he would no longer have to endure the temper of those who tied up rickshaws, nor would he need to humor others; with his own strength and rickshaw, he could earn a living as soon as he opened his eyes.

He was not afraid of hardship, nor did he have the excusable but undesirable bad habits of ordinary rickshaw pullers. His intelligence and diligence were enough to make his aspirations a reality. If his circumstances had been better, or if he had received more education, he certainly would not have ended up in the "rubber gang," and no matter what he did, he would never have squandered his opportunities. However, he had to pull a rickshaw; well, even in this profession, he proved his ability and intelligence. It was as if he could be a good ghost even in hell. Born in the countryside, having lost his parents and a few meager acres of land, he came to the city at the age of eighteen. With the strength and honesty of a country lad, he had done almost every job that involved physical labor for food. But soon he realized that pulling a rickshaw was an easier way to earn money; other hard labor had limited income; pulling a rickshaw offered more variety and opportunities, and he never knew when or where he might encounter rewards beyond his expectations. Of course, he also knew that such opportunities were not entirely accidental, but required both the person and the rickshaw to be presentable and spirited, to have something to sell to attract discerning customers. After some thought, he believed he had the qualifications: he had strength, and he was still young; the only thing he lacked was experience, and he dared not start by pulling a beautiful rickshaw right away. But this was not an insurmountable difficulty. With his body and strength as a foundation, he only needed to try for ten days or half a month, and he would surely get the hang of it. Then he could rent a new rickshaw, and perhaps soon he could get a long-term contract. Then, by saving and being frugal for one or two years, even three or four years, he would surely be able to buy his own rickshaw, the most beautiful rickshaw! Looking at his strong muscles, he thought this was only a matter of time, an aspiration and goal that was sure to be achieved, not just a dream!

His physique and muscles developed ahead of his age; at around twenty, he was already very tall and large. Although his limbs had not yet been molded into a fixed pattern by the years, he already looked like an adult—an adult with an innocent and mischievous look on his face and body. Watching the high-class rickshaw pullers, he planned how to cinch his waist to better display his iron-fan-like chest and straight, stiff back; he turned his head to look at his shoulders, how broad, how majestic! After cinching his waist, he would wear wide-legged white trousers, with the cuffs tied with chicken-gut-like straps, revealing his "oversized" feet! Yes, he could undoubtedly become the most outstanding rickshaw puller; he laughed to himself like a fool. He wasn't particularly handsome, but what made him endearing was the spirit on his face. His head wasn't very big, he had round eyes, a fleshy nose, two short and thick eyebrows, and his head was always shaved shiny. There was no excess flesh on his cheeks, but his neck was almost as thick as his head; his face was always flushed, and particularly bright was a not-so-small scar between his cheekbone and right ear—he was bitten by a donkey while sleeping under a tree when he was a child. He didn't pay much attention to his appearance; he loved his face just as he loved his body, both so sturdy and strong; he seemed to count his face among his limbs, as long as it was strong. Yes, after coming to the city, he could still stand on his head for half a day. Standing like that, he felt, he was very much like a tree, with no part from top to bottom that wasn't firm and upright.

He was indeed somewhat like a tree, sturdy, silent, yet full of vitality. He had his own plans, some thoughts, but he wasn't good at discussing them with others. Among rickshaw pullers, individual grievances and difficulties were public fodder; at "rickshaw stands," in small teahouses, in large courtyards, everyone reported, described, or complained about their own affairs, and then these affairs became common property, spreading from one place to another like folk songs. Xiangzi was a countryman,His speech was not as agile as that of city dwellers; if eloquence came from genius, he was naturally disinclined to speak much, and thus unwilling to learn the glib and malicious tongues of city folk. He knew his own affairs and disliked discussing them with others. Because his mouth was often idle, he had time to think, and his eyes seemed to always be looking into his own heart. Once his mind was made up, he would follow the path he had opened in his heart; if the path proved impassable, he could go a day or two without a sound, gritting his teeth as if biting his own heart! He decided to pull a rickshaw, and so he went to pull a rickshaw. He rented a broken rickshaw and first practiced his legs. The first day he didn't earn much money. Business was good on the second day, but after lying down for two days, his ankles were swollen like two gourds, and he couldn't lift them anymore. He endured it, no matter how painful. He knew this was inevitable, a necessary hurdle for pulling a rickshaw. Until he passed this hurdle, he couldn't run with abandon.

After his feet healed, he dared to run. This made him very happy, because there was nothing else to fear: he was very familiar with the place names, and even if he sometimes took a detour, it didn't matter much, as he had plenty of strength. The method of pulling a rickshaw, understood through his experience of pushing, pulling, carrying, and shouldering, was not too difficult. Moreover, he had his own idea: pay more attention, compete less, and generally, there wouldn't be any problems. As for bargaining and competing for passengers, his slow speech and strong temper meant he couldn't outdo those old hands. Knowing this weakness, he simply didn't go to the "rickshaw stands" much; he would park wherever there were no rickshaws. In these quiet spots, he could bargain leisurely, and sometimes he wouldn't even name a price, just saying: "Get in, just pay what you think is fair!" His appearance was so honest, his face so simple and lovable, that people seemed to have no choice but to trust him, not daring to think that this big, simple man would cheat them. Even if people were suspicious, they could only suspect that he was a country bumpkin new to the city, probably didn't know the way, and therefore couldn't name a price. When people asked, "Do you know the way?", he would smile, as if playing dumb and being coy at the same time, leaving people unsure how to react.

In two or three weeks, he had trained his legs. He knew his running style was very graceful. Running style was evidence of a rickshaw puller's ability and qualification. Those who splayed their feet, like a pair of cattail-leaf fans fanning the ground, were undoubtedly newcomers from the countryside. Those who lowered their heads very deeply, dragging their feet, running at almost the same speed as walking, but with the appearance of running, were the old men over fifty. Those with ample experience but little strength had another method: their chests were deeply caved in; their legs were lifted very high; they would take a step and then crane their necks; this way, they gave the impression of running very hard, but in fact, they were no faster than anyone else; they relied on "showmanship" to maintain their dignity. Xiangzi, of course, would never adopt these postures. His legs were long, his strides large, his waist very steady, and he ran with little noise, each step having some elasticity, the handlebars not moving, making the passenger feel safe and comfortable. When told to stop, no matter how fast he was running, he would lightly drag his big feet on the ground twice and stop; his strength seemed to reach all parts of the rickshaw. His back slightly bent, his hands loosely gripping the handlebars, he was active, nimble, and precise; he didn't appear hurried but ran very fast, fast yet without danger. Even among those who pulled private rickshaws, this was considered very valuable.

He got a new rickshaw. From the day he got the new rickshaw, he understood clearly that a rickshaw like the one he rented—with soft springs, genuine brass fittings, a large canvas curtain, double lamps, and a slender-necked brass horn—was worth over a hundred; if the paintwork and brass fittings contained a bit of iron, a hundred yuan would be enough. Roughly speaking, he could get a rickshaw with just a hundred yuan. Suddenly, he thought, if he could save a dime a day, a hundred yuan would be a thousand days, a thousand days! Piling a thousand days together, he almost couldn't calculate how far away that was. But he made up his mind, a thousand days, even ten thousand days, he had to buy a rickshaw! His first step, he thought, should be to pull a private rickshaw. If he encountered a master with many social engagements and dinner parties, averaging ten or so dinner parties a month, he could earn two or three yuan in extra meal money. Plus, if he saved another eighty cents or perhaps three to five yuan each month, he could save fifty or sixty yuan a year! This way, his hope would be much closer. He didn't smoke, drink, gamble, had no vices, no family burdens; as long as he was willing to grit his teeth, nothing was impossible. He swore to himself that in a year and a half, he—Xiangzi—must buy his own rickshaw! It had to be newly built, not an old one that had seen new ones.

He really did get a monthly contract. However, reality didn't fully support his hopes. It's true, he did grit his teeth, but after a year and a half, he hadn't fulfilled that wish. He did get a contract, and he was cautious and careful with his work; unfortunately, things in the world are not one-sided. He was careful on his part, but the employer didn't necessarily keep him; it might be two or three months, or even ten days or eight days, and then he'd be fired! He had to find another job. Naturally, while looking for a job, he also had to pull occasional passengers; like riding a horse to find a horse, he couldn't afford to be idle. During these times, he often made mistakes. He still forced himself to stay energetic, not just to earn a day's living [3], but also to continue saving money to buy a rickshaw. But forcing oneself to be energetic is never a good thing: when pulling a rickshaw, he couldn't concentrate on running, as if he was always thinking about something, and the more he thought, the more afraid he became, and the more resentful. If this continued, when would he be able to buy a rickshaw? Why was it like this? Was he not strong-willed? While thinking these chaotic thoughts, he forgot his usual caution. The rubber tire ran over broken copper and porcelain shards, causing a flat tire; he had to stop pulling. More seriously, sometimes he bumped into pedestrians, and once, in his eagerness to squeeze through, he even knocked off a hubcap. If he had been pulling a private rickshaw, these mistakes would never have happened; once he stopped working, he felt unhappy and became a bit dazed. Damaging the rickshaw naturally meant paying for repairs; this made him even more anxious, adding fuel to the fire; for fear of causing greater trouble, he sometimes slept lazily for an entire day. By the time he opened his eyes, a whole day had passed in vain, and he regretted and hated himself. Moreover, during this period, the more anxious he became, the more he tormented himself, and his eating and drinking became more irregular; he thought he was made of iron, but it turned out he could also get sick. When he got sick, he was reluctant to spend money on medicine, enduring it himself; as a result, his illness became more severe, and not only did he have to buy medicine, but he also had to rest for several days straight. These difficulties made him grit his teeth and work harder, but the money for the rickshaw did not accumulate any faster because of it. After a full three years, he had saved a hundred yuan!

He couldn't wait any longer. His original plan was to buy the most complete, newest, and most desirable rickshaw, but now he had to settle for what a hundred yuan could buy. He couldn't wait; what if something happened and he lost a few more yuan! Coincidentally, a newly built rickshaw (custom-made but not picked up due to lack of funds) was quite similar to the one he had hoped for; it was originally worth over a hundred, but because the deposit was forfeited, the rickshaw shop was willing to sell it for a little less. Xiangzi's face turned red, his hands trembling, as he pulled out ninety-six yuan: "I want this rickshaw!" The shop owner intended to round up the price, saying countless words, pulling the rickshaw out and then back in, setting up the canopy, then lowering it, pressing the horn, each action accompanied by a string of the best adjectives; finally, he kicked the steel spokes twice, "Listen to the sound, like a bell! Take it away, even if you pull the rickshaw to pieces, if one steel spoke softens, bring it back and throw it in my face! A hundred yuan, if it's a penny less, we're done!" Xiangzi counted the money again: "I want this rickshaw, ninety-six!" The shop owner knew he had met a straightforward person, looked at the money, looked at Xiangzi, and sighed: "Let's be friends, the rickshaw is yours; six months warranty: unless you smash the main body, I'll repair it for free; here's the warranty, take it!"

Xiangzi's hands trembled even more violently. He tucked away the warranty, pulled the rickshaw, and almost cried. He pulled it to a secluded spot and carefully examined his rickshaw, trying to see his face reflected in the lacquered panel! The more he looked, the more he loved it; even the parts that didn't quite meet his ideal could be forgiven, because it was now his own rickshaw. After admiring the rickshaw, he seemed to feel it could rest for a while, so he sat on the new foot mat, looking at the shining brass horn on the handlebars. He suddenly remembered that he was twenty-two this year. Because his parents died early, he had forgotten his birthday. Since coming to the city, he had never celebrated a birthday. Well, today he bought a new rickshaw, so let's call it his birthday; it's both his and the rickshaw's, easy to remember, and since the rickshaw was his own hard work, there was simply no reason not to consider the person and the rickshaw as one.

How to celebrate this "double birthday"? Xiangzi had an idea: the first customer must be a well-dressed person, absolutely not a woman. Ideally, he would pull them to Qianmen, or second best, Dong'an Market. Once he got there, he should eat a good meal at the best food stall, something like hot sesame cakes with stir-fried lamb. After eating, if there was good business, he would pull one or two more; if not, he would put the rickshaw away; it was his birthday!

Since he got this rickshaw, his life became more and more energetic. Whether pulling monthly contracts or occasional passengers, he didn't have to worry about the "rickshaw fee" every day; all the money he earned was his own. Feeling good inside, he was more amiable to people, and business went more smoothly. After half a year, his hopes grew even larger: if things continued this way, in two years, at most two years, he could buy another rickshaw, one, two... he could even open a rickshaw factory!

However, most hopes are dashed, and Xiangzi's was no exception.

[1]

 Rickshaw stand, i.e., a parking spot.

[2]

 Pulling late, means starting work after 4 PM and continuing until before dawn.

[3]

 Chew grain, i.e., food.

Two

Since buying the rickshaw, Xiangzi ran even faster. He also became a bit more agile, and his courage grew. But he was always extra careful with his rickshaw, yet when he looked at himself and then at his rickshaw, he felt something wasn't right if he didn't run fast.

Xiangzi had grown over an inch taller since coming to the city. He himself felt it, as if he was still growing taller. Indeed, his skin and appearance had become tougher and more defined, and he already had a small mustache on his upper lip; but he thought he should still grow taller. When he had to duck his head significantly to enter a small house door or street gate, although he said nothing, he secretly delighted in it, because he was already so tall, and felt he was still growing, he seemed to be both an adult and a child, very interesting.

Such a big man, pulling such a beautiful rickshaw, his own rickshaw, with springs so soft they trembled, even the handlebars moved slightly; the carriage was so shiny, the cushion so white, the horn so loud; how could he not run fast and be worthy of himself, how could he not be worthy of that rickshaw? This was not vanity, but rather a sense of responsibility; not running fast, flying fast, was not enough to fully display his strength and the rickshaw's elegance. That rickshaw was truly lovely; after half a year of pulling it, it seemed to have developed consciousness and feelings everywhere; with Xiangzi's twist of the waist, squat of the leg, or straightening of the back, it would immediately respond, giving Xiangzi the most comfortable assistance, with no awkwardness or disconnect between them. When they encountered a flat, uncrowded area, Xiangzi could hold the handlebars with one hand, and the slightly rustling rubber wheels would urge him on like a swift, gentle breeze, fast and steady. When they reached their destination, Xiangzi's clothes and trousers would be wringing wet with sweat, gushing, as if he had just been pulled out of a washbasin. He felt tired, but it was a very satisfying, proud kind of fatigue, like riding a famous horse for dozens of miles. If being bold wasn't carelessness, Xiangzi was certainly not careless when running with abandon. If not running fast was letting people down, then running fast and damaging the rickshaw was letting himself down. The rickshaw was his life, and he knew how to be careful. With caution and boldness combined, he became increasingly confident, deeply believing that both he and the rickshaw were made of iron.

Therefore, he not only dared to run with abandon, but also didn't give much thought to when to go out. He felt that pulling a rickshaw with effort to earn a living was the most honorable thing in the world; he was willing to go out, and no one could stop him. He didn't pay much attention to rumors outside, such as soldiers arriving in Xiyuan, fighting breaking out in Changxindian, arrests being made outside Xizhimen, or Qihuamen being closed for half a day. Naturally, when shops on the street had already closed, and the roads were filled with armed police and security forces, he also didn't deliberately seek trouble, and like others, quickly put his rickshaw away. However, he didn't believe the rumors. He knew how to be cautious, especially because the rickshaw was his own, but he was ultimately a countryman, not like city dwellers who believed every rumor. Furthermore, his physique made him believe that even if he unfortunately ran into trouble, he would surely find a way out and wouldn't suffer too great a loss; he wasn't easily bullied, with such a large build and broad shoulders!

News and rumors of war grew almost annually with the spring wheat; wheat ears and bayonets could be considered symbols of hope and fear for northerners. When Xiangzi's new rickshaw was just half a year old, it was precisely the season when wheat needed spring rain.Spring rain does not necessarily fall according to people's wishes, but war always comes whether anyone wishes for it or not. Rumors, true stories, Xiangzi seemed to have forgotten that he once worked as a farmer; he didn't care much about how war destroyed fields, nor did he pay much attention to whether there was spring rain. He only cared about his rickshaw, which could produce pancakes and all kinds of food. It was an all-purpose field, obediently following him, a living, precious land. Because of the lack of rain and the news of war, food prices had risen; Xiangzi knew this. But like the city dwellers, he could only complain about expensive food, without any solution; food is expensive, so be it, who can make it cheap? This attitude made him only care about his own life, putting all misfortunes and disasters out of his mind.

If city people had no solutions for anything, they would spread rumors—sometimes completely baseless, sometimes exaggerating a small truth into a big one—to show that they were neither foolish nor idle. They were like small fish, putting their mouths on the water's surface when idle, blowing out a few completely useless bubbles, and feeling quite pleased. Among the rumors, those about war were the most interesting. Other rumors often remained rumors, like talking about ghosts or foxes, never actually encountering a ghost just by talking about it. But with war, precisely because there was no accurate news, rumors could have an immediate effect. In minor details, there might be a big discrepancy with the truth, but regarding the existence of war itself, it was correct nine times out of ten. "There's going to be a war!" Once this sentence was uttered, war would surely come sooner or later; as for who would fight whom, and how, that was a matter of individual opinion. Xiangzi was not unaware of this. However, laborers—including rickshaw pullers—though they wouldn't welcome war, didn't necessarily suffer misfortune when it came. Whenever war broke out, the most anxious were the wealthy. As soon as they heard bad news, they quickly wanted to flee for their lives; money made them come quickly and run quickly. But they themselves couldn't run, because their legs were too heavy with money. They had to hire many people to be their legs, boxes had to be carried, old and young, men and women had to be pulled by rickshaws; at this time, the hands and feet of those who specialized in manual labor became expensive: "Qianmen, East Station!" "Where?" "East—Station!" "Oh, just give me one yuan forty cents! No bargaining, it's wartime!"

It was under these circumstances that Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw out of the city. Rumors had been circulating for about ten days, and prices had already risen, but the war seemed still far away, not likely to reach Beiping anytime soon. Xiangzi continued to pull his rickshaw as usual, not slacking off because of the rumors. One day, pulling to the West City, he noticed something amiss. At the west entrance of Huguosi Street and Xinjiekou, no one was calling out, "Xiyuan? Tsinghua?" He lingered around Xinjiekou for a while. He heard that rickshaws no longer dared to leave the city, and that outside Xizhimen, all kinds of rickshaws—large, small, mule-drawn, foreign-style—were being seized. He thought of having a bowl of tea and then heading south; the quietness at the rickshaw stand revealed a real danger. He had considerable courage, but it was not advisable to deliberately walk into a dead end. Just at this critical moment, two rickshaws came from the south, with what looked like students inside. The rickshaw pullers, as they walked, shouted, "Anyone going to Tsinghua? Hey, Tsinghua!"

The few rickshaws at the stand didn't respond. Some looked at the two rickshaws with a faint, unannoyed smile, others sat smoking small pipes, not even raising their heads. The two rickshaws continued to shout, "Are you all mute? Tsinghua!"

"Two yuan, I'll go!" A short, young bald man, seeing no one else speak, jokingly agreed. "Pull over! Find another one!" The two rickshaws stopped.

The young bald man was stunned for a moment, seemingly unsure what to do. The others still didn't move. Xiangzi realized that leaving the city must be dangerous, otherwise, why would no one snatch up a two-yuan fare to Tsinghua—which was usually only twenty or thirty cents? He didn't want to go either. But the bald young man seemed to have made up his mind; if someone would accompany him on the trip, he would go for it; he spotted Xiangzi: "Big guy, what about you?"

The three words "Big guy" made Xiangzi smile; it was a compliment. His mind started to turn: with such praise, it seemed he should indeed help out the short, bold bald man; besides, two yuan was two yuan, not something you encountered every day. Danger? Could it be that coincidental? Moreover, two days ago someone said the Temple of Heaven was full of soldiers; he had seen with his own eyes that there wasn't even a single soldier there. Thinking this, he pulled his rickshaw over.

When they reached Xizhimen, there were almost no pedestrians in the city gate. Xiangzi's heart grew a little cold. The bald man also saw that things were not good, but still smiled and said, "Go for it, partner! If it's fortune, it's not misfortune; today is the day!" Xiangzi knew things were going to be bad, but having been on the streets for these past few years, he couldn't go back on his word, couldn't act like an old woman!

After leaving Xizhimen, they truly didn't encounter a single rickshaw; Xiangzi lowered his head, not daring to look left or right on the road. His heart felt like it was pressing against his ribs. When they reached Gaoliang Bridge, he glanced around and saw no soldiers, which relieved him a little. Two yuan was two yuan after all, he mused, how could one find such a good deal without some courage? He usually didn't like to talk, but at this moment, he wanted to say a few words to the short bald man; the street was eerily quiet. "Shall we take the dirt road? The main road—" "No need to say," the short man guessed his meaning, "as soon as we're on the side road, we'll have some peace of mind!"

Before they even reached the side road, Xiangzi and the short bald man, rickshaw and all, were captured by about ten soldiers!

Although it was the season for pilgrimages to Miaofeng Mountain, the night chill was still too much for a single shirt to ward off. Xiangzi had no burdens on him, except for a gray single military uniform top and a pair of blue cloth military trousers, both reeking of sweat—they had been like that even before he wore them. From this tattered military uniform, he remembered his original white cloth undershirt and the indigo blue padded jacket and trousers; how clean and decent they were! Yes, there were many things in the world more decent than indigo blue, but Xiangzi knew how difficult it had been for him to achieve such cleanliness and neatness. Smelling the sweat on him now, he regarded his past struggles and successes as extraordinarily glorious, ten times more glorious than they originally were. The more he thought about the past, the more he hated those soldiers. His clothes, shoes, hat, rickshaw, and even the cloth belt around his waist, had all been stolen by them; they only left him with a body covered in bruises and blisters on his feet! However, clothes were nothing; the injuries on his body would heal soon. His rickshaw, the rickshaw he had earned with years of blood and sweat, was gone! It had disappeared as soon as it was pulled into the camp! All the past hardships and difficulties could be forgotten in a blink of an eye, but he couldn't forget this rickshaw!

He wasn't afraid of hardship; but getting another rickshaw wasn't something that could be done just by saying so; it would take at least a few more years! All his past successes were in vain, he had to start all over again! Xiangzi shed tears! He not only hated those soldiers but also everything in the world. Why should people be bullied to this extent? Why? "Why?" he shouted. This shout—though somewhat satisfying—immediately reminded him of danger. Never mind anything else for now, escaping was paramount!

Where was he? He couldn't answer accurately himself. These days, he had been running with the soldiers, sweat flowing from his head all the way to his heels. When moving, he had to carry, pull, or push the soldiers' belongings; when stopped, he had to fetch water, make fires, and feed the animals. All day long, he only knew how to put his last ounce of strength into his hands and feet, his mind a blank. At night, as soon as his head touched the ground, he would fall asleep as if dead, and never opening his eyes again wasn't necessarily a bad thing.

Initially, he seemed to remember the soldiers retreating towards Miaofeng Mountain. By the time they reached the back of the mountain, he was only concerned with climbing, constantly thinking that he might fall into a mountain stream at any moment, his flesh and bones pecked clean by wild eagles, disregarding everything else. After wandering in the mountains for many days, suddenly one day the mountain paths became fewer and fewer. When the sun was behind him, he saw flat ground in the distance. The bugle call for dinner brought the soldiers back to camp, and a few, carrying guns, led several camels.

Camels! Xiangzi's heart stirred, and suddenly he could think, as if a lost person had suddenly found a familiar landmark and quickly recalled everything. Camels couldn't cross mountains, so he must have reached flat ground. In his knowledge, he knew that in the area west of Beijing, like Balizhuang, Huangcun, Beixinan, Moshikou, Wulitun, Sanjiadian, there were camel breeders. Could it be that after all the winding, he had arrived at Moshikou? What kind of strategy was this—if this group of soldiers who only knew how to run and rob also had strategies—he didn't know. But he knew for sure that if this was indeed Moshikou, the soldiers would not be able to get out of the mountains and would come down to find a way out. Moshikou was a good place; to the northeast, one could return to Xishan; to the south, one could go to Changxindian or Fengtai; going straight west was also a way out. He planned for the soldiers in this way, and in his heart, he also drew a path for himself: it was time for him to escape. If the soldiers retreated back into the chaotic mountains, even if he escaped their grasp, there would still be the danger of starving to death. To escape, he had to seize this opportunity. Running from here, he believed, he could reach Haidian in one step! Although there were so many places in between, he knew them all; closing his eyes, he had a map: here was Moshikou—God, this must be Moshikou!—he would turn northeast, pass Jindingshan, Liwangfen, and then it would be Badachu; from Sitai platform, head east to Xingzikou, and he would reach Nanxinzhuang. For some cover, it was best to follow the mountains, from Beixinzhuang, north, past Weijiacun; north, past Nanhetan; then further north, to Hongshantou, Jiewangfu; Jingyiyuan! Once he found Jingyiyuan, he could even find Haidian with his eyes closed! His heart was pounding! These days, his blood seemed to have all flowed to his limbs; at this moment, it seemed to have all returned to his heart; his heart felt hot, but his limbs grew cold; the fervent hope made him tremble all over!

He couldn't close his eyes until midnight. Hope made him happy, fear made him anxious. He wanted to sleep but couldn't, his limbs felt scattered as they lay on some dry grass. There was no sound, only the stars in the sky accompanying his own heartbeat. The camels suddenly let out two mournful cries, not far from him. He liked this sound, it made him sad like hearing a rooster crow in the night, yet he also felt some comfort.

There was the sound of cannon fire in the distance, very far, but clearly cannon fire. He dared not move, but immediately the camp became chaotic. He held his breath, the opportunity had arrived! He knew for sure that the soldiers would have to retreat again, and it would definitely be into the mountains. His experience of these days told him that these soldiers' fighting methods were like bees trapped in a room, only bumping around aimlessly. With the sound of cannon fire, the soldiers would definitely have to run; then, he himself should also be alert. Slowly, holding his breath, he crawled on the ground, his goal being to find those camels. He knew perfectly well that the camels wouldn't help him, but since he and they were both captives, it seemed there had to be some sympathy. The military camp became even more chaotic, and he found the camels—like several earthen mounds lying prostrate in the darkness, with no movement except their heavy breathing, as if the world was very peaceful. This gave him a little more courage. He lay beside the camels, like a soldier hiding behind sandbags. Very quickly, he figured out a reason: the cannon fire was coming from the south, and even if it wasn't a real battle, it was at least a "road closed" warning. Then, these soldiers would have to flee back into the mountains. If they really went up the mountain, they couldn't take the camels. Thus, the fate of the camels was also his fate. If they didn't abandon these animals, he would also be finished; if they forgot the camels, he could escape. Pressing his ear to the ground, he listened for footsteps, his heart beating extremely fast.

He didn't know how long he waited, but no one came to lead the camels. He boldly sat up and looked between the camels' humps, seeing nothing, it was pitch black all around. Escape! Whether it was good or bad, escape!

[1]

 Yibian'er, meaning the same.

[2]

 Zhaohu ba, meaning go for it, brave it.

[3]

 Jin'er ge jiu shi jin'er ge, meaning it's a critical moment, success or failure depends on today.

Three

Xiangzi had only run a few dozen steps when he stopped. It turned out that Xiangzi was reluctant to leave those camels, because all he had left now was his life. Even a piece of hemp rope on the ground, he would gladly pick up; even if useless, it could still comfort him a little, at least he would have a piece of hemp rope in his hand, not completely empty. Escaping was important, but what good was a bare life? He had to take these animals with him; although he hadn't yet thought of what use the camels could be, they were still something, and not small things either.

He pulled the camels up. He didn't know much about how to handle camels, but he wasn't afraid of them, because coming from the countryside, he dared to approach animals.The camels slowly, slowly stood up. He didn't bother to investigate whether they were all tied together. Feeling that he could lead them away, he started walking, whether it was one or all of them.

As soon as he took a step, he regretted it. Camels—accustomed to carrying heavy loads in the interior—couldn't walk fast. Not only did they have to walk slowly, but they also had to walk extremely carefully, as camels were afraid of slipping; a puddle of water, a patch of mud, could cause them to splay their legs or twist their knees. A camel's value lay entirely in its four legs; if the legs were gone, everything was gone! And Xiangzi wanted to escape with his life!

But he refused to let them go again. Everything was left to fate; he couldn't let go of camels he had acquired for free!

Having pulled carts for a long time, Xiangzi had a good sense of direction. Nevertheless, his mind was a bit muddled now. When he found the camels, his heart seemed entirely focused on them; but once he had them standing, he couldn't tell where he was. The sky was so dark, and his heart was so anxious, that even if he could look at the stars and adjust his direction, he didn't dare to do so calmly; the stars—in his eyes—seemed even more anxious than he was, bumping into each other and moving chaotically in the dark sky. Xiangzi dared not look at the sky anymore. He lowered his head, his heart anxious but his steps not daring to quicken as he walked forward. He remembered this: since he was leading camels, he had to follow the main road and could no longer walk along the hillside. From Moshikou—if this was Moshikou—to Huangcun, it was a straight road. This was a main road for camels, and it wasn't a detour at all. "No detour" had great value in the heart of a rickshaw puller. However, there was no cover on this road! What if he encountered soldiers again? Even if he didn't encounter regular soldiers, his own tattered military uniform, the mud on his face, and that head of long hair, could they make people believe he was a camel driver? No, absolutely not like a camel driver! More like a deserter! A deserter, being caught by the authorities was a minor matter; being caught by the villagers meant at least being buried alive! Thinking of this, he shivered, and the soft thud of camel hooves behind him suddenly startled him. If he wanted to escape with his life, he still had to abandon these burdens. But he still refused to let go of the rope on the camels' noses. Let's go, go, wherever we end up, whatever we encounter; if I live, I'll earn a few animals; if I die, it's fate!

However, he took off his military uniform, and tore off the collar with a yank; the brass buttons that still held responsibility were also pulled off and thrown into the darkness, without even a sound. Then, he draped this collarless, buttonless single garment diagonally over his body, tying the two sleeves into a knot in front of his chest, like carrying a bundle. He thought this might reduce the suspicion of being a defeated soldier; his trousers were also rolled up a bit. He knew this still didn't look quite like a camel driver, but at least it wasn't entirely like a deserter. Coupled with the mud on his face and the sweat on his body, it probably made him look like a "coal black" [1]. His thoughts were slow, but very thorough, and as soon as he thought of something, he immediately acted on it. In the dark of night, no one saw him; he didn't need to do this immediately; but he couldn't wait. He didn't know the time, and it might suddenly be dawn. Since he wasn't following the mountain road, he wouldn't have a chance to hide during the day; if he wanted to continue traveling during the day, he had to make people believe he was a "coal black". As soon as he thought of this, he did it immediately, and he felt a little relieved, as if the danger had passed, and Beiping was right in front of him. He had to get to the city quickly and steadily, because he didn't have a single penny or any dry food, and couldn't waste any more time. Thinking of this, he wanted to ride a camel to save some energy and endure hunger a little longer. But he didn't dare to ride; even if it was very stable, he would have to make the camel kneel down first before he could get on; time was precious, and he couldn't bother with it anymore. Besides, if he got up that high, it would be even harder to see underfoot, and if the camel fell, he would fall with it. No, he would just walk like this.

He probably felt he was following the main road; the direction and location were somewhat vague. It was late at night, and the fatigue of many days, combined with the fear of escape, made him feel uncomfortable both physically and mentally. As he walked some distance, his steps were so even and slow that he gradually felt sleepy. The night was still very dark, with some damp and cold mist in the air, making him feel even more lost. He tried hard to look at the ground, and it always seemed to have bumps, but when he put his foot down, it was flat. This caution and deception made him even more restless, almost irritable. He simply stopped paying attention to the ground, looking straight ahead and shuffling his feet. He couldn't see anything around him, as if all the darkness in the world was waiting for him, stepping out of darkness and walking back into darkness; the silent camels followed behind him.

He gradually got used to the darkness outside, and his mind seemed to stop working, his eyes involuntarily closing. He didn't know if he was still walking forward or had already stopped, his mind only felt waves of fluctuations, like a fluctuating black sea, darkness and his heart merging into one, all vague, all rising and falling, all hazy. Suddenly, his heart stirred, as if remembering something, or perhaps hearing some sound, he couldn't tell; but then he opened his eyes again. He was indeed still walking forward, he had forgotten what he had just remembered, and there was no movement anywhere. His heart pounded for a while, then gradually calmed down again. He told himself not to close his eyes again, and not to think wildly; getting to the city quickly was the most important thing. But if his mind wasn't thinking, his eyes would easily close again, he had to think about something, he had to stay awake. He knew that once he collapsed, he could sleep for three days straight. What to think about? His head was a little dizzy, his body was uncomfortably damp, his hair was itchy, his feet ached, and his mouth was dry and parched. He couldn't think of anything else, only felt sorry for himself. But he couldn't even think about his own affairs in detail anymore, his head was so empty and swollen, as if he had just remembered himself, and then forgotten himself again, like a candle about to go out, unable to illuminate even itself. Coupled with the surrounding darkness, it made him feel like he was floating in a cloud of black gas, although he knew he still existed and was still moving forward, there was nothing else to prove where he was going, much like floating alone in a desolate sea, not daring to believe in himself. He had never experienced such an uneasy and absolutely lonely distress. In ordinary times, although he didn't particularly like making friends, when he was alone in the sunlight, with the sun shining on his limbs, and various things appearing before him, he wouldn't be afraid. Now, he wasn't afraid, but the inability to be certain of everything made him unbearable. If the camels were as unruly as mules and horses, it might have made him perk up and pay attention to them, but the camels were so docile, so docile that it made him impatient; at his most dazed, he suddenly doubted whether the camels were still behind him, which startled him; he seemed to believe that these large animals would quietly slip into a dark side road, and he wouldn't know it at all, like pulling a piece of ice that would gradually melt away.

He sat down at some unknown point. If he were to die like this, even if he had consciousness after death, he wouldn't remember how or why he sat down. He sat for five minutes, or perhaps an hour, he didn't know. He also didn't know if he sat down first and then fell asleep, or fell asleep first and then sat down. He probably fell asleep first and then sat down, because his fatigue was already enough to make him sleep standing up.

He suddenly woke up. It wasn't a natural awakening from sleep, but a sudden fright, like jumping from one world to another, all in the blink of an eye. What he saw was still darkness, but he clearly heard a rooster crow, so clearly, as if something hard had scratched his brain. He was fully awake. Where were the camels? He didn't have time to think about anything else. The rope was still in his hand, and the camels were still beside him. His heart was calm. He was too lazy to get up. His body ached and felt sluggish, he didn't want to get up, but he didn't dare to sleep again either. He had to think, think carefully, for a good idea. It was at this moment that he remembered his cart, and shouted, "Why not?"

"Why not?" But empty shouting was useless. He went to feel the camels, he still didn't know how many he had brought. He felt them clearly, there were three in total. He didn't feel this was too many or too few; he focused his thoughts on these three, and although he hadn't decided what to do, he vaguely thought that his future depended entirely on these three animals.

"Why not sell them and buy another cart?" He almost jumped up! But he didn't move, as if he felt ashamed for not having thought of such a natural and easy solution before. Joy overcame shame, and he made up his mind: hadn't he just heard a rooster crow? Even if roosters sometimes crowed at one or two in the morning, it wasn't far from dawn anyway. Where there was a rooster crow, there must be a village, maybe it was Beixinan? There were camel breeders there, he had to hurry, if he could get there by dawn and sell the camels, he could buy a cart as soon as he entered the city. During wartime, carts would definitely be cheaper; he only thought about buying a cart, as if selling camels was a completely easy matter.

Thinking about the relationship between camels and rickshaws, his spirits lifted, and his body seemed to have no discomfort at all. If he had thought that these three camels could buy a hundred mu of land, or could be exchanged for a few pearls, he wouldn't have been so happy. He quickly stood up, pulled the camels, and left. He didn't know the current market price for camels, he had only heard that in olden times, when there were no trains, a camel was worth a "big treasure" [2], because camels were strong and ate less than mules and horses. He didn't hope to get three "big treasures", he only hoped to exchange them for eighty or a hundred, just enough to buy a cart. The brighter it got, the more he walked; indeed, the light was ahead, he was definitely walking east. Even if he had taken the wrong path, the direction was not wrong; the mountains were to the west, the city to the east, he knew this. The uniform darkness around him gradually began to show shades, and although he still couldn't distinguish colors, the fields and distant trees already had shapes in the general grayness. The stars became sparse, and the sky was covered with a layer of gray air, like clouds and mist, dim, but much higher than before. Xiangzi seemed to dare to raise his head. He also began to smell the grass by the roadside and heard a few bird calls; because he saw vague shapes, his ears, eyes, mouth, and nose seemed to have recovered their proper functions. He could also see everything on himself, although it was so tattered and disheveled, he could believe that he was indeed still alive; just like waking up from a nightmare, feeling how lovely life was. After looking at himself, he looked back at the camels—as ugly as he was, and as lovely. It was shedding season for the animals, and the camels' bodies were already showing their grayish-red skin, with only scattered, weak, long hairs hanging here and there, ready to fall off at any time, like huge beggars among beasts. The most pitiful thing was their long, hairless necks, so long, so bald, curved, clumsy, stretching far out, like a frustrated skinny dragon. But Xiangzi didn't dislike them, no matter how unpresentable they were, they were living things after all. He admitted that he was the luckiest person in the world, heaven had sent him three living treasures that could be exchanged for a rickshaw; this was not something one encountered every day. He couldn't help but laugh.

The gray sky showed some red, and the ground and distant trees appeared darker; the red gradually blended with the gray, some places becoming grayish-purple, some places particularly red, and most of the sky was grape-gray. After a while, bright golden yellow emerged from the red, and all colors showed some light; suddenly, everything became very clear. Then, the morning glow in the east turned a deep red, and the sky above showed blue. The red glow shattered, and golden rays shot out one by one, horizontal were the glow, vertical were the rays, weaving a magnificent and brilliant spider web in the southeast corner of the sky: the green fields, trees, and wild grass all changed from dark green to glowing emerald. The trunks of old pines were stained golden-red, the wings of flying birds shimmered with golden light, and everything brought out a smile. Xiangzi wanted to shout a few times at that red light, ever since he was taken away by the soldiers, he seemed to have never seen the sun, his heart was always cursing, his head always lowered, forgetting that there were sun and moon, forgetting heaven. Now, he was walking freely, and the brighter it got, the more he walked, the sun gave a little golden light to the dewdrops on the grass leaves, and also illuminated Xiangzi's eyebrows and hair, warming his heart. He forgot all hardships, all dangers, all pains; no matter how ragged and dirty he was, the light and heat of the sun did not exclude him, he lived in a universe with light and heat; he was happy, he wanted to cheer!

Looking at his tattered clothes, and then at the three shedding camels behind him, he smiled. With four such unpresentable people and animals, he thought, it was truly strange that they could escape danger and walk towards the sun again! There was no need to think about who was right or wrong, everything was fate, he thought. He relaxed, walking slowly, as long as heaven blessed him, there was nothing to fear. Where had he gotten to? He didn't want to ask anymore, although there were already men and women working in the fields. Let's go,Even if he couldn't sell the camels right away, it didn't seem to matter much; he'd get to the city first. He longed to see the city again, even though he had no parents, relatives, or possessions there. But it was his home, the whole city was his home, and once he got there, he'd find a way. In the distance was a village, a rather large one, with willows outside like a row of tall, green guards, looking down at the low houses, from which wisps of cooking smoke rose. From afar, he heard the barking of village dogs, which sounded very pleasant. He ran straight towards the village, not expecting to encounter anything special, as if just to show that he wasn't afraid of anything, that he was a good person, and of course not afraid of the good people in the village; everyone was now under the bright, peaceful sun. If possible, he wanted some water to drink; even if he couldn't get water, it didn't matter; since he hadn't died in the mountains, what was a little more thirst?

The village dogs barked at him, but he didn't pay much attention; the stares of the women and children made him uncomfortable. He must be a very strange camel driver, he thought: otherwise, why were they all staring at him so blankly? He felt extremely awkward: the soldiers didn't treat him like a person, and now in the village, everyone looked at him like a monster! He didn't know what to do. His stature and strength had always made him proud and self-respecting, but in the past few days, he had suffered endless grievances and hardships for no reason. From the ridge of a house, he looked up and saw the bright sun again, but the sun no longer seemed as lovely as before!

On the only main road in the village, pig urine, horse urine, and sewage converged into several stinking puddles. To the north of the road was a relatively grand house, with tiled roofs in the back, but the main gate was only blocked by a wooden fence, with no wooden door or gatehouse. Xiangzi's heart stirred: tiled roof—wealthy family; wooden fence but no gatehouse—camel owner! All right, he'd rest here for a while, just in case a good opportunity arose to get rid of the camels!

"Se! Se! Se!" Xiangzi commanded the camels to kneel. For camel commands, he only knew "Se..." meant to kneel. He used it with great satisfaction, specifically to let the villagers know he wasn't an amateur. The camels actually knelt, and he himself sat down grandly under a small willow tree. Everyone looked at him, and he looked at everyone; he knew this was the only way to reduce the villagers' suspicion.

After sitting for a while, an old man came out of the courtyard. He wore a blue cloth jacket open at the chest, and his face was very bright; one could tell at a glance that he was a wealthy villager. Xiangzi made up his mind: "Old man, is there water ready? I'll have a bowl!"

"Ah!" The old man rubbed the dirt rolls on his chest, sized up Xiangzi, and then carefully looked at the three camels. "There's water! Where are you from?"

"West!" Xiangzi dared not say the place name, because it was not allowed to be known.

"Are there soldiers in the west?" The old man's eyes fixed on Xiangzi's military trousers.

"The big soldiers took me, I just escaped."

"Ah! No danger for camels going out the west pass, right?"

"The soldiers have all gone into the mountains, the road is very safe."

"Hmm!" The old man nodded slowly. "You wait, I'll get you some water."

Xiangzi followed him in. In the courtyard, he saw four camels. "Old man, keep my three, make a team?"

"Hmph! A team? Thirty years ago, I had three teams! Times have changed, who can afford to feed camels anymore!" The old man stopped, staring blankly at the four animals. After a long pause: "A few days ago, I wanted to team up with neighbors to take them out of the pass to graze [3]. But there are soldiers in the east and soldiers in the west, who dares to go! Just keep them at home for the summer, it's heartbreaking to watch, heartbreaking to watch, look at these flies! When it gets really hot tomorrow, plus the mosquitoes, watching good animals suffer alive, sigh!" The old man nodded repeatedly, seemingly with endless emotion and complaints.

"Old man, keep my three, make a team and go out of the pass to graze. These lively animals, if they stay here all summer, the flies and mosquitoes will surely half-kill them!" Xiangzi almost pleaded.

"But who has money to buy them? These aren't the years for raising camels!" "Just keep them, whatever you can give; I'll get rid of them so I can go to the city to make a living!"

The old man looked Xiangzi over carefully again, feeling that he was definitely not a bandit. Then he turned to look at the animals outside the gate, and in his heart, he seemed to genuinely like those three camels—even though he knew there was no benefit in buying them, just as a book lover wants to buy every book they see, and a horse owner can't bear to part with horses, so it was for someone who had once owned three teams of camels. Moreover, Xiangzi said he could sell them cheaply; an expert who gets a bargain easily forgets whether the item will be beneficial once acquired.

"Young man, if I were rich, I'd really want to keep them!" The old man spoke honestly.

"Just keep them then, let's see how it goes!" Xiangzi was so sincere that the old man felt a little embarrassed.

"Honestly, young man, thirty years ago, these would be worth three 'dabao' [2]; but in these times, with war and chaos, I—you'd better try selling them elsewhere!" "Give whatever you can!" Xiangzi couldn't think of anything else to say. He understood that the old man's words were very practical, but he didn't want to go all over the world selling camels—if they didn't sell, other problems might arise.

"Look, look, twenty or thirty yuan is really hard to say, but it's also really hard to part with; in these times, there's no way!"

Xiangzi's heart also grew colder. Twenty or thirty yuan? That was still far from buying a cart! But, first, he wanted to get it done as soon as possible, and second, he didn't believe he could coincidentally meet another buyer. "Old man, give whatever you can!"

"What do you do, young man; I can tell you're not in this line of work!"

Xiangzi told the truth.

"Oh, you risked your life for these animals!" The old man sympathized with Xiangzi and was relieved that they weren't stolen; although not far from stealing, there was still a layer of soldiers in between. After the disaster of war, nothing could be judged by common sense.

"How about this, fellow, I'll give you thirty-five yuan; if I say this isn't a bargain, I'm a little dog; if I could give even one more yuan, I'd also be a little dog! I'm over sixty. Hmph, what else can I say!"

Xiangzi was at a loss. When it came to money, he had always been unwilling to let go of a single cent. However, after these days in the army, suddenly hearing the old man's sincere and emotional words, he felt embarrassed to argue further. Moreover, the thirty-five yuan in cash he could get seemed more reliable than the ten thousand yuan he had hoped for, even though thirty-five yuan for a life was indeed a small amount! Just for three large, living camels, it absolutely, absolutely could not be worth only thirty-five silver dollars! But what could he do! "The camels are yours, old man! I'll just ask for one more thing, find me a jacket and something to eat!"

"That's fine!"

Xiangzi drank a gulp of cold water, then, holding thirty-five shiny silver dollars, two cornmeal cakes, and wearing a tattered white jacket that reached his chest, he was ready to stride into the city!

[1] Pu'er, meaning appearance. Has a similar meaning.

[2] Dabao, a silver ingot weighing fifty taels.

[3] Fangqing, letting livestock graze on green grass.

Xiangzi lay in a small shop in Haidian for three days, alternately hot and cold, his mind in a daze, a row of purple blisters on his gums. He only wanted to drink water, not eat anything. After three days of hunger, his fever subsided, and his body felt as soft as taffy. It was probably during these three days that his connection with the three camels was overheard by others in his delirium or incoherent ramblings. When he fully regained consciousness, he was already "Camel Xiangzi."

Since he came to the city, he had been "Xiangzi," as if he never had a surname. Now, with "Camel" placed above "Xiangzi," no one cared what his surname actually was. With or without a surname, he himself didn't care. However, three animals were only exchanged for a few coins, and he ended up with a nickname. He felt it wasn't a good deal.

As soon as he could struggle to stand up, he wanted to go out and look around. He didn't expect his legs to be so weak. He stumbled and sat down on the ground at the shop's entrance, sitting there in a daze for a long time, a cold sweat breaking out on his forehead. After enduring for a while longer, he opened his eyes. His stomach rumbled, and he felt a pang of hunger. He slowly stood up and found a wonton vendor. He ordered a bowl of wontons and still sat on the ground. He took a sip of the soup and felt nauseous, holding it in his mouth for a long time before forcing himself to swallow it. He didn't want to drink any more. But after a while, the hot soup, like a thread, reached his abdomen, and he let out two loud burps. He knew he was alive again.

With some food in his stomach, he could finally look at himself. He had lost a lot of weight, and his tattered trousers were dirtier than ever. He was too lazy to move, but he wanted to quickly regain his neat and tidy appearance. He refused to enter the city looking so disheveled. However, being neat and tidy required money: a haircut, new clothes, shoes, and socks all cost money. The thirty-five yuan in his hand should remain untouched, and even then, it was still far from enough to buy a rickshaw. But he pitied himself. Although he had only been taken away by the soldiers for a short time, thinking about it now, everything felt like a nightmare. This nightmare had aged him considerably, as if he had suddenly aged several years in one go. Looking at his large hands and feet, they were clearly his own, yet it was as if he had suddenly found them somewhere. He was very sad. He dared not think about the past grievances and dangers. Even if he didn't think about them, they still existed, just like on a cloudy day, even without looking at the sky, one knows it is dark. He felt his body was particularly precious and should not be subjected to too much hardship anymore. He stood up, knowing his body was still very weak, but he urgently wanted to spruce himself up, as if a haircut and a change of clothes would instantly make him strong again.

After sprucing himself up, it only cost him two yuan and two mao. A set of plain coarse cloth trousers and jacket, almost like canvas, cost one yuan, blue cloth shoes eight mao, socks woven from yarn one mao five, and a straw hat two mao five. The old, worn-out items he took off were exchanged for two packs of matches. Holding the two packs of matches, he walked along the main road towards Xizhimen. He hadn't gone far before he felt weak and tired. But he gritted his teeth. He couldn't take a rickshaw. From every perspective, he couldn't take a rickshaw: could a country bumpkin consider ten or eight li a long distance, especially when he himself pulled rickshaws? Not to mention, for someone of his stature and strength to be overcome by such a small illness was a joke. Unless he fell down and couldn't get up again, he would roll all the way into the city, refusing to give in! If he couldn't walk into the city today, he thought, Xiangzi would be finished. He only trusted his own body, no matter what illness he had!

Swaying, he quickened his pace. Not far from Haidian, golden stars appeared before his eyes. Leaning against a willow tree, he steadied himself for a long time. The world spun around him in a dizzying rush, but he never sat down. The spinning of heaven and earth slowly calmed down, and his heart seemed to return to his chest from a distant place. Wiping the sweat from his forehead, he started walking again. He had already had a haircut, and changed into new clothes and new shoes. He thought this was enough to treat himself well. So, his legs had to do their duty, walk! In one go, he reached Guanshuan. Seeing the bustling crowd and horses, hearing the complex and piercing sounds, smelling the dry, foul odor, and stepping on the fine, soft, dirty dust, Xiangzi wanted to crawl down and kiss that dusty, foul-smelling ground, that lovely ground, the ground where foreign money grew! Without parents or siblings, without relatives from his clan, his only friend was this ancient city. This city gave him everything. Even starving here was more pleasant than in the countryside. Here there was much to see, much to hear, colors everywhere, sounds everywhere. As long as he worked hard, there was an endless supply of money here, and countless good things to eat and wear. Here, even beggars could get meat broth and cured meats, while in the countryside there was only cornmeal. Just west of Gaoliang Bridge, he sat on the riverbank, and a few hot tears fell!

The sun was setting in the west, and the old willows by the river swayed, their tips adorned with a touch of golden light. There wasn't much water in the river, but there was a lot of green algae, like a greasy, long green ribbon, narrow, dark green, emitting a slightly fishy, damp smell. The wheat on the north bank of the river had already sprouted ears, short and dry, with a layer of dust on its leaves. The small, weak green leaves of the lotus pond on the south bank floated on the water, and tiny bubbles occasionally rose from around the leaves. On the bridge to the east, people and vehicles came and went, appearing particularly hurried in the slanting sunlight, as if all felt an uneasiness as dusk approached. All of this was very interesting and lovely in Xiangzi's eyes and ears. Only such a small river could be considered a river; such trees, wheat, lotus leaves, and bridges could be considered trees, wheat, lotus leaves, and bridges. Because they all belonged to Beiping.

Sitting there, he was no longer in a hurry. Everything before his eyes was familiar and lovely. Even if he were to sit there and die, he seemed quite willing. After resting for a long time, he went to the bridgehead and ate a bowl of old tofu: vinegar, soy sauce, prickly ash oil, and chopped chives, scalded by the hot, snow-white tofu, giving off a wonderfully fragrant taste, so fragrant that Xiangzi almost held his breath. Holding the bowl and looking at the dark green chopped chives, his hands trembled uncontrollably. He took a bite, and the tofu opened a path inside him. He added two small spoonfuls of chili oil himself. After finishing the bowl, his waist was soaked with sweat. With his eyes half-closed, he handed out the bowl: "Another bowl!"

Standing up, he felt like a human again. The sun was still at its lowest point in the west, and the river water was slightly reddish from the glow of the sunset. He felt so exhilarated he wanted to shout. He touched the smooth scar on his face, felt the money in his pocket, and glanced at the sunlight on the corner tower again. He stubbornly forgot his illness, forgot everything, as if he had some wish, and decided to enter the city.

The city gate archway was crowded with all kinds of vehicles and people. No one dared to walk fast, yet everyone wanted to pass quickly. The sound of whips, shouts, curses, horns, bells, and laughter all merged into a buzzing sound in the archway—like a loudspeaker—as if everyone was making some noise, all buzzing. Xiangzi's big feet stepped east, then west, his hands pushing left and right, like a slender, large fish joyfully leaping with the waves, squeezing into the city. He immediately saw Xinjiekou. The road was so wide and straight, his eyes lit up, as bright as the reflection on the rooftops to the east. He nodded.

His bedding was still at Renhe Rickshaw Depot on Xi'anmen Street, so naturally, he wanted to go there. Since he had no family, he had always lived in the rickshaw depot, although he didn't always pull rickshaws from the depot. Liu Siye, the owner of Renhe, was almost seventy years old. Old in age, but not in heart. In his youth, he had been a treasury soldier, set up gambling dens, trafficked people, and lent money at exorbitant rates. Liu Siye possessed all the necessary qualifications and skills for these trades—strength, cunning, means, connections, reputation, and so on. In the Qing Dynasty, he had participated in gang fights, robbed respectable women, and knelt on iron chains. When kneeling on iron chains, Liu Siye didn't even frown, nor did he beg for mercy. He endured the lawsuit, which was called "reputation." After being released from prison, it happened to be the Republic of China, and the power of the police grew stronger. Liu Siye realized that the heroes of the underworld were a thing of the past, and even if Huang Tianba were reborn, he wouldn't have many opportunities. He opened a rickshaw depot. Coming from a rough background, he knew how to deal with poor people, when to tighten the reins, and where to loosen them. He had a natural talent for manipulation. No rickshaw puller dared to play tricks on him. With a glare, or a hearty laugh, he could make people dizzy, as if one foot was in heaven and the other in hell, leaving them no choice but to obey him. By now, he had over sixty rickshaws, the worst of which were still seven or eight tenths new. He didn't keep broken rickshaws. His rickshaw rental fees were higher than others, but during the three major festivals, he gave two extra days' grace compared to others. Renhe Depot had living quarters, and the bachelors who pulled his rickshaws could live there for free—but they had to pay their rental fees. If they couldn't pay and complained to him, he would confiscate their bedding and throw them out like a broken teapot. If anyone had an urgent matter or illness, they only needed to tell him, and he wouldn't hesitate. He would enthusiastically help them through thick and thin. This was called "reputation."

Liu Siye had a tiger-like appearance. Nearing seventy, his back was not bent, and he could still walk ten or twenty li. He had two large round eyes, a big nose, a square mouth, a pair of large tiger teeth, and when he opened his mouth, he looked like a tiger. He was almost as tall as Xiangzi, his head was shaved very clean, and he had no beard. He considered himself a tiger, but unfortunately, he had no sons, only a thirty-seven or thirty-eight-year-old tiger daughter—anyone who knew Liu Siye would also know Huniu. She also had a tiger-like appearance, which scared off men. She was a good hand at helping her father with business, but no one dared to marry her. She was like a man in everything, even her cursing had a man's straightforwardness, sometimes with even more flair. Liu Siye managed the outside, and Huniu managed the inside. The father and daughter governed Renhe Rickshaw Depot like an iron barrel. Renhe Depot became an authority in the rickshaw world, and the methods of the Liu family father and daughter were often discussed by rickshaw pullers and owners, like scholars quoting classics.

Before buying his own rickshaw, Xiangzi had pulled rickshaws from Renhe Depot. His savings were entrusted to Liu Siye for safekeeping. When he had saved enough money, he retrieved it and bought that new rickshaw.

"Liu Siye, look at my rickshaw!" Xiangzi pulled his new rickshaw to Renhe Depot. The old man glanced at the rickshaw and nodded, "Not bad!"

"I still have to live here. Only when I get a monthly contract will I move out!" Xiangzi said quite proudly.

"Alright!" Liu Siye nodded again.

So, when Xiangzi got a monthly contract, he would move out. When he lost his job and went back to pulling individual fares, he would live at Renhe Depot.

Not pulling Liu Siye's rickshaw yet being able to live at Renhe Depot was, in the eyes of other rickshaw pullers, a rare thing. Therefore, some even speculated that Xiangzi must be related to old man Liu; others even said that old man Liu probably took a liking to Xiangzi and wanted to find a "son-in-law" for Huniu. Although this speculation contained a bit of envy, what if it were true? If Liu Siye died in the future, Renhe Depot would surely belong to Xiangzi.
