This made them only dare to guess wildly, but not say anything unpleasant in front of Xiangzi. In reality, old man Liu's preferential treatment of Xiangzi had another reason. Xiangzi was such a person: he could maintain old habits in a new environment. If he had become a soldier, he would never have immediately put on that uniform and then foolishly bullied people. In the rickshaw depot, he wasn't idle. As soon as his sweat dried, he would find something to do. He would wipe rickshaws, pump tires, dry rain covers, oil parts... No one needed to tell him; he was willing to do it himself, and he did it happily, as if it were a great pleasure. About twenty rickshaw pullers usually lived in the depot. After returning their rickshaws, everyone either sat around chatting or slept soundly. Xiangzi, only Xiangzi's hands were not idle. At first, everyone thought he was trying to curry favor with Liu Siye, sucking up to him. After a few days, they saw that he had no intention of showing off or seeking praise. He was so sincere and natural that they had nothing more to say. Old man Liu never praised him, nor did he give him an extra glance. The old man knew what he was doing. He knew Xiangzi was a good hand, and even if he didn't pull his rickshaws, he still wanted Xiangzi to be in the depot. With Xiangzi there, not to mention anything else, the courtyard and entrance were always swept clean. Huniu liked this big, simple fellow even more. Whatever she said, Xiangzi always listened attentively and didn't argue with her. Other rickshaw pullers, having suffered so much, always spoke aggressively. She wasn't afraid of them at all, but she also didn't want to bother with them much. So, her words were all reserved for Xiangzi to hear. When Xiangzi went to pull a monthly contract, both father and daughter of the Liu family seemed to lose a friend. When he returned, even the old man's scolding seemed more cheerful and benevolent.

Xiangzi, holding two packs of matches, entered Renhe Depot. It wasn't dark yet, and the Liu family father and daughter were eating dinner. Seeing him enter, Huniu put down her chopsticks: "Xiangzi! Did a wolf carry you off, or did you go to Africa to dig for gold?" "Hmph!" Xiangzi didn't say anything.

Liu Siye's large round eyes circled Xiangzi, but he said nothing. Xiangzi, wearing his new straw hat, sat opposite them.

"If you haven't eaten yet, join us!" Huniu seemed to be treating a good friend.

Xiangzi didn't move. A sudden, indescribable warmth filled his heart. He had always regarded Renhe Depot as his home: when pulling a monthly contract, the employers often changed; when pulling individual fares,"The seats change constantly; only here does he always stay, and people always chat with him. Now, having just escaped with his life, he returned to familiar faces, and they even offered him food. He almost suspected they were trying to trick him, but tears nearly welled up in his eyes.

"I just ate two bowls of old tofu!" he said, showing a little politeness.

"Where have you been?" Liu Siye's big round eyes were still fixed on Xiangzi. "Where's the cart?" "The cart?" Xiangzi spat.

"Come and eat first! It won't poison you! What good are two bowls of old tofu?!" Huniu pulled him over, as if an elder sister-in-law doting on her younger brother-in-law. Xiangzi didn't pick up the bowl, but first took out his money: "Siye, hold this for me, thirty yuan." He put the loose change back in his pocket.

Liu Siye asked with a raised eyebrow, "Where did it come from?"

Xiangzi ate while recounting how he had been taken by soldiers.

"Hmph, you foolish boy!" Liu Siye shook his head after listening. "If they had brought them into the city and sold them to a soup kitchen, each would be worth more than ten yuan; if it were winter when their camel hair was full, three could sell for sixty yuan!"

Xiangzi had already felt a bit of regret, and hearing this made him even sadder. However, he then thought that selling three living animals to a soup kitchen to be slaughtered was somewhat immoral; both he and the camels had escaped, so they should all live. Without saying anything, his heart calmed down.

Miss Hu cleared away the dishes, and Liu Siye tilted his head as if remembering something. He suddenly smiled, revealing two tiger teeth that grew stronger with age: "Fool, you said you were sick in Haidian? Why didn't you just come back directly via Huangcun Avenue?"

"I still came back around Xishan, afraid that if I took the main road, someone would chase me, and if the villagers caught wind of it, they'd still treat me as a deserter!"

Liu Siye smiled, his eyes turning inward twice. He was afraid there was something fishy about Xiangzi's words; what if the thirty yuan was stolen? He couldn't keep stolen goods for someone else. When he was young, he had done all sorts of illegal things; now, he considered himself reformed and had to be careful, and he knew how to be careful. Xiangzi's narrative had only this one loophole, but Xiangzi didn't explain it away nervously, so the old man felt relieved.

"What should we do?" the old man said, pointing at the money.

"I'll listen to you!"

"Buy another cart?" The old man showed his tiger teeth again, as if saying, "Buy your own cart, and still live in my place for free?!"

"Not enough! If I buy, I have to buy a new one!" Xiangzi didn't look at Liu Siye's teeth, only at his own heart.

"Lend it to you? One percent interest, others borrow at two and a half percent!"

Xiangzi shook his head.

"It's better to give me one percent interest than to print a receipt with a cart shop!"

"I won't print a receipt either," Xiangzi said distractedly, "I'll save slowly, and when I have enough, I'll buy with cash!"

The old man looked at Xiangzi as if he were looking at some strange character, annoying but impossible to get angry at. After a while, he picked up the money: "Thirty? Don't try to fool me!"

"That's right!" Xiangzi stood up: "I'm going to sleep. Here's a pack of matches for you, old man!" He placed a pack of matches on the table, then paused: "Don't tell anyone about the camels!"

Five

Old Man Liu indeed didn't publicize Xiangzi's story, but the tale of the camels quickly spread from Haidian into the city. Previously, although people couldn't find fault with Xiangzi, his stubbornness made them somewhat think he wasn't very sociable or agreeable. After "Camel Xiangzi" spread, even though Xiangzi still worked with his head down and wasn't very friendly, people began to look at him differently. Some said he had picked up a gold watch, others said he had gotten three hundred silver dollars for free, and those who confidently claimed to know the most accurate details nodded and said he had brought back thirty camels from Xishan! Although the stories differed, the conclusion was the same—Xiangzi had acquired ill-gotten wealth! For someone who had acquired ill-gotten wealth, no matter how "unfriendly" this fellow was, people usually respected him. Since earning money through hard work was so difficult, everyone hoped to get some ill-gotten wealth. Since ill-gotten wealth was so rare, those who had some luck must be extraordinary, blessed with great fortune. Therefore, Xiangzi's silence and unsociability transformed into the slow speech of a distinguished person. He should be like this, and they should rush to curry favor with him. "Come on, Xiangzi! Tell us, tell us how you got rich?" Xiangzi heard such words every day. He remained silent. Only when he was pushed too hard, and the scar on his face turned a little red, did he say, "Get rich, damn it, where's my cart?"

Yes, that's true, where was his cart? Everyone began to ponder. But worrying about others is not as good as being happy for them, so everyone forgot about Xiangzi's cart and thought about his good luck. After some days, everyone saw that Xiangzi was still pulling a cart and hadn't changed professions or bought a house or land, so they became a bit indifferent to him. When "Camel Xiangzi" was mentioned, they no longer questioned why he was specifically "Camel," as if he should have been called that from the beginning.

Xiangzi himself, however, did not casually forget about this matter. He wished he could buy a new cart immediately, and the more anxious he became, the more he thought about his original one. All day long, he worked tirelessly, but as he worked, he would remember that incident. Whenever he thought of it, his heart felt heavy, and he couldn't help but think, what's the use of being strong? This world isn't fairer just because one is strong. By what right was his cart taken away for nothing? Even if he got another one immediately, who knew if he wouldn't encounter such a thing again? He felt that the past was like a nightmare, making him almost afraid to hope for the future. Sometimes he saw others drinking, smoking, and visiting brothels, and he almost felt a little envy. If being strong was useless, why not enjoy the present? They were right. He, even if he didn't visit brothels first, should at least have a couple of drinks and relax. Smoke and alcohol now seemed to have a special allure for him; he felt that these two things didn't cost much money, but were definitely enough to comfort him; they would enable him to continue toiling forward, while at the same time forgetting the past pain.

However, he still didn't dare to touch them. He had to save every penny he could, otherwise he wouldn't be able to buy his own cart early. Even if he bought one today and lost it tomorrow, he still had to buy it. This was his aspiration, his hope, even his religion. Without pulling his own cart, he felt as if he was living in vain. He didn't think about becoming an official, getting rich, or acquiring property. His ability was only to pull a cart, and his most reliable hope was to buy a cart; he couldn't face himself without buying a cart. He pondered this matter all day long, calculating his money; if he ever forgot about it, he would forget himself, and feel that he was just an animal that could run, without any improvement or humanity. No matter how good a cart was, as long as it was rented, he never pulled it with enthusiasm, as if carrying a stone, so unnatural. Even with a rented cart, he didn't slack off, always keeping it clean for others, and never bumping or crashing; but these were just careful precautions, not a kind of joy. Yes, cleaning his own cart, like counting his own money, was true joy. He still had to abstain from smoking and drinking, and even a good pack of tea was inconvenient to drink. In the teahouse, a rickshaw puller as respectable as him, after a strenuous run, would meticulously drink ten-cent-a-pack tea, with two packs of white sugar, to replenish energy and dispel heat. When sweat dripped from his "earlobes" from running, and his chest felt a little spicy, he really wanted to do the same; this was absolutely not a habit or a pose, but a genuine need for two bowls of tea to settle him down. But even when he thought of it, he still drank the one-cent-a-pack tea dust. Sometimes he really wanted to scold himself, why was he so self-tormenting; but, how could a rickshaw puller save a couple of dollars a month without doing this? He hardened his heart. Buy a cart first, then talk about it! Having a cart would be enough to compensate for everything!

He was so tight-fisted with spending, and even more relentless with earning money. If he didn't have a monthly contract, he would pull all day, leaving early and returning late. He wouldn't stop until he had earned a certain amount of money, regardless of the time or his legs; sometimes he would even pull for a day and a night straight. In the past, he wouldn't snatch others' business, especially from the old, weak, and disabled; with his physique and his cart, how could they compete with him for a seat? Now, he didn't care much about this; he only saw money, the more the better, regardless of whether the business was good or bad, or who he was competing with; he only cared about getting the business, nothing else, like a starving beast. He would grab a fare and run, feeling a little more comfortable in his heart, believing that only by constantly moving could he hope to buy a cart. Gradually, the reputation of "Camel Xiangzi" was far less than when he was simply "Xiangzi." Many times, he would snatch a fare and run, followed by a chorus of curses. He wouldn't talk back, running with his head down, thinking to himself: "If it weren't for buying a cart, I would never be so shameless!" It was as if he was using these words to ask for everyone's forgiveness, but he wouldn't say it directly to them. At the cart stand or in the teahouse, he saw everyone glaring at him; he originally wanted to explain to everyone, but when he saw how indifferent they were, and considering he usually didn't drink, gamble, play chess, or chat with them, his words could only stay in his stomach, unable to be spoken. The embarrassment gradually turned into shame and anger, and his temper flared; they glared at him, and he glared back at them. Remembering how respected he was when he first escaped from the mountains, and now being looked down upon like this, he felt even more sad. Holding a pot of tea alone, if he happened to be in a teahouse, or counting the copper coins he had just earned alone, if he was at the cart stand, he used all his strength to suppress his anger. He didn't want to fight, although he wasn't afraid of fighting. As for everyone else, they weren't afraid of fighting, but fighting with Xiangzi was something to think about; none of them were his match, and everyone ganging up on one person wasn't very honorable. Forcibly suppressing his anger, he couldn't think of any other method, only to endure for a while, and things would be easier once he bought a cart. With his own cart, he wouldn't have to worry about rent every day, and he could naturally be more generous and no longer offend people by snatching business. Having thought this through, he glanced at everyone, as if to say: Let's wait and see!

Personally, he shouldn't have worked so hard. After escaping back to the city, he didn't wait for his illness to fully recover before pulling the cart, and although he never gave in, he often felt tired. Tired, he dared not rest, always thinking that running a few more sweats would reduce his soreness and laziness. As for food, he dared not pout, but he also dared not eat too much good food. He saw that he had lost a lot of weight, but his stature was still tall and strong, and his bones were still tough, which reassured him. He always thought that because he was taller and bigger than others, he could endure more hardship than others, seemingly never realizing that a large stature, enduring more fatigue, should require more nourishment. Miss Hu had warned him several times: "If you keep working like this, spitting blood will be your own business!"

He understood that these were kind words, but because things weren't going well and his body was poorly maintained, he was a bit irritable. He narrowed his eyes slightly: "If I don't run like this, when will I be able to buy a cart?"

If anyone else had narrowed their eyes like that, Huniu would have cursed for at least half a day; but for Xiangzi, she was truly 100% polite and caring. She just pursed her lips: "Buying a cart also needs to be done steadily, do you think you're made of iron! You should rest for three days!" Seeing that Xiangzi wasn't listening: "Alright, you have your old ideas, but don't blame me if you die!"

Liu Siye also looked down on Xiangzi a bit: Xiangzi's desperate efforts, leaving early and returning late, were certainly not good for his cart. Although renting a cart for the whole day had no time limit, and one could start and stop whenever they wanted, if everyone worked as hard as Xiangzi, a cart would break down at least half a year earlier, no matter how sturdy it was, it couldn't withstand constant heavy use! Moreover, Xiangzi was so focused on working hard that he didn't have much time to help clean the cart or anything, which was another loss. The old man was a little unhappy in his heart. But he didn't say anything; renting for the whole day without time limits was a common rule; helping to clean the cart was a favor, not an obligation; given his reputation, he couldn't make a fool of himself by expressing anything to Xiangzi. He could only show a hint of dissatisfaction from the corner of his eye, and keep his mouth tightly shut. Sometimes he really wanted to kick Xiangzi out; but looking at his daughter, he didn't dare to do so. He didn't consider Xiangzi as a prospective son-in-law at all, but since his daughter liked this stubborn young man, he found it inconvenient to interfere. He only had this one daughter, and it seemed there was no hope of her getting married, so he couldn't drive away her friend. To be honest, Huniu was so useful that he really didn't want her to get married; he felt a bit guilty about this selfishness, so he was somewhat afraid of her. The old man had been fearless all his life, but in his old age, he became afraid of his own daughter. He came up with a reason in his own embarrassment: as long as he was afraid of someone, it proved that he was not completely lawless. With this fact, perhaps he wouldn't suffer bad karma when he was about to die. Well, he admitted that he should be afraid of his daughter, and so he was unwilling to drive Xiangzi away. This, of course, didn't mean that he could let his daughter do whatever she wanted, even marry Xiangzi. No. He saw that his daughter might indeed have that intention,"But Xiangzi didn't dare to curry favor.

So, he just needed to be careful, no need to upset his daughter first. Xiangzi didn't notice the old man's expression; he couldn't spare the attention for such trivial matters. If he had any desire to leave Renhe Factory, it was certainly not out of spite, but rather a hope to get a monthly contract. He was already a bit tired of pulling individual fares, partly because he was looked down upon by everyone for snatching business, and partly because his daily income was uncertain, sometimes more, sometimes less, making it impossible to predict when he would save enough money to buy a rickshaw. He wanted to have a clear goal in mind, even if he saved little, as long as he could reliably save a fixed amount each month, he would feel hopeful and at ease. He was a person who preferred a clear-cut situation.

He got a monthly contract. Hmph, it was just as unsatisfactory as pulling individual fares! This time, it was at the Yang residence. Mr. Yang was from Shanghai, Mrs. Yang was from Tianjin, and the second Mrs. Yang was from Suzhou. One husband, two wives, speaking different dialects, had countless children. On his first day of work, Xiangzi almost fainted. Early in the morning, the first madam took the rickshaw to the market to buy groceries. Upon returning, she separately sent the young masters and misses to school; some to junior high, some to elementary, some to kindergarten; different schools, different ages, different appearances, but all equally annoying, especially when sitting in the rickshaw, even the most well-behaved ones were more mischievous than monkeys. After all the children were sent off, Mr. Yang went to the yamen. After dropping him off, Xiangzi hurried back to take the second madam to Dong'an Market or to visit relatives. Upon returning, he picked up the students for lunch. After eating, he sent them off again. After sending the students back, Xiangzi thought he could eat, but the first madam, in her Tianjin accent, told him to fetch water. The Yang residence had sweet water delivered, but the laundry water was for the rickshaw pullers to fetch. This task was outside his agreed duties, but Xiangzi, to avoid trouble, didn't dare to argue and silently filled the vat. Putting down the buckets, just as he was about to pick up his rice bowl, the second madam told him to go buy things. The first madam and the second madam had always been at odds, but in household management, their views were consistent: one was not to allow servants a moment of idleness, and the other was not to let servants eat in their sight. Xiangzi didn't know this; he just thought it was a coincidence that the household was so busy on his first day, so he said nothing and bought a few sesame cakes out of his own pocket. He loved money like his life, but to keep the job, he had to harden his heart.

After returning with the purchases, the first madam told him to clean the courtyard. Mr. Yang, Mrs. Yang, and the second Mrs. Yang all dressed beautifully when they went out, but the house and courtyard were like a big garbage dump. Xiangzi felt sick looking at the courtyard, so he just focused on cleaning and forgot that rickshaw pullers were not also responsible for odd jobs. After the courtyard was clean, the second madam told him to also sweep the rooms while he was at it. Xiangzi didn't refuse; what surprised him was how, despite the two madams' elegance and beauty, the rooms could be so dirty that one couldn't even walk! After tidying up the rooms, the second madam handed him a little mud-devil of a child, just one week old. He was at a loss. He was good at hard labor, but he had never held a baby. He held the little young master with both hands, not daring to use too little force for fear he might slip, and not daring to use too much force for fear of injuring his bones; he broke into a sweat. He wanted to hand this precious baby over to Aunt Zhang—a big-footed woman from north of the Yangtze River. When he found her, she immediately cursed him out. The Yang household's servants were always replaced every three to five days; Mr. and Mrs. Yang always believed that servants were mere house slaves, and unless they worked the poor to death, they weren't earning their wages. Only Aunt Zhang had been with them for five or six years, the sole reason being that she dared to curse openly, regardless of Mr. or Mrs. Yang; if she was annoyed, she would unleash a torrent of abuse. With Mr. Yang's vicious Shanghai curses, Mrs. Yang's booming Tianjin accent, and the second madam's fluent Suzhou dialect, they had always been invincible; but when they met Aunt Zhang's ferocity, they began to feel a sense of "an eye for an eye," a meeting of equals, so they quite appreciated her and took her in as their trusted aide.

Xiangzi was born in the northern countryside and most disliked casual cursing. But he didn't dare to hit Aunt Zhang, because a good man doesn't fight with women, nor did he want to talk back. He just glared at her. Aunt Zhang stopped speaking, as if sensing some danger. Just then, the first madam called Xiangzi to pick up the students. He quickly returned the mud-doll to the second madam. The second madam thought he was deliberately disrespecting her and immediately cursed him out. The first madam also didn't want Xiangzi to hold the baby for the second madam, and hearing the second madam curse him, she also unleashed her smooth, oily voice to curse him; Xiangzi became the scapegoat. He quickly pulled the rickshaw and left, seemingly even forgetting his anger, because he had never seen such a thing before, and suddenly encountering it, he felt a bit dizzy.

Batch after batch of children were brought back, and the courtyard was even livelier than a market. The curses of three women and the cries of a group of children made it as chaotic as Dashilar when a play ended, and inexplicably so. Fortunately, he still had to pick up Mr. Yang, so he hurried out again. The shouts of people and horses on the main street seemed a bit more bearable than the chaos in the house.

It wasn't until twelve o'clock that Xiangzi found a moment to sigh. He not only felt physically exhausted, but his head was also buzzing; the Yang family, young and old, were indeed all asleep, but in his ears, he still seemed to hear the shouts and curses of Mr. and Mrs. Yang, like three different phonographs spinning wildly in his mind, making him agitated. He couldn't think of anything else; he just wanted to sleep. As soon as he entered his small room, his heart grew cold, and he was no longer sleepy. It was a gatehouse, with two doors, separated by a wooden partition. Aunt Zhang lived on one side, and he on the other. There was no light in the room; on the street-facing wall, there was a small window about two feet wide, directly under a street lamp, providing a little light to the room. The room was damp and smelly, the dirt on the floor was as thick as a copper coin, and a sleeping board was placed against the wall, with nothing else. He felt the bed board and knew that if he lay his head down, his feet would have to be propped against the wall; if he laid his feet flat, he would have to sit halfway up. He couldn't sleep in a "yuanbao" (ingot) position. After thinking for a long time, he pulled the sleeping board diagonally, so that both ends were against the corners, allowing him to lay his head flat and let his legs dangle a bit, just to get through the night.

He moved his bedding in through the doorway, roughly spread it out, and lay down. His legs dangled in the air, unaccustomed, and he couldn't sleep. He forced his eyes shut, comforting himself: "Sleep, you have to get up early tomorrow! You've endured all sorts of hardships, why can't you endure this one? Don't mind the poor food and drink, the tiring work, maybe there are often card games, entertaining guests, and banquets [1]. What are we out here for, Xiangzi? Isn't it for money? As long as we earn more money, we have to endure anything!" Thinking this, he felt much better. He sniffed the room, and it no longer smelled as bad as before. Slowly, he drifted into a dream. He vaguely felt bedbugs, but he didn't bother to deal with them.

Two days later, Xiangzi's heart had sunk to the bottom. But on the fourth day, female guests arrived, and Aunt Zhang was busy setting up the card table. His heart was like a small frozen lake suddenly touched by a spring breeze. When the madams played cards, they handed all the children over to the servants; since Aunt Zhang had to serve tea, cigarettes, and towels, the group of little monkeys naturally fell under Xiangzi's command. He disliked these monkeys, but he secretly glanced into the room and saw the first madam managing the "head money," looking very serious. He thought to himself: "Don't underestimate this formidable woman, she might not be foolish, she knows to take advantage of such times to get an extra three or five cents for the servants." He showed extra patience with the monkeys, for the sake of the "head money," he had to treat these little rascals as young masters and misses.

The card game ended, and the madam told him to take the guests home. The two female guests were in a hurry to leave at the same time, so another rickshaw had to be hired. Xiangzi called one over, and the first madam, with her robes trailing, fumbled for money all over her body, preparing to pay for the guests' rickshaw fare; the guests politely declined a couple of times, and the first madam shouted as if she were fighting for her life: "What's wrong with you, my dear sister! You're at my place, don't you have rickshaw money! My dear sister! Get in!" Only then did she pull out a dime.

Xiangzi saw clearly that when she handed over the dime, the madam's hand trembled a little.

After seeing off the guests and helping Aunt Zhang put away the card table and other things, Xiangzi glanced at the madam. The madam told Aunt Zhang to get some boiled water, and after Aunt Zhang left the room, she took out a dime: "Take this, don't keep staring at me!"

Xiangzi's face suddenly turned purple. He straightened his back, as if his head would hit the ceiling beam, grabbed the paper bill, and threw it on the madam's plump face: "Give me four days' wages!" "What about you?" The madam said this, then glanced at Xiangzi again, said nothing, and gave him four days' wages. As soon as he walked out the gate with his bedding, he heard cursing erupt in the courtyard.

[1]

 Banquet, i.e., a feast.

Six

On an early autumn night, with gusts of wind rustling through starlight and leaf shadows, Xiangzi looked up at the distant Milky Way and sighed. It was such a cool night, and his chest was so broad, yet he felt as if there wasn't enough air, and his chest was very constricted. He wanted to sit down and cry bitterly. With his physique, his endurance, and his strong will, to be treated like a pig or a dog, to be unable to keep a job, he not only resented the Yang family but also felt a vague sense of hopelessness, fearing that he would never have any improvement in his life. Pulling his bedding roll, he walked slower and slower, as if he was no longer the Xiangzi who could run ten or eight li just by lifting his legs.

On the main street, there were few pedestrians, but the streetlights were bright, making him feel even more vast and desolate, not knowing where to go. Where to go? Naturally, back to Renhe Factory. He felt a little sad again. For those who do business, who work hard, they are not afraid of having no business, but rather afraid of having a patron [1] and not closing a deal, like customers entering a restaurant or barbershop, taking a look, and then leaving. Xiangzi knew that starting and quitting jobs were common occurrences; if one place didn't keep him, another would. However, he had maintained the job humbly, sacrificing his dignity to buy a rickshaw, and yet it still ended up being a three-and-a-half-day affair, just like those old hands who frequented wealthy households. He felt heartbroken. He almost felt too ashamed to enter Renhe Factory again and become a laughingstock for everyone: "Look, Camel Xiangzi also quit after three and a half days, huh!"

If not Renhe Factory, then where to go? To avoid thinking about this matter again, he walked straight towards Xi'anmen Street. The front of Renhe Factory consisted of three shop rooms, the middle one serving as the office, where rickshaw pullers were only allowed to come to settle accounts or discuss matters, and were not allowed to casually walk through, as the east and west rooms were the bedrooms of the Liu family father and daughter. Next to the west room was a rickshaw gate, two large green-painted doors, with a thick iron bar bent over them, from which hung a very bright, unshaded electric light. Under the light, four gold-painted iron characters hung horizontally—"Renhe Rickshaw Factory." Rickshaw pullers used this gate for going out, returning, and coming and going at any time. The deep green paint on the gate, paired with the gold characters, glowed under the bright white electric light. The rickshaws coming and going were all beautiful, black-painted and yellow-painted, all gleaming with oil, paired with snow-white seat covers, making even the rickshaw pullers feel a sense of pride, as if they considered themselves the aristocracy among rickshaw pullers. Entering through the main gate and turning past the west room of the front, there was a square courtyard with an old locust tree in the middle. The east and west rooms were all open-fronted, serving as storage for rickshaws; the south room and the few small rooms in the small courtyard behind the south room were all dormitories for rickshaw pullers.

It was probably past eleven o'clock when Xiangzi saw the very bright but strangely lonely light of Renhe Factory. The office and the east room had no lights, but the west room was still lit. He knew that Miss Hu hadn't slept yet. He wanted to go in quietly, so Miss Hu wouldn't see him; precisely because she usually thought highly of him, he didn't want her to be the first to witness his failure.

He had just pulled the rickshaw to her window when Huniu came out of the rickshaw gate:

"Oh, Xiangzi? Wh—" She was about to ask further, but seeing Xiangzi's dejected look and the bedding roll on the rickshaw, she swallowed her words.

What he feared came true. Xiangzi's shame and frustration condensed into a ball, and he immediately stopped, standing there dumbfounded. Unable to speak, he stared blankly at Miss Hu. She was also unusual today; whether it was the electric light or if she had powdered her face, her face was much whiter than usual; with a whiter face, much of her fierceness was concealed. There was indeed some rouge on her lips, which gave Huniu a touch of charm. Seeing this, Xiangzi felt very strange, and his heart became even more flustered, because he had never regarded her as a woman before, and suddenly seeing those red lips, he felt a sudden sense of embarrassment. She was wearing a light green silk padded jacket on top, and a pair of dark blue crepe wide-legged trousers below. The green jacket shimmered with a soft, slightly melancholic silk sheen under the electric light, and because it was short, a bit of white waistband was exposed, making the green even more distinct and plain. The wide black trousers below swayed slightly in the breeze, like some eerie aura trying to break free from the bright light and merge with the darkness. Xiangzi dared not look anymore, lowering his head blankly, his mind still holding a small, glowing green jacket. Given the Liu family's wealth, she could have worn silk and satin every day, but constantly dealing with rickshaw pullers, she always wore plain clothes, and even if there were patterns, they were inconspicuous on cloth.Xiangzi seemed to see something very new and strange, both familiar and unfamiliar, so his heart was a little flustered.

His heart was already troubled, and under the extremely strong light, he met this strange living thing. He lost his composure. Since he didn't want to move, he hoped that Miss Hu would quickly go into the house, or order him to do something. He simply couldn't stand such torment, a torment that was nothing like anything else and very difficult to bear.

"Hey!" She took a step forward and said in a low voice, "Don't just stand there! Go, put the rickshaw away, and come back quickly. I have something to tell you. See you inside."

Having been accustomed to helping her with things, he had no choice but to obey. But today she was different from usual, and he really wanted to think about it. Standing there thinking felt awkward. He was undecided and pulled the rickshaw inside. He looked at the south room; there was no light, so everyone was probably asleep; or perhaps some rickshaws hadn't been put away yet. After putting the rickshaw away, he returned to her door. Suddenly, his heart pounded.

"Come in, I have something to tell you!" She poked her head out, half-smiling, half-annoyed. He slowly walked in.

On the table were several unripe white pears, their skins still green. A wine pot and three white porcelain wine cups. A large platter held half a braised chicken and some smoked liver and tripe.

"Look," Miss Hu pointed to a chair for him, and only after seeing him sit down did she say, "Look, I'm having a celebratory meal today, you should have some too!" With that, she poured him a cup of wine. The spicy taste of baijiu, mixed with the flavor of smoked and braised meat, seemed particularly rich and heavy. "Drink up, eat this chicken. I've already eaten, no need to be polite! I just cast a divination with dominoes, and I knew you'd be back. Was it accurate?"

"I don't drink!" Xiangzi stared at the wine cup in a daze.

"If you don't drink, then get out. I'm being kind, why aren't you appreciative? You silly camel! It won't burn you to death! Even I can drink four liang. If you don't believe me, look!" She picked up the wine cup, drank more than half of it, closed her eyes, and let out a sigh. Holding up the cup, she said, "You drink! Otherwise, I'll pull your ear and force it down!"

Xiangzi was full of resentment with nowhere to vent, and encountering such teasing, he really wanted to glare at her. But he knew that Miss Hu had always been good to him, and she was always so straightforward with everyone, so he shouldn't offend her. Since he didn't want to offend her, he thought again and decided to just frankly tell her his grievances. He usually wasn't very talkative, but today it seemed as if a thousand words were stifled in his heart, and he wouldn't feel comfortable until he spoke them. With this thought, he felt that Miss Hu wasn't teasing him, but rather genuinely caring for him. He took the wine cup and drank it dry. A spicy sensation slowly, accurately, and powerfully went down. He stretched his neck, straightened his chest, and let out two not-so-convenient burps.

Huniu laughed. He had just managed to swallow the wine, and hearing this laughter, he quickly looked towards the east room.

"No one," she stopped laughing, but a smile still lingered on her face. "My old man went to celebrate my aunt's birthday, it'll be delayed for two or three days. My aunt lives in Nanyuan." As she spoke, she refilled his cup.

Hearing this, his mind took a turn, and he felt that something seemed off somewhere. At the same time, he was reluctant to leave; her face was so close to him, her clothes were so clean and smooth, her lips were so red, all of which gave him a new kind of stimulation. She was still old and ugly, but she had added some vitality compared to usual, as if she had suddenly become another person, still her, but with something more. He dared not think about this new something in detail, nor did he dare to accept it casually for a while, but he also couldn't bear to refuse. His face turned red. As if to bolster his courage, he took another sip of wine. Just now he wanted to tell her his grievances, but now he had forgotten. Blushing, he couldn't help but look at her a few more times. The more he looked, the more confused his heart became. She increasingly revealed something he didn't understand, and a hot, spicy power was increasingly transmitted, gradually turning her into an abstract something. He warned himself to be careful; but he also wanted to be bold. He drank three cups of wine in a row, forgetting what caution was. Staring at her in a daze, he didn't know why he felt extremely happy and bold; he was extremely brave and wanted to immediately grasp a new experience and joy. In the past, he was a little afraid of her; now, she had no frightening qualities at all. He himself, on the contrary, had become majestic and powerful, as if he could hold her in his hand like a cat. The lights in the room went out. The sky was very dark. From time to time, one or two stars pierced the Milky Way, or streaked into the darkness, carrying reddish or whitish light trails, lightly floating or stiffly, falling straight or sweeping horizontally, sometimes also flickering and trembling, giving the sky some luminous and hot turbulence, giving the darkness some shimmering bursts. Sometimes one or two stars, sometimes several stars, fell at the same time, making the silent autumn sky tremble slightly, making all the stars momentarily confused. Sometimes a single giant star pierced horizontally into the corner of the sky, with an extremely long light tail, radiating starbursts; red, gradually yellow; in the final thrust, it suddenly illuminated a strip of the sky white as if in ecstasy, as if piercing through layers of darkness, letting in and lingering some milky white light. As the afterglow dissipated, the darkness seemed to sway a few times, then closed in again, and the quiet, lazy clusters of stars returned to their original positions, smiling in the autumn wind. On the ground, some autumn fireflies seeking mates also played star-like games.

The next day, Xiangzi got up very early, pulled his rickshaw, and went out. His head and throat were a little sore, which was due to drinking for the first time, but he didn't pay attention to it. Sitting at the entrance of a small alley, the morning breeze blew on his head, and he knew that this headache would soon pass. But there were other things in his heart that made him feel stifled, and he had no way to relieve them for a while. What happened last night made him doubtful, ashamed, sad, and also felt a little dangerous.

He didn't understand what was going on with Miss Hu. She was no longer a virgin, Xiangzi had only found out a few hours ago. He had always respected her and had never heard of her doing anything improper; although she was very casual and straightforward with everyone, no one had ever talked about her behind her back; even if some rickshaw pullers spoke ill of her, they only said she was fierce, nothing else. So, why did that happen last night?

This seemed confusing, and Xiangzi also doubted what happened last night. She knew he wasn't at the rickshaw depot, so how could she have been waiting for him wholeheartedly? If it could have been just anyone... Xiangzi lowered his head. He came from the countryside, and although he had never thought about getting married, he wasn't without a plan in his heart: if he had his own rickshaw, his life was a little more comfortable, and he was willing to get married, he would definitely go to the countryside to marry a young, strong, hardworking, capable, and clean girl. For young men his age, even if someone was watching them, who wouldn't secretly run to "white houses [3]"? Xiangzi had always refused to go along with it. Firstly, he considered himself a strong-willed person and couldn't spend money on women; secondly, he had personally seen those fools who wasted money—some only eighteen or nineteen—unable to urinate even when leaning their heads against the wall in the toilet. Finally, he had to be proper to be worthy of his future wife, because once he decided to marry, he would definitely marry a pure and innocent girl, so he also had to be that way. But now, now... Thinking of Huniu, if he considered her a friend, she was indeed good; if he considered her a woman, she was ugly, old, fierce, and shameless! Even thinking of the soldiers who stole his rickshaw and almost took his life wasn't as hateful and disgusting as thinking of her! She had completely destroyed the bit of freshness he brought from the countryside; he was now a man who stole women!

Moreover, if this matter were to be spread and Liu Si found out? Did Liu Si know that his daughter was a loose woman? If he didn't know, wouldn't Xiangzi be the only one to take the blame? If he had known all along but was unwilling to restrain his daughter, then what kind of people were they, father and daughter? If he got involved with such people, what kind of person would he himself be? Even if both father and daughter were willing, he couldn't have her; no matter if old man Liu had sixty rickshaws, or six hundred, or six thousand! He had to leave Renhe Factory immediately and break all ties with them. Xiangzi had his own abilities, and by relying on his own abilities to buy a rickshaw and marry a wife, that would be truly honorable! Thinking of this, he raised his head, feeling that he was a good man, with nothing to fear, nothing to worry about, as long as he worked hard, he would surely succeed.

He gave up his seat twice but couldn't get a fare. That awkward feeling suddenly returned. He didn't want to think anymore, but his heart felt heavy. This matter seemed completely different from other things; even if there was a solution, it wasn't easy to forget casually. Not only did something seem to stick to his body, but a black spot also seemed to appear in his heart, which could never be washed away. No matter how much he resented her, how much he disliked her, she seemed to always hold onto his heart. The more he didn't want to think about her, the more she suddenly jumped out of his heart, a naked her, giving him all her ugliness and beauty at once, completely, like buying a pile of junk, among the broken copper and rotten iron, there were also one or two shining colored small objects that made people unable to refuse. He had never been so intimate with anyone, although it was sudden, although it was a deception, such a relationship could not be easily forgotten. Even if he wanted to put it aside, it would naturally linger in his heart, as if it had taken root. This was not only an experience for him but also an indescribable disturbance that made him not know what to do. He had no way with her, with himself, with the present and the future, as if he were a small insect caught in a spider web, too late to struggle.

In a daze, he pulled a few fares. Even when he was running, he didn't forget this matter in his heart. It wasn't a clear, complete thought, but rather a constant thought of some meaning, or some taste, or some feeling, all vague yet intimate. He really wanted to go drinking alone, to drink until he was unconscious, and perhaps he would feel better, no longer suffering this torment! But he didn't dare to drink. He couldn't ruin himself over this matter. He thought of buying a rickshaw again. But he couldn't concentrate on thinking; there was always something blocking his mind; before he could think of the rickshaw, this thing had already secretly slipped out and occupied his heart, like a black cloud covering the sun, interrupting the light. In the evening, when he planned to put away the rickshaw, he felt even worse. He had to go back to the rickshaw depot, but he was really afraid to go back. What if he met her, what then? He pulled the empty rickshaw around the streets, two or three times he was not far from the depot, then turned back and went elsewhere, much like a child playing truant for the first time who dared not enter his home. The strange thing was, the more he wanted to avoid her, the more he also wanted to meet her. The darker the sky, the stronger this thought became. A kind of boldness and confusion, knowing it was wrong but wanting to try it, tightly gripped his heart. It was like poking a hornet's nest with a stick when he was little, afraid, but his heart was pounding to try it, as if some evil spirit was urging him on. Vaguely, he felt a force more powerful than himself, wanting to knead him into a ball and throw him into a raging fire; he couldn't stop himself from moving forward.

He circled back to Xi'an Gate. This time, he didn't want to hesitate anymore; he wanted to go straight to her, as if entering a public court. She was no longer anyone; she was just a woman. His whole body grew hot. Just as he reached the doorway, a man in his forties walked out into the lamplight. He seemed to recognize the man's face and demeanor but dared not greet him. Almost instinctively, he said, "Rickshaw?" The man paused for a moment, "Xiangzi?" "Yes," Xiangzi smiled. "Mr. Cao?"

Mr. Cao smiled and nodded. "I say, Xiangzi, if you're not working at the mansion, why don't you come back to me? The person I'm using now is too lazy; he never bothers to wipe the rickshaw, although he's quite quick [4]. Are you coming?"

"How could I not come, sir!" Xiangzi seemed to have forgotten how to smile, constantly wiping his face with a small towel. "Sir, when do I start work?"

"Well," Mr. Cao thought for a moment, "the day after tomorrow."

"Alright, sir!" Xiangzi also thought for a moment, "Sir, shall I take you back?" "No need. Didn't I go to Shanghai for a while? After I came back, I don't live in the old place anymore. I'm currently living on Beichang Street; I come out for a walk in the evening. See you the day after tomorrow." Mr. Cao told Xiangzi the house number and added, "I'll still use my own rickshaw."

Xiangzi was so happy he felt like flying. All the troubles of these past days were suddenly swept clean, like a white stone road after a heavy rain. Mr. Cao was his former employer. Although they hadn't been together for long, their relationship was excellent; Mr. Cao was a very kind person, and there were few people in his household, only a wife and a small boy. He pulled the rickshaw straight to Renhe Factory. The light in Miss Hu's room was still on. Seeing this light, Xiangzi suddenly froze there.

After standing for a long time, he decided to go in and see her, tell her that he had found a monthly job again; pay the rickshaw fees for the past two days; take out his savings; and from then on, break all ties—this, of course, was inconvenient to say explicitly, but she would understand.

He went in, first put away the rickshaw, and then came back and boldly called out, "Miss Liu." "Come in!"He pushed open the door, and she was reclining on the bed, dressed in her usual clothes, barefoot. Still reclining, she said, "Well? Have you gotten a taste for the good life or what?"

Xiangzi's face turned as red as an egg given away at a baby's birth. After a long pause, he stammered, "I've found another job, starting the day after tomorrow. They have their own rickshaws..."

She cut him off, "You little brat, you don't know what's good for you!" She sat up, half-smiling, half-annoyed, pointing at him, "You have food and clothes here; why do you insist on going out and sweating your guts out? My old man can't control me, and I can't be a widow for my daughter my whole life! Even if the old man really gets stubborn, I have some savings, and we could get two or three rickshaws, earning a few dimes a day. Isn't that better than you running around the streets all day with stinky feet? What's wrong with me? Besides being a little older than you, it's not by much! I can protect you and care for you!"

"I want to pull a rickshaw!" Xiangzi couldn't find any other rebuttal.

"A real bumpkin brain! Sit down first, I won't bite you!" She finished, smiled, revealing a pair of fangs.

Xiangzi sat down, his veins throbbing. "Where's my money?"

"It's with the old man; it won't get lost, don't be scared; don't even ask him for it yet, do you know his temper? When it's enough to buy a rickshaw, then you can ask, and he won't short you a single penny; if you ask now, it'd be a miracle if he didn't curse your soul out! He's good to you! It won't get lost, if one is missing, I'll pay you two! You country bumpkin! Don't make me insult you!"

Xiangzi had nothing more to say, he lowered his head and fumbled for a long time, then took out two days' rickshaw rent and placed it on the table: "Two days'." He suddenly remembered: "Consider today as returning the rickshaw, I'll rest tomorrow." He didn't want to rest for a day at all; however, this made it seem decisive; once the rickshaw was returned, he would never live in Renhe Factory again.

Tiger Girl came over, grabbed the money in her hand, and stuffed it into his pocket: "These two days, both the rickshaw and the person are free! You're a lucky guy! Just don't be ungrateful!" After saying that, she turned around and double-locked the door.

[1]

 "Zhǔr" (主儿) means "master" or "owner". Here, it refers to the owner of the rented rickshaw.

[2]

 "Chuī" (吹) means "to blow", but here it means "to fall apart" or "to be over".

[3]

 "Bái fángzi" (白房子), literally "white house", refers to the lowest class of brothels.

[4]

 "Málì" (麻利) means "quick" or "nimble".

Seven

Xiangzi arrived at the Cao residence.

He felt a little ashamed about Miss Hu. But since the matter originated from her seduction, and he didn't want to covet her money, he thought that breaking off all ties with her from now on wouldn't be too unfair to anyone. What he was worried about was Liu Siye holding onto his money. If he went to ask for it immediately, the old man might become suspicious. If he stopped seeing them, Miss Hu might get angry and say bad things to the old man, causing the money to be "swallowed up." If he continued to let the old man keep his money, he would run into her as soon as he went to Renhe Factory, which would be awkward. He couldn't think of a proper solution, and the more he couldn't, the more uneasy he felt.

He quite wanted to ask Mr. Cao for advice, but how could he explain it? The part about Miss Hu was something he couldn't tell anyone. Thinking of this, he truly regretted it. He began to understand that this matter couldn't be severed cleanly. Such things could never be washed away, like a black blemish on the flesh. Losing his rickshaw for no reason, and then getting entangled in this mess for no reason, he felt that his life was probably over, and no matter how strong-willed he was, it would all be in vain. After much thought, he realized this: in the end, he might still have to shamelessly pursue Miss Hu; not for her, but for those few rickshaws, right? "A pimp gets to eat two stir-fried meat dishes!" He couldn't bear it, but when the time came, it might be unavoidable! He could only keep moving forward, hoping for the best, expecting the worst; he no longer dared to be as confident as before. His stature, strength, and courage were all insignificant; his life was his own, but it was controlled by others; controlled by some utterly despicable things.

Logically speaking, he should have been very happy, because the Cao residence was the most pleasant among all the households he had worked in. The wages at the Cao residence were not higher than elsewhere, and besides the holiday bonuses, there wasn't much extra money. However, Mr. and Mrs. Cao were very kind and treated everyone as human beings. Xiangzi wanted to earn more money, and he worked desperately to earn it, but he also wanted a place to live that was like a room, and enough food to eat. The Cao residence was clean everywhere, even the servants' quarters; the food at the Cao residence was not bad, and they never gave rotten food to the servants. He had a spacious room of his own, and he could eat three meals in peace, plus the hosts were very polite. Xiangzi, even Xiangzi, was no longer solely focused on money. Moreover, the living conditions were suitable, the work was not tiring, and keeping himself healthy was not a disadvantage. If he had to pay for his own food, he would never eat so well. Now that there was ready-made food, and it wouldn't go to waste, why shouldn't he eat his fill? Food also cost money, and he understood this very clearly. Furthermore, although the Cao family didn't play cards, didn't often entertain guests, and didn't have much extra money, they could always get a dime or two for doing some temporary work. For example, if the mistress asked him to buy medicine for the child, she would always give him an extra dime and tell him to take a rickshaw, even though she knew he could run faster than anyone. This small amount of money was nothing, but it made him feel a sense of human kindness, a consideration that made him feel happy. Xiangzi had met quite a few masters, and nine out of ten would delay paying wages by a day if they could, showing that it was best to use people for free, and that servants were essentially cats and dogs, or even worse than cats and dogs. The Cao family was an exception, which is why he liked it here. He would clean the yard and water the flowers without being told, and whenever they saw him doing these things, they would say kind words. They would also take advantage of such times to find some old, worn-out items and tell him to exchange them for matches, even though those items were still usable, and he would keep them for himself. Here, he felt a touch of humanity.

In Xiangzi's eyes, Liu Siye could be considered Huang Tianba. Although formidable, he valued reputation and his name, and was never completely ruthless. The respectable figures in his mind, besides Huang Tianba, had to be Confucius. He had no idea what kind of person Confucius was, but it was said that he knew many characters and was quite reasonable. Among the households he had worked in, there were both scholarly and martial types; among the martial types, there wasn't even one who could compare to Liu Siye; among the scholarly types, although there were professors teaching at universities and officials holding good positions in government offices, who certainly knew many characters, he hadn't met a single reasonable one. Even if the master was somewhat reasonable, the mistresses and young ladies were very difficult to serve. Only Mr. Cao was both literate and reasonable, and Mrs. Cao was also proper and well-liked. Therefore, Mr. Cao must be Confucius, and if Xiangzi couldn't imagine what Confucius looked like, then he must be like Mr. Cao, whether Confucius liked it or not.

In reality, Mr. Cao was not that brilliant. He was just an ordinary person who sometimes taught and sometimes did other things. He considered himself a "socialist" and also an aesthetician, somewhat influenced by William Morris. In politics and art, he didn't have profound insights; but he had one good quality: the little he believed in, he could implement in the small events of life. He seemed to realize that he didn't have astonishing talent to accomplish earth-shattering deeds, so he arranged his work and family according to his ideals; although it didn't benefit society, he at least wanted his words and actions to be consistent, not to be a hypocrite. Therefore, he paid great attention to small things, as if to say that as long as the small family was well-managed, then society could be whatever it wanted. This sometimes made him feel ashamed, and sometimes made him happy, as if he clearly saw that his family was a small oasis in the desert, only able to provide some clear water and food to those who came there, with no greater meaning.

Xiangzi happened to arrive at this small oasis; after walking in the desert for so many days, he thought it was a miracle. He had never met anyone like Mr. Cao, so he regarded him as a sage. Perhaps this was due to his limited experience, or perhaps such people were rare in the world. When he pulled Mr. Cao out, Mr. Cao's clothes were so elegant, and he was so lively and generous, and Xiangzi himself was so clean, neat, strong, and robust, that he ran with extra joy, as if only he was worthy of pulling Mr. Cao. At home, everything was so clean and always so quiet, making him feel comfortable and at peace. When he was in the countryside, he often saw old people sitting silently in winter or under the autumn moon, puffing on bamboo pipes. Although he was still young and couldn't imitate these old people, he loved to watch them sitting so quietly, it must be—he speculated—that there was some kind of flavor to it. Now, although he was in the city, the tranquility of the Cao residence was enough to remind him of the countryside, and he truly wished he could smoke a pipe and ponder some kind of flavor.

Unfortunately, that woman and that money made him uneasy; his heart was like a green leaf, entangled by a worm with silk, preparing to make a cocoon. Because of this matter, he couldn't put his mind at ease; to others, even to Mr. Cao, he often spaced out and gave irrelevant answers. This made him very sad. The Cao residence went to bed early, and by past nine in the evening, there was nothing to do. He sat alone in his room or in the yard, tossing and turning, thinking about these two things. He even thought of getting married immediately, believing that this would surely put an end to Huniu's thoughts. But how could he support a family by pulling a rickshaw? He knew the plight of the poor fellows in the crowded courtyards, where men pulled rickshaws, women mended clothes, children picked up coal cinders, scavenged watermelon rinds to chew on dirt piles in summer, and all went to the soup kitchens in winter. Xiangzi couldn't bear this. Moreover, if he got married, the money in old man Liu's hands would definitely not be recovered; how could Huniu let him off easily! He couldn't give up that money, it was earned with his life!

His own rickshaw was bought in early autumn last year. More than a year had passed, and now he had nothing but over thirty yuan that he couldn't get back, and a lot of entanglement! The more he thought about it, the more unhappy he became.

It had been more than ten days since the Mid-Autumn Festival, and the weather was gradually getting cooler. He calculated that he needed to add two more layers of clothing. More money! If he bought clothes, he couldn't save money at the same time, and the hope of buying a rickshaw, he simply dared not hope anymore! Even if he pulled a rickshaw by the month forever, what would his life amount to?

One evening, Mr. Cao returned a little late from the East City. Xiangzi, being careful, walked all the way on the road in front of Tiananmen. The flat road had few people, a slight cool breeze, and quiet streetlights. He ran with vigor. The long-held frustration in his heart was temporarily forgotten. Listening to his own footsteps and the soft sound of the rickshaw's shafts, he forgot everything. He unbuttoned his shirt, and the cool breeze blew against his chest, making him feel refreshed, as if he could just keep running, running until he didn't know where, and even dying while running would be straightforward. He ran faster and faster. There was a rickshaw ahead, and he "drove" one, passing Tiananmen in a short while. His feet seemed like two springs, almost bouncing up as soon as they touched the ground; the rear wheels spun so fast that their spokes were indistinguishable, and the rubber tires seemed to have left the ground, as if both he and the rickshaw were being blown by a gust of wind. Mr. Cao, perhaps half-asleep from the cool breeze, would otherwise have stopped Xiangzi from running so fast. Xiangzi was running freely, vaguely thinking that after a good sweat, he could sleep soundly tonight and wouldn't have to worry about anything anymore.

Not far from Beichang Street, the northern half of the road was very dark, shaded by the locust trees outside the red wall. Xiangzi was about to slow down when his foot hit something raised. His foot hit it, and the rickshaw wheel hit it too. Xiangzi fell out. Crack, the rickshaw shaft broke. "What happened?" Mr. Cao tumbled out with his words. Xiangzi didn't make a sound, and got up from the ground. Mr. Cao also sat up quickly. "What happened?"

A newly unloaded pile of stones for road repair, but no red light was placed.

"Did you fall?" Xiangzi asked.

"No. I'll walk back, you pull the rickshaw." Mr. Cao remained calm, feeling around the stones to see if anything had fallen.

Xiangzi felt the broken shaft: "It's not broken much, sir, you can still sit, I can pull it!" Saying that, he pulled the rickshaw out of the stones. "Sit down, sir!"

Mr. Cao didn't want to sit anymore, but hearing the crying in Xiangzi's voice, he had no choice but to get in.

Under the electric light at the intersection of Beichang Street, Mr. Cao saw a patch of skin scraped off his right hand. "Xiangzi, stop!"

Xiangzi turned around, his face covered in blood.

Mr. Cao was frightened and couldn't think of what to say, "You quickly, quickly—"

Xiangzi was puzzled, thinking he was being told to run quickly, so he straightened his back and ran all the way home in one breath.

Putting down the rickshaw, he saw blood on Mr. Cao's hand and rushed into the courtyard, wanting to ask the mistress for medicine.

"Don't worry about me, look at yourself first!" Mr. Cao ran inside. Xiangzi looked at himself and began to feel pain. Both knees and his right elbow were broken; on his cheek, he thought it was sweat, but it turned out to be blood. Without bothering to do or think anything, he sat on the stone steps of the gate arch, staring blankly at the rickshaw with the broken shaft. The brand new, black lacquered rickshaw had its shaft broken off, revealing two bald, white wood splinters, very incongruous and ugly, like a beautifully made paper figure without feet, just two bare sorghum stalks sticking out. Xiangzi stared blankly at these two white wood splinters. "Xiangzi!" Gao Ma, the Cao family's maid, called out loudly, "Xiangzi! Where are you?"

He sat motionless, staring intently at the broken rickshaw shaft, those two white wood splinters seemed to pierce his heart.

"What's wrong with you! Not a sound, hiding here; look, you scared me! The master is calling you!" Gao Ma's words always mixed matters and emotions, appearing both complex and moving. She was a widow in her early thirties, clean, straightforward, and efficient and meticulous in her work. Elsewhere, some people found her too assertive, opinionated, and often a bit mysterious. The Cao family liked to employ clean and bright people, and didn't pay much attention to minor issues[1], so she had been with them for two or three years, and even when the entire Cao family went elsewhere, they always took her with them. "The master is calling you!" she repeated. When Xiangzi stood up, she saw the blood on his face: "You scared me to death, my goodness! What happened? Why aren't you moving? You'll get tetanus! Hurry up! The master has medicine!"

Xiangzi walked in front, and Gao Ma chattered behind, entering the study together. Mrs. Cao was also there, bandaging the master's hand. Seeing Xiangzi enter, she also exclaimed, "Oh!"

"Madam, he really took quite a fall this time." Gao Ma, fearing that the mistress wouldn't notice, busily poured cold water into the washbasin and spoke even more busily: "I knew it all along, he doesn't care about his life when he runs, something was bound to happen sooner or later. And sure enough! Why don't you hurry up and wash? After washing, put some medicine on, alas!"

Xiangzi held his right elbow, motionless. The study was so clean and elegant, and a big man with a bloody face standing there looked very out of place. Everyone seemed to feel that something was wrong, even Gao Ma fell silent.

"Sir!" Xiangzi lowered his head, his voice very low but powerful: "Sir, please find someone else! Keep this month's wages to fix the rickshaw: the shaft is broken, and the glass of the left lamp is shattered; everything else is fine."

"Wash up first, put some medicine on, then we'll talk about other things." Mr. Cao said, looking at his hand, as his wife slowly wrapped gauze around it."Go wash up first!" Gao Ma also remembered what she wanted to say. "Mr. Cao didn't say anything, don't you go blaming him first!"

Xiangzi still didn't move. "No need to wash, it'll be fine in a moment! A monthly hire, who threw someone, hit a car, has no face to..." His words weren't enough to fully express his meaning, but his emotions had been completely vented, all that was left was to cry out loud. Resigning and forfeiting wages, in Xiangzi's eyes, was almost like suicide. But responsibility and face, at this moment, seemed more important than life itself, because it wasn't just anyone he had thrown, but Mr. Cao. If he had thrown that Mrs. Yang, then so be it, she deserved it! With Mrs. Yang, he could display the rudeness of the streets, because she didn't treat him like a human, so he didn't need to be polite; money was everything, there was no talk of face, no rules. Mr. Cao was fundamentally not that kind of person, he had to sacrifice money to save face. He couldn't hate anyone, he only hated his own fate, he almost thought: once he left the Cao family, he would never pull a rickshaw again; even if his own life was worthless, he could risk it; but what about other people's lives? What if he really killed someone by throwing them? He hadn't thought of this before, but because he had injured Mr. Cao this time, he understood this principle. All right, he could forgo the wages, change professions from now on, and no longer do this job that involved human lives. Pulling a rickshaw was his ideal profession, giving it up was like abandoning hope. He felt that his whole life would be spent in a muddle, and he didn't even need to think about becoming a good rickshaw puller, he had grown such a big body for nothing! When he was pulling individual fares outside, he had unceremoniously "snatched" [2]

 business, and was ridiculed by everyone, but such shamelessness was precisely because he wanted to be strong, to buy a rickshaw, he could forgive himself. Now that he had caused trouble as a monthly hire, what could he say? If people found out, Xiangzi threw someone, damaged a car; what kind of rickshaw puller is that, what a piece of trash! Xiangzi had no way out! He couldn't wait for Mr. Cao to dismiss him, he had to leave himself!

"Xiangzi," Mr. Cao's hand was already bandaged, "Go wash up! Don't talk about resigning yet. It's not your fault, there should have been a red light where the stone was. Forget it, wash up, put some medicine on it."

"Yes, sir," Gao Ma remembered what she wanted to say again, "Xiangzi is too embarrassed; originally, to throw the sir like this! But, since the sir said it's not your fault, you don't need to be so awkward anymore! Look at him, strong and healthy, yet still like a child, he's really worried! Madam, say something, tell him not to worry!" Gao Ma's words were like a gramophone record, spoken in circles, including everyone without any trace of beginning, development, transition, or conclusion.

"Hurry up and wash, I'm scared!" Mrs. Cao only said this one sentence.

Xiangzi's mind was in a mess, and finally, hearing his wife say she was afraid of blood, he seemed to find something that could comfort her. He moved the washbasin out and washed his hands a few times at the study door. Gao Ma stood inside the door with the medicine bottle, waiting for him.

"What about your arms and legs?" Gao Ma applied medicine to his face for a while. Xiangzi shook his head, "It's nothing!"

Mr. and Mrs. Cao went to rest. Gao Ma, holding the medicine bottle, followed Xiangzi out. When they reached his room, she put down the medicine bottle and stood at the doorway: "You can apply it yourself later. I say, there's no need to be so foolish over such a small matter. In the past, when my old man was alive, I often resigned. Firstly, I suffered outside, and he wasn't strong, which made me angry. Secondly, I was young and hot-headed, if a word didn't suit me, I'd leave! Working hard for money isn't being a slave; you have your stinking money, but I, a clay figure, also have my earthy nature; the old lady won't be served! Now I'm much better, since my old man died, I have nothing to worry about, and my temper has improved a bit. Here – I've been here for almost three years; it's true, I started work on the ninth of September – the small change is too little, but they treat people pretty well. We sell our labor for money; just talking about good things doesn't count for much. But then again, looking at things long-term also has its benefits: quitting every three days, taking six months off a year, isn't worth it; it's better to meet a kind boss, and if you work for a long time, even if the small change is less, you can still save some money by getting by regularly. As for today's matter, since the sir didn't say anything, let it be, why bother. It's not that I'm trying to be superior, you're still a young man, easily angered. There's no need at all, a strong temper won't get you food. For someone as honest as you, settling down here for a while is always better than flying around aimlessly. I'm not speaking for them at all, I'm speaking for you, we're all pretty good together!" She took a breath: "Alright, see you tomorrow. Don't be stubborn, I'm straightforward, I say what I mean!"

Xiangzi's right elbow hurt a lot, and he couldn't sleep all night. After much deliberation, he felt that Gao Ma's words made sense. Everything else was fake, only money was real. Save money to buy a rickshaw; getting angry won't get you food! Thinking of this, a little peaceful sleepiness came over him.

[1]

 "Guo jier," details, small rules.

[2]

 To "snatch" business means to take over a business that someone else is currently doing.

Remember to take a break if you're tired~

Learning resource sharing eight

Mr. Cao had the rickshaw repaired and did not deduct Xiangzi's wages. Mrs. Cao gave him two "Sanhuang Baola" pills, but he didn't take them. He didn't mention resigning again. Although he felt a bit embarrassed for several days, Gao Ma's words ultimately won. After some time, life returned to its routine, and he gradually forgot about the incident, and all his hopes sprouted anew. When he sat alone in his room, his eyes shone brightly as he calculated how to save money and how to buy a rickshaw; he kept muttering to himself, as if he had a secret worry. His calculations were not very clever, but he often chanted "six times six is thirty-six" in his mind and out loud; this had little to do with the amount of money he had, but by chanting it, his mind seemed to feel more substantial, truly like he had an account book.

He had considerable admiration for Gao Ma, feeling that this woman had more insight and ability than ordinary men, and that her words were deeply rooted. He didn't dare to approach her for idle chat, but if he met her in the courtyard or at the door and she had time to say a few words, he was very willing to listen. Every time she spoke, it was enough for him to ponder for half a day, so whenever he met her, he would give a silly smile, letting her know that he admired her words, and she would feel a bit proud, even if she didn't have time, she would still chat for a few sentences.

However, regarding the handling of money, he dared not blindly follow her advice. Her advice, he thought, was not bad at all; but it was somewhat risky. He was very willing to listen to her, to learn more tricks, feeling more at ease in his mind; but in practice, he still stuck to his old idea – not to easily part with money.

Indeed, Gao Ma was resourceful: ever since she became a widow, she would lend out the small amount of money she could save each month, whether it was one yuan or two yuan, to servants, second and third-class policemen, and small vendors, with an interest rate of at least three percent. These people often became desperate for a yuan, their eyes red, and even if someone lent them a yuan but counted it as two, they would still reach out for it. Other than this, money would not let them see it; the money they saw was poisoned, and taking it would drain their blood, but they still had to take it. Anything that could give them a moment's respite, they would have the courage to take; life was about taking a breath first, and tomorrow would explain tomorrow's. Gao Ma, when her husband was alive, had suffered from this poison. Her husband would come home drunk looking for her, and she couldn't get rid of him without a yuan; if she didn't have it, he would make a drunken scene outside the mansion gate; she had no choice, no matter how high the interest, she had to borrow that yuan immediately. From this experience, she learned this method, not out of revenge, but treating it as reasonable, almost a charitable act of emergency relief. There were those in urgent need of money, and those willing to lend it out, like Zhou Yu fighting Huang Gai, both willing to give and receive blows!

In principle, since she believed there was nothing wrong with it, then in terms of method, she had to be a bit ruthless, not letting money go to waste; she meant what she said. This required foresight, tactics, caution, and boldness, so as not to let all the "eagles" [1] fly away. She spent no less effort than a bank manager, because she needed more caution and prudence. Capital may vary in size, but the principle is the same, because this is a capitalist society, like a very fine and large sieve, sifting money down from the top little by little, the less money there is, the further down it goes; at the same time, it also sifts principles, but there are just as many above and below, because principles are not afraid of small sieve holes like money, they are intangible, and can slip through any tiny hole. Everyone said Gao Ma was formidable, and she herself admitted it; her formidable nature was forged through hardship and torment. Whenever she thought of past sufferings, even her own husband being so heartless and unreasonable, she would grit her teeth. She could be very kind, and also very vicious, she knew that without being so, she could not survive in this world.

She also advised Xiangzi to lend out money, entirely out of good intentions, and if he was willing, she could help him: "Let me tell you, Xiangzi, if it stays in your pocket, one coin will always be one coin! If you lend it out, money will make money! No mistake, what are our eyes for? Look carefully before letting go of the money, don't let a bald-tailed eagle fly away. If a policeman doesn't pay interest on time, or doesn't return the principal, go find his police officer! In short, his job will be on the line, dare! Find out when they get paid, and block their nest; if they don't pay back, then what! One to ten, no matter who you lend to, we must have a bottom line; alright, if you lend it out and fish for a pot in the sea, how can that work? Listen to me, you won't go wrong!"

Xiangzi didn't need to say anything, his expression was enough to show that he greatly admired Gao Ma's words. But when he calculated it alone, he felt that money in his own hands was more secure than anything else. True, this way it was dead money, money wouldn't make money; but it was also true that it wouldn't get lost. He carefully took out the few yuan he had saved in the past two or three months – all silver dollars – and turned them over one by one, afraid of making a sound; the silver dollars were so bright, solid, and eye-catching, he felt even more that he absolutely could not let go of them, unless it was to buy a rickshaw. Everyone had their own methods, and he couldn't completely follow Gao Ma.

Originally, at a family named Fang, all the members of the household, including the servants, had savings accounts at the post office. Mrs. Fang had also advised Xiangzi: "You can open an account with one yuan, why don't you open one? As the saying goes, always think of days without when you have, don't wait until you have nothing to wish for something; you're young, why don't you save a few while you're young and strong, three hundred and sixty days a year can't all be sunny days. This is not troublesome, it's reliable, and it earns interest, and you can withdraw some when you need it, how much more convenient can it be? Go, go get a form, you can't write, I'll fill it out for you, with good intentions!"

Xiangzi knew she meant well, and he also knew that the cook Wang Liu and the wet nurse Qin Ma both had accounts, and he really wanted to try it. But one day, Miss Fang asked him to deposit ten yuan, and he carefully looked at the small passbook, which had words and small red stamps on it; all in all, humph, it was about as thick as a small stack of toilet paper. He handed over the money, and they drew a few words and stamped a small seal on the passbook. He felt that even if this wasn't a scam, it had to be a scam; putting in bright silver dollars, and they just drew a few lines and stamped it, Xiangzi wouldn't fall for this trick. He suspected that the Fang family had a connection with the post office business – he always thought the post office was a business with branches everywhere, probably a very old brand, at least comparable to Ruifuxiang and Hongji – which was why they were so enthusiastic about bringing in business. Even if that wasn't the case, cash in hand was ultimately better than in a small passbook, much better! The money in the passbook was just a few words!
