"I can't! I won't make money! I can pull a rickshaw, I love pulling a rickshaw!" The veins on Xiangzi's head throbbed.

"Let me tell you, you're not allowed to pull a rickshaw! I won't allow you to be covered in stinky sweat, stinking up my bed! You have your ideas, I have mine, let's see who can be more stubborn! You married a wife, but I paid for it, you didn't spend a single penny. Think about it, who should listen to whom between us?"

Xiangzi was speechless again.

[1]

Three months' worth, meaning the first month's rent payment included three months' rent.

Sixteen

Idle until the Lantern Festival, Xiangzi couldn't bear it anymore.

Huniu was very happy. She busied herself cooking tangyuan and making dumplings, visiting temples during the day and enjoying the lantern displays at night. She wouldn't allow Xiangzi to have any opinions, but she always kept his mouth full, finding ways to buy and make him fresh things to eat. In the large courtyard, there were seven or eight households, most living in a single room; some rooms housed seven or eight people, old and young. Some of these people pulled rickshaws, some ran small businesses, some were policemen, and some were servants. Everyone had their own affairs, and no one had any free time, even the small children carried small baskets, going out to get porridge in the morning and picking up coal cinders in the afternoon. Only the very youngest children played or fought in the courtyard, their bottoms frozen red. Ash, dust, and dirty water were all dumped in the courtyard, and no one bothered to clean it up. The middle of the courtyard was covered in ice, and the older children, returning from picking coal cinders, used it as an ice rink, shouting and sliding around. The most miserable were the old people and women. The old people had no clothes or food, lying on cold kangs, waiting for the young ones to earn a little money so they could have a bowl of porridge. The young laborers might earn money, or they might come back empty-handed, and then they would lose their temper and pick fights. The old people, with empty stomachs, had to swallow tears like water. The women had to take care of both the old and the young, and also appease the young men who earned money. They had to work as usual even when pregnant, eating only steamed corn bread and sweet potato porridge; no, not only did they have to work as usual, but they also had to go get porridge and solicit odd jobs—fortunately, when the old and young were full and lying down, they had to hold a small kerosene lamp to wash, make, and mend clothes for others. The rooms were so small, the walls so dilapidated, cold wind would seep in through cracks on one side and exit through the other, carrying away all the warmth. They wore only rags, their stomachs holding a bowl or half a bowl of porridge, or perhaps a six or seven-month-old fetus. They had to work, they had to ensure the old and young ate first. Their bodies were full of ailments, their hair had fallen out before they were thirty, but they couldn't rest for a moment, walking from sickness to death; when they died, coffins had to be begged from "charitable people." The girls, sixteen or seventeen years old, had no trousers, only wrapped in some rags indoors—a natural prison—helping their mothers with chores, rushing to finish work. To go to the outhouse, they had to make sure no one was in the courtyard before sneaking out like thieves; all winter, they never saw the sun or the blue sky. The ugly ones would inherit everything from their mothers; the pretty ones, even they knew, would sooner or later be sold by their parents to "enjoy a good life"!

It was in such a mixed courtyard that Huniu felt very proud. She was the only one who had food and clothes, didn't have to worry, and could walk around and sightsee. She held her head high, coming and going, feeling her superiority and also fearing that others would bother her; she ignored the group of suffering people. Almost all the small vendors who came here sold the cheapest things, such as scraped meat, frozen cabbage, raw soy milk, donkey and horse meat, all coming here to find patrons. Since Huniu moved in, vendors selling lamb's head meat, smoked fish, hard steamed buns, braised fried tofu, also shouted their wares at her door. She held a bowl, her head held high, carrying these snacks into the room, and the children all put their iron-bar-like fingers in their mouths, watching her as if she were some princess. She was here to enjoy herself, she couldn't, wouldn't, and didn't want to see the suffering of others.

Xiangzi first disliked her actions, he came from a poor background and knew what hardship was. He didn't want to eat those odds and ends, regretting the money spent. Second, what made him even more uncomfortable was that he figured something out: she wouldn't let him pull a rickshaw, but fed him good food and drink every day, just like fattening a cow to squeeze milk from it! He had completely become her plaything. He had seen it: a skinny old female dog on the street, when in heat, would also choose a strong male dog. Thinking of this, he not only detested this life but also worried about himself. He knew how a hardworking man should protect his body, as the body was everything. If he continued to live like this, he would one day become a dry skeleton, still as big, but empty inside. He shivered. To save his life, he had to go pull a rickshaw immediately, go out and run, run all day, then come back and fall asleep, oblivious to everything; not eating her good food meant not serving her entertainment. He decided to do this, he couldn't give in anymore; if she wanted to pay for a rickshaw, fine; if she didn't, he would rent one. Without a word, he decided to go rent a rickshaw. On the seventeenth, he started pulling a rickshaw, renting it for the "whole day." After pulling two relatively long fares, he felt a problem he hadn't experienced before, his calves were tight, and his hip joints ached. He knew the source of his ailment, but to comfort himself, he thought it was probably because he hadn't pulled a rickshaw for over twenty days, making his legs stiff; after a few more trips, his legs would loosen up, and it would probably be fine.

He picked up another fare, this time a shared rickshaw, four of them going together. Grabbing the rickshaw handles, everyone let a tall man in his forties go first. The tall man smiled, agreeing, he knew the other three rickshaws were all "better" than his own. But he put in effort, even though he knew he couldn't outrun the three young men behind him, he wouldn't rely on his age. After running for more than a li, someone behind him praised him: "How's it going, need some strength? You're really not bad!" He panted and replied: "Running with you guys, how can I be slow?!" He indeed ran not slowly, even Xiangzi had to exert seventy or eighty percent of his strength to keep up with him. But his running style was not pretty: tall, he couldn't bend his waist, his waist and back seemed like a single piece of wood, so his whole body had to lunge forward; his body forward, his hands appeared to be behind; it didn't look like running, but rather like pulling something and drilling forward. With a stiff waist, his hips had to be active; his feet almost dragged on the ground, twisting forward with increased effort. He twisted quite fast, but one could tell he was exerting extreme effort. At turns and corners, he turned stiffly with his whole body, everyone held their breath for him; he always seemed to only care about his body drilling forward, and not whether the rickshaw could pass.

When they arrived, sweat dripped from the tip of his nose and the edges of his ears, continuously. Putting down the rickshaw, he quickly straightened his back and grimaced. When taking the money, his hands trembled as if he couldn't hold anything.

Having pulled a rickshaw together once made them friends, and the four of them parked their rickshaws together. Xiangzi and the others wiped their sweat and started chatting and laughing as usual. The tall man wandered alone for a long time, coughed loudly for a while, spitting out a lot of white foam, before he seemed to recover a bit and started talking to them: "I'm done! I still have the will; but my waist, my legs, they're all out of strength! No matter how I try to lift my waist, my legs won't lift; I'm just anxious!"

"Those two steps just now were not bad, you think you were slow?!" A short young man in his twenties chimed in: "Don't be discouraged, all three of us are pretty good, who didn't sweat?" The tall man was a little proud, but also seemed ashamed, and sighed.

"Just your running style, if it were anyone else, you'd really make them collapse, believe it or not?" another young man said. "It's age, it's not a joke." The tall man smiled, shaking his head: "It's not all about age, brothers! Let me tell you something true, for those of us in this line of work, don't get married, really!" Seeing everyone leaning in to listen, he lowered his voice a bit: "Once you get married, you're busy day and night, it's over! Look at my waist, it's stiff, not a bit of flexibility! Better not run too hard, one clench of the teeth and I cough, my chest feels like it's burning! No need to say more, those of us in this line of work have to be bachelors our whole damn lives! Even those damn little sparrows are in pairs, but they won't let us get married! And another thing, after getting married, one child a year, I have five now! All with open mouths waiting to eat! Rickshaw fees are high, grain is expensive, business is tough, what can we do! Better to be a bachelor for life, if you get horny, go to a brothel, get syphilis, accept your fate! One person, if you die, you die! This thing, once you get married, with old and young, several mouths, you can't even close your eyes when you die! Don't you think so?" He asked Xiangzi.

Xiangzi nodded, unable to speak.

Just then, a customer came, the short man first negotiated the price, but he gave way, calling to the tall man: "Big brother, you take it! This guy still has five children at home!"

The tall man laughed: "Alright, I'll make another trip! Strictly speaking, this isn't how it's done! Well, I'll bring back a few more flatbreads later! See you later, brothers!" Watching the tall man walk away, the short man muttered to himself: "He's been messing around his whole damn life, can't even touch a wife! In those damn rich households, one man has four or five women!"

"Don't even mention them," another young man interjected. "You see, doing this kind of business, you really have to be careful, the tall man wasn't wrong. Just put it this way, what's the point of getting married? Can you just put it on display like a toy? No! Alright, this is a brothel! Eating steamed corn bread all day, attacked by both hunger and cold, even the strongest young man will collapse!" Hearing this, Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw, casually saying: "Let's go south, no business here."

"See you!" the two young men said in unison.

Xiangzi seemed not to have heard. He walked, kicking his legs, his hip joints still a bit sore! He had wanted to put away the rickshaw and stop pulling, but he simply didn't have the courage to go home. The one at home wasn't a wife, but a blood-sucking demon!

The day was slowly getting longer, and he had wandered around two or three more times, it was only around five o'clock. He returned the rickshaw and lingered in the teahouse for a while. After drinking two pots of tea, he felt hungry and decided to eat outside before going home. After eating twelve taels of meat pies and a bowl of red bean millet porridge, he slowly walked home, burping loudly. He knew there was a storm waiting for him at home, but he was very calm; he had made up his mind: not to argue with her, not to make a fuss with her, just fall asleep, and go out to pull a rickshaw as usual tomorrow, she could do whatever she wanted!

As soon as he entered the house, Huniu was sitting in the outer room, she glanced at him, her face so dark it looked like water would drip from it. Xiangzi intended to smooth things over, pulling a long face and greeting her. But he wasn't used to doing such things, he walked into the inner room with his head down. She didn't make a sound, the small room was as quiet as an ancient cave in a deep mountain. The coughing, talking, and crying of children from the neighbors in the courtyard were heard very clearly, yet also seemed very distant, just like hearing distant sounds on a mountain.

Neither of them wanted to speak first, they lay down one after another with their mouths shut, like a pair of silent old turtles. After waking up from a nap, Huniu spoke, her voice carrying a hint of annoyance and amusement: "Where did you go? You were out all day!"

"Pulled a rickshaw!" he said, half-asleep, half-awake, as if something was stuck in his throat. "Oh! You can't stand not sweating, your heart is itching, you cheap bone! I fried dishes for you, but you didn't come back to eat, you went wandering around the world to enjoy yourself? Don't provoke me, my father was a bachelor, I can do anything! If you dare to go out again tomorrow, I'll hang myself for you to see, if I say it, I'll do it!" "I can't be idle!"

"Why don't you go find the old man?"

"No!"

"How arrogant!"

Xiangzi was truly furious, he couldn't not speak his mind, he couldn't bear it anymore: "Pull a rickshaw, buy my own rickshaw, whoever stops me, I'll leave and never come back!" "Hmm—" she hummed, a long and winding sound in her nose. In this sound, she expressed her pride and contempt for Xiangzi, but her heart also took a turn. She knew Xiangzi was a—though very honest—tough guy. A tough guy's words were never said lightly. It was hard to catch him, she couldn't let go easily. He was the ideal man: honest, diligent, strong; considering her appearance and age, it was truly difficult to find such a treasure again. Being both firm and gentle was skill, she had to rein him in: "I know you're strong-willed, but you also have to know that I truly care for you. If you don't want to go find the old man, then do this: I'll go. After all, I'm his daughter, losing face is nothing."

"Even if the old man wants us, I still have to pull a rickshaw!" Xiangzi spoke his mind completely.

Huniu was silent for a long time. She hadn't expected Xiangzi to be so clever. Although his words were simple, they clearly showed that he would no longer fall for her tricks, he wasn't a stupid donkey. Therefore, she found him even more interesting, she had to put in some effort to control this adult, or big thing, who would kick up a fuss when provoked. She couldn't push him too hard, finding such a big thing wasn't easy. She had to alternate between loosening and tightening her grip, so he would never escape her grasp. "Alright, if you want to pull a rickshaw, I can't do anything. You have to swear not to pull chartered rickshaws, and you must come back every day; you see, if I don't see you for a day, my heart gets anxious! Promise me, you'll come back early every night!"

Xiangzi remembered what the tall man had said during the day! He stared into the darkness, seeing a group of rickshaw pullers, small vendors, and laborers, their backs unable to straighten, dragging their legs. He would be like that in the future. But he couldn't bother her anymore, as long as he could go pull a rickshaw, he considered it a victory. "I'll always pull individual fares!"He agreed.

Although she said so, she wasn't very enthusiastic about going to see Fourth Master Liu. Fathers and daughters naturally bickered often in daily life, but the situation was different now. It wasn't something that could be resolved with a few words and then forgotten, because she was no longer considered part of the Liu family. A married woman always becomes somewhat estranged from her parents. She didn't dare to go back directly to the family home. What if the old man really turned hostile and disowned her? She could make a fuss, but if he stubbornly held onto his property, she would be helpless. Even if someone mediated, when things became desperate, they could only advise her to return to her own home.

Xiangzi went out to pull his rickshaw as usual. She walked back and forth in the room alone, several times wanting to get dressed and go see her father, but her mind was willing while her hands were lazy. She was in a dilemma. For her own comfort and happiness, she had to go back; for her own dignity, it was better not to go. If the old man had calmed down, she only needed to take Xiangzi to the Renhe factory, and he would naturally have work, no longer needing to pull a rickshaw, and she could steadily take over her father's business. A light flashed in her mind. What if the old man remained stubborn? She would lose face, no, not just lose face, but she would have to accept being a rickshaw puller's wife; she, hmph! would be no different from the women in the courtyard. Her heart suddenly turned dark. She almost regretted marrying Xiangzi, no matter how strong-willed he was, if her father didn't approve, he would be a rickshaw puller his whole life. Thinking of this, she even wanted to go back to her parents' home alone, break up with Xiangzi, and not lose everything for him. Then, she thought about the joy of being with Xiangzi, which was beyond words. She sat on the kang, dazed and lost, reminiscing about the happiness after marriage; her whole body was like a large red flower, fragrant and warm, blooming in the sun. No, she couldn't bear to leave Xiangzi. Let him pull a rickshaw, let him beg for food, she would always stay with him. Look, look at those women in the courtyard, if they could endure it, so could she. It was over, she decided not to go to the Liu family.

Xiangzi, ever since leaving the Renhe factory, refused to go down Xi'anmen Street again. These past two days, when pulling his rickshaw, he always headed east as soon as he left, to avoid seeing the Renhe factory's rickshaws everywhere in the west, which would be awkward. This day, however, after returning the rickshaw, he deliberately passed by the factory gate, not for anything else, but just to take a look. Huniu's words were still in his mind, as if he wanted to test if he had the courage to return to the factory, if Huniu could talk to the old man; before returning to the factory, he would first try if he dared to walk down this street. Pulling his hat down, he stealthily walked along the factory side from a distance, fearing being seen by acquaintances. From afar, he saw the light of the rickshaw gate, and for some reason, he felt extremely sad. He remembered his initial arrival there, Huniu's seduction, and the night of the birthday celebration. These were all very clear, like pictures floating before his eyes. Between these pictures, there were also others, clear and brief, interspersed among them: Xishan, camels, Cao's residence, detectives... all distinct, terrifying, forming a continuous scene. These pictures were so clear that he felt somewhat bewildered, almost as if he were truly looking at a few paintings and had forgotten that he was also in them. When he thought about his relationship with them, his heart became chaotic, they suddenly spun up, down, left, and right, disorganized and confused, and he couldn't figure out why he should endure these torments and grievances. The time these scenes occupied seemed very long, yet also very short, and he couldn't tell how old he was. He only felt that, compared to when he first arrived at the Renhe factory, he had aged a great deal. At that time, his heart was full of hope; now, his belly was full of worries. He didn't understand why, but these pictures would never deceive him.

The Renhe factory was right in front of him. He stopped on the other side of the street, staring blankly at the extremely bright electric light. As he watched, his heart suddenly stirred. The four golden characters under the light—Renhe Rickshaw Factory—had changed! He was illiterate, but he remembered what the first character looked like: like two sticks joined together, neither a cross nor a triangle, such a simple yet strange character. Finding the character by sound, it was probably "人" (rén, person). This "人" had changed its appearance, becoming "仁" (rén, benevolence)—a character even stranger than "人". He couldn't figure out why. Looking at the east and west rooms—the two rooms he could never forget—neither had lights on.

He stood there until he himself grew impatient, then he lowered his head and walked home. As he walked, he wondered if the Renhe factory had closed down? He would have to inquire slowly, and it wasn't convenient to tell his wife yet. When he got home, Huniu was in the room cracking melon seeds to relieve boredom. "So late again!" There was no kindness in her expression. "Let me tell you, I can't take this anymore! You're out all day, and I don't even dare to move from my nest, with a whole courtyard of poor people, afraid of losing things. I don't even have anyone to talk to all day long, this won't do, I'm not a wooden dummy. You figure something out, this isn't working!" Xiangzi didn't say a word.

"Why don't you speak! Are you deliberately trying to provoke me? Do you have a mouth or not? Do you have a mouth or not?" Her words became faster and sharper, like a string of small firecrackers going off. Xiangzi still had nothing to say.

"This is how it'll be then," she was truly anxious, but also somewhat helpless about him, her face neither crying nor laughing, just very agitated and unable to fully vent. "Let's buy two rickshaws and rent them out. You can stay home and collect the rental fees, how about that? How about that?" "Two rickshaws bring in three mao a day, not enough to eat! Rent out one, and I'll pull one myself, that'll do!" Xiangzi spoke very slowly, but very naturally; hearing about buying a rickshaw, he forgot everything else.

"Isn't that the same? You still won't be home!"

"This way works too," Xiangzi's ideas seemed to come with the rickshaw problem, "Rent one out, get a full day's fee. For the other one, I'll pull it for half a day, then rent it out for the other half. If I pull during the day, I'll go out early and come back at three o'clock; if I pull at night, I'll go out at three and come back at night. That's great!" She nodded. "Let me think about it. If there's no better idea, we'll do this."

Xiangzi was very happy. If this idea could be realized, he would be pulling his own rickshaw again. Although his wife bought it for him, he could slowly save money and buy another rickshaw himself. Only at this moment did he realize that Huniu also had some good qualities. He actually smiled at her, a naive, heartfelt smile, as if all the previous hardships were wiped away, and he was smilingly entering a new world, as easy and pleasant as changing clothes!

[1]

 To be defeated, to lose. To be frustrated.

Learning resource sharing seventeen

Xiangzi slowly inquired and understood the situation at the Renhe factory: Fourth Master Liu sold off some rickshaws, and the rest were all transferred to a well-known rickshaw owner in the West City. Xiangzi could guess that the old man was getting on in years, and without his daughter's help, he couldn't manage the business, so he simply closed it down and took the money to enjoy himself. Where did he go? Xiangzi, however, couldn't find out.

Regarding this news, he couldn't say whether he should be happy or unhappy. From the perspective of his own ambition and pride, since Fourth Master Liu had decided to abandon his daughter, Huniu's plan had completely fallen through; he could honestly pull a rickshaw to earn a living, without relying on anyone. From the perspective of Fourth Master Liu's property, it was indeed a bit of a pity; who knew how the old man Liu had squandered the money, and he and Huniu hadn't gotten a single penny.

However, since things had come to this, he didn't think much about it, let alone be moved. He thought that his strength was his own anyway, and if he was willing to work hard to earn money, eating would not be a problem. He told Huniu simply, without any emotion. But she was moved. Hearing this, she immediately saw her future clearly—it was over! Everything was over! She would just have to be a rickshaw puller's wife for the rest of her life! She would never escape this large courtyard! She had thought her father would marry another wife, but never expected him to just walk away like this. If the old man really married a young wife, Huniu would fight for the property, and perhaps even conspire with the stepmother to get some benefits... there were plenty of ideas, as long as the old man kept the rickshaw factory open. She never expected the old man to be so resolute, so vicious, turning all his property into cash and secretly hiding it! When she had fallen out with him before, she thought it was just a tactic, and they would soon reconcile. She knew the Renhe factory couldn't do without her; who would have thought the old man would abandon the rickshaw factory?!

Spring had already arrived, and the bud scales on the tree branches were showing a plump red. But in this large courtyard, spring did not first appear on the branches; there were no flowering plants here. Here, the spring breeze first blew some small pockmarks onto the melting ice in the courtyard, blew out some fishy, foul odors from the dirty soil, and blew chicken feathers, scraps, and shredded paper into the corners, forming small whirlwinds. The people in the courtyard suffered all year round. The old people now dared to come out and bask in the warmth; the young girls now dared to wipe away a little of the coal dust from their noses, revealing some reddish-yellow skin; the women now dared to somewhat shamelessly shoo their children into the courtyard to play; the small children now dared to pull a torn piece of paper as a kite, running freely in the courtyard, without their small black hands cracking from the cold. But the porridge kitchen had stopped cooking, the relief distribution had stopped giving out rice, and the charitable had stopped giving out money; it was as if the suffering people were all handed over to the spring breeze and spring light! It was precisely when the spring wheat was just green like grass, and old grain was scarce, that the price of grain and rice rose as usual. The days grew longer, and even the old people couldn't lie down early to deceive their hungry stomachs with dreams. Spring had arrived in the human world, but in this large courtyard, it only brought more difficulties. The old lice—especially fierce—sometimes crawled out onto the cotton padding of old people or small children, to enjoy a bit of the spring light!

Huniu looked at the melting ice in the courtyard and the tattered clothes, smelled the complex and slightly warm odor, and listened to the old people's sighs and the children's cries, her heart chilled by half. In winter, people hid indoors, and dirt froze on the ice; now, people came out, and things revealed their true forms, even the walls made of broken bricks were shedding dirt, seemingly preparing to collapse when the rainy season arrived. The entire courtyard was a riot of colors, blooming with poor, evil flowers, many times uglier than in winter. Hmph, only at this moment did she feel that she would live here forever; her money would run out someday, and Xiangzi was just a rickshaw puller!

She told Xiangzi to watch the house, and she went to Nanyuan to find her aunt to inquire about the old man. Her aunt said that Fourth Master had indeed visited her, probably on the twelfth day of the first lunar month. First, he came to thank her, and second, to tell her that he planned to go to Tianjin or Shanghai to have some fun. He said: having lived a lifetime without leaving the capital, he couldn't be considered a hero after all, so while he still had some breath left, he wanted to go and see the world. Besides, he himself had no face to continue living in the city, because his daughter had disgraced him. Her aunt's report was only this much, and her judgment was even simpler: the old man might have really gone out, or he might just be saying so and hiding somewhere secluded; who knew!

Back home, she threw herself onto the kang and cried mournfully, crying for a long time without any pretense or cunning, her eyes swollen from crying.

After crying, she wiped her tears and said to Xiangzi, "Alright, you're arrogant! Everything has to go your way! I made a wrong bet. A married woman follows her husband, there's nothing more to say. Here's a hundred yuan, go buy a rickshaw and pull it!"

Here, she kept a close eye: originally, she wanted to buy two rickshaws, one for Xiangzi to pull himself, and one to rent out. Now she changed her mind, only buying one for Xiangzi to pull; the rest of the money would remain in her hands. With money in her hands, power would also be with her, and she was unwilling to give it all out; what if Xiangzi—after all the money was spent on rickshaws—changed his mind? This had to be guarded against! Moreover, the old man Liu's departure made her feel that nothing was reliable, no one could know for sure what tomorrow would bring, it was best to enjoy what she could, have some money in hand, eat whatever she liked, as she had always been accustomed to snacks. Living off what Xiangzi earned—he was a top-notch rickshaw puller—and supplementing her own spending with her own money, she would first enjoy the present. Money would run out someday, but people wouldn't live forever! Marrying a rickshaw puller—though reluctantly—was already a compromise for herself, and she couldn't keep asking him for money with empty pockets. This decision made her a little happier, although she knew the future would be difficult, at least for now, she wouldn't immediately hit rock bottom; it was like walking at sunset, the distance was already dim, but there was still some light in front of her, so she might as well walk a few more steps while there was light.

Xiangzi didn't argue with her. Buying one was fine, as long as it was his own rickshaw, he could still earn six or seven mao a day,He could chew on the grain. Not only did he not argue, but he also felt a little happy. All the hardships he had endured in the past were just to buy a cart. Now that he could buy another one, what more was there to say? Of course, with one cart providing for two people, there wouldn't be any money left; this cart would eventually get old, and there was no preparation to buy a new one, which was dangerous! But since buying a cart was so difficult, being able to buy one now should be satisfying, why think so far ahead?

Erqiangzi in the courtyard was about to sell his cart. Last summer, Erqiangzi sold his nineteen-year-old daughter, Xiaofuzi, to a soldier for two hundred yuan. After Xiaofuzi left, Erqiangzi was quite extravagant for a while, redeeming all his pawned items and even making several new clothes, so the whole family looked quite neat. Erqiang's wife was the shortest and ugliest woman in the whole courtyard, with a bulging forehead, large cheeks, little hair on her head, teeth always exposed, and a face covered in freckles, which was disgusting to look at. She also had red eyelids, crying for her daughter while putting on a new blue jacket. Erqiangzi had always had a violent temper, and after selling his daughter, he often drank a few cups of wine; after drinking, with tears in his eyes, he would be particularly prone to finding fault. Although Erqiang's wife wore a new jacket and ate her fill, her joy was no match for her suffering, and the number of times she was beaten almost doubled. Erqiangzi was over forty and planned to stop pulling carts. So he bought a pair of baskets and set up a sundry stall, selling fruits, peanuts, and cigarettes, with a complete range of goods. After two months of business, a rough stack of accounts showed not only a loss but a significant one. Accustomed to pulling carts, he couldn't handle business; pulling a cart was a matter of push and pull, either it worked or it didn't; doing small business required painstaking effort, which he couldn't do. Cart pullers knew how to sell things on credit, so he couldn't bring himself to refuse familiar people from owing money; once owed, it was not easy to get it back. Thus, good customers were not attracted, and those who traded with him were greedy for credit and didn't pay, so he couldn't help but lose money. Losing money made him sad; sadness led to more drinking. When drunk, he often argued with the police outside and took out his anger on his wife and children at home. Offending the police and beating his wife were all due to alcohol. When he sobered up, he felt extremely regretful and pained. Thinking again, this money was exchanged for his daughter, and to lose it like this, and also drink and hit people, he felt he was not human. At such times, he would sleep in for a day, leaving his troubles to his dreams.

He decided to give up the business and go back to pulling carts, not wanting to waste all that money for nothing. He bought a cart. When he was drunk, he was completely unreasonable. When he was sober, he valued his dignity greatly. Because he valued dignity, he often put on airs of poverty, doing everything with a certain style. Having bought a new cart and dressed neatly, he felt he was a high-class rickshaw puller, and he deserved to drink good tea and pull respectable passengers. He would stand at the cart stand, showing off his cart and his white trousers and jacket, chatting with everyone, always disdaining to solicit business. He would sometimes snap his new blue duster at the cart, sometimes stomp his new white-soled double-faced shoes, sometimes gaze at the tip of his nose, standing by the cart with a smile, waiting for others to praise his cart, and then he would start a conversation that never ended. He could "idle" like this for a day or two. But when he finally got a good passenger, his legs couldn't keep up with his cart and clothes, he couldn't run! This, again, made him extremely sad. When he was sad, he thought of his daughter, and could only go drinking. In this way, all his money was spent, leaving only the cart.

Around the beginning of winter, he got drunk again. As soon as he entered the house, his two sons—one thirteen, one eleven—tried to hide. This angered him, and he kicked each of them. Erqiang's wife said something, and he rushed to her, kicking her in the lower abdomen, and she lay on the ground for a long time without making a sound. The two children panicked, one picked up a coal shovel, the other grabbed a rolling pin, and fought their father desperately. The three fought in a tangle, and in the chaos, they stepped on Erqiang's wife a few more times. The neighbors came over and finally managed to pin Erqiangzi down on the kang, while the two children cried, hugging their mother. Erqiang's wife woke up but could never get out of bed again. On the third day of the twelfth lunar month, her breathing stopped, wearing the blue jacket made when her daughter was sold. Erqiang's wife's family refused to accept it and insisted on a lawsuit. After much persuasion from friends, her family finally relented, and Erqiangzi also promised to give her a proper funeral and fifteen yuan to her family. He pawned the cart for sixty yuan. The following year, he wanted to sell the cart, having no hope of redeeming it himself. When drunk, he even thought of selling a son, but no one would take him. He had also looked for Xiaofuzi's husband, but the man didn't acknowledge him as a father-in-law at all, so there was no need to say anything else.

Xiangzi knew the history of this cart and wasn't very keen on having it. There were plenty of carts, why buy this particular one, this inauspicious cart, this cart bought with a daughter and sold because of beating his wife to death! Huniu didn't see it that way; she wanted to buy it for just over eighty yuan, a bargain! The cart had only been pulled for half a year, even the color of the leather straps hadn't changed much, and it was genuinely made by Decheng, a famous factory in the West City. Wouldn't a 70% new cart cost fifty or sixty yuan? She couldn't resist this bargain. She also knew that soon after the New Year, money would be tight everywhere, Erqiangzi wouldn't be able to sell it for a high price, and he was in urgent need of money. She personally went to see the cart, personally negotiated the price with Erqiangzi, and paid the money; Xiangzi could only wait to pull the cart, saying nothing, and it was inconvenient to say anything, as the money wasn't his own. After buying the cart, he examined it carefully and indeed, it was sturdy. But he always felt a bit awkward. What displeased him most was the black painted body with white brass fittings. When Erqiangzi had this cart made, it was originally to create a striking black and white contrast; Xiangzi always felt it was a bit unlucky, like wearing mourning clothes. He really wanted to change the set of covers, to earthy yellow or moonlight white, or something that would reduce a bit of the plainness. But he didn't discuss it with Huniu, to avoid another lecture from her.

When this cart was pulled out, everyone paid special attention, some even called it "Little Widow." Xiangzi felt unhappy. He tried various ways not to think about it, but the cart was with him all day long, and he was always muttering, as if he didn't know when something might go wrong. Sometimes he suddenly thought of Erqiangzi and his fate, and it was as if he wasn't pulling a cart, but a coffin. On this cart, he always saw some ghostly figures, or so it seemed.

However, since he started pulling this cart, nothing had gone wrong, although he was constantly uneasy in his heart. The weather was getting warmer and warmer, and after shedding his padded clothes, he hardly needed lined clothes, and could wear single trousers and a single jacket; Beiping didn't have much spring. The days were so long that they almost made people impatient, everyone felt tired. Xiangzi went out early in the morning, and by four or five o'clock, he already felt he had exerted enough effort. But the sun was still high. He didn't want to run anymore, but he also didn't want to put the cart away, yawning long and lazily, undecided.

The days were so long, and if Xiangzi felt tired and bored, Huniu was even lonelier at home. In winter, she could warm herself by the stove, listening to the wind outside, and although she was depressed, she always had a bit of self-consolation, thinking "it's good not to go out." Now, the stove was moved under the eaves, and there was simply nothing to do in the house. The courtyard was so dirty and smelly, there wasn't even a blade of grass. Going out to the street, she didn't trust the neighbors, so even if she went to buy something, she had to go straight there and back, not daring to wander around for long. She was like a bee trapped in the house, watching the sunshine outside in vain but unable to fly out. She couldn't talk to the women in the courtyard. They talked about household gossip, while she was used to being wild and unrestrained, not liking to talk or listen to such things. Their grievances came from the hardships of life, and every small matter could bring tears; her grievances were dissatisfactions with life, she had no tears to shed, but wanted to scold someone to vent her frustration. She and they couldn't understand each other, so it was best for each to do their own thing, without much conversation.

It wasn't until mid-April that she found a companion. Erqiangzi's daughter, Xiaofuzi, returned. Xiaofuzi's "man" was an officer. He would set up a very simple home wherever he went, spending one or two hundred yuan to get a young girl, and then buying a large bed board and two chairs, and he could live happily for a few days. When the army was transferred elsewhere, he would leave, abandoning the girl and the bed board where they were. Spending one or two hundred yuan like this for a year or a year and a half was not a loss, considering that if he hired a servant for small tasks like sewing, washing clothes, cooking, etc., wouldn't it cost ten or eight yuan a month, including food and wages? So, marrying a girl like this, she was both a servant and could sleep with him, and was guaranteed to be clean and disease-free. If he was happy, he would buy her a floral cloth jacket, which cost a few yuan. If he was unhappy, he would make her squat at home naked, and she couldn't do anything about it. When he was transferred, he didn't regret the bed board and one or two chairs at all, because she would have to figure out how to pay the two months of back rent, and even selling the bed board might not be enough to cover the bill.

Xiaofuzi sold the bed board, paid the rent, and returned home wearing only a floral calico jacket and a pair of silver earrings.

After Erqiangzi sold the cart, besides paying back the pawn money and interest, he had about twenty yuan left. Sometimes he felt like a widower in middle age, very pitiful; since others didn't pity him, he would drink a cup of wine and eat something good, pitying and comforting himself. At such times, he seemed to have a grudge against money, spending it wildly. Sometimes he thought he should work harder pulling the cart, raising his two boys well, so they would have some hope in the future. When he thought of his sons, he would haphazardly buy a large pile of food for them to eat. Watching them gobble down the food, he would have tears in his eyes and mutter to himself, "Children without a mother! Poor children! Dad works hard for the children! I don't feel wronged, whether I eat my fill or not doesn't matter, the children must eat enough first! Eat! Just don't forget me when you grow up!" At such times, he also spent a lot of money. Slowly, the twenty yuan was all gone.

Without money, and then when he drank and lost his temper, he wouldn't care what the children ate for a day or two. The children had no choice but to figure out how to get a few coppers to buy something to eat. They would help out at weddings and funerals, or follow dirt carts to pick up broken copper and waste paper. Sometimes they could buy a few sesame cakes, sometimes they could only buy a pound of wheat bran sweet potatoes, swallowing them skin and all. Sometimes the two of them only had one large copper coin, so they had to buy peanuts or iron broad beans, which couldn't stave off hunger but could be chewed for a longer time.

Xiaofuzi returned, and they saw their loved one, each hugging one of her legs, speechless, only smiling at her through tears. Their mother was gone, their sister was their mother!

Erqiangzi showed no particular reaction to his daughter's return. Her return simply meant another mouth to feed. However, seeing his two sons so happy, he couldn't help but admit that there should be a woman in the house to cook and wash clothes for everyone. He didn't feel comfortable saying anything, just let things be.

Xiaofuzi was not bad-looking. Although she was originally very thin and small, she had gained some weight and grown taller since she had been with the officer. She had a round face, well-proportioned eyebrows and eyes, nothing particularly outstanding, but she was solid and not unattractive. Her upper lip was very short, so whether she was angry or smiling, her lips would part first, revealing very white and neat teeth. The officer particularly loved these teeth of hers. With these teeth exposed, she appeared somewhat naive and indecisive, and at the same time, a little charmingly innocent. This expression made her—like all poor but not unattractive girls—like flowers and plants; as long as there was a slight fragrance or color, she would be picked and sold in the market.

Huniu, who usually didn't bother with the people in the courtyard, regarded Xiaofuzi as a friend. First, Xiaofuzi was somewhat good-looking; second, she still had a floral calico gown; third, Huniu thought that since she had been married to an officer, she must have seen the world, so she was willing to associate with her. Women don't easily make friends, but once they do, it's very fast; within a few days, they had become close friends. Huniu loved snacks, and whenever she got some melon seeds or similar things, she would always call Xiaofuzi over, chatting and laughing while eating. During their conversations, Xiaofuzi would foolishly reveal her white teeth and tell Huniu many things she had never heard before. With the officer, she hadn't enjoyed much luxury, but when the officer was happy, he would take her to restaurants and plays, so she had many stories to tell, which made Huniu envious. She also had many unspeakable things: for her, these were violations; for Huniu, these were enjoyments. Huniu begged her to tell, but she was embarrassed to speak, yet also embarrassed to refuse. She had seen erotic pictures, which Huniu had never seen. Huniu loved to hear such stories a second time after hearing them once. She regarded Xiaofuzi as the most lovable, most enviable, and even most jealous person. After hearing those stories, and then looking at her own appearance, age, and husband, she felt that her life was too wronged. She had never had youth, and there was no hope for the future, and now,Xiangzi was again like a solid brick-like object! The more dissatisfied she was with Xiangzi, the more she loved Xiaofuzi. Although Xiaofuzi was so poor and pitiful, in her eyes, she was someone who had enjoyed blessings and seen the world, and even if she died immediately, it wouldn't be unfair. In her view, Xiaofuzi represented all the enjoyment a woman should have.

Huniu seemed not to notice Xiaofuzi's hardships. Xiaofuzi brought nothing back, but she had to—no matter how weak her father was—take care of her two younger brothers. Where could she get money to prepare food for them?

Erqiangzi, drunk, had an idea: "If you truly care for your brothers, you'll find a way to earn money to support them! Don't rely on me. I work like an animal all day for others; I need to eat first. Can I run on an empty stomach? If I fall and die, would you find that amusing? You're idle anyway, there's a ready-made option, what are you waiting for if not to sell it?"

Looking at her drunken father, at herself, and at her two brothers, hungry like mice, Xiaofuzi was left with only tears. Tears couldn't move her father, and tears couldn't feed her brothers. She had to come up with something more substantial. To feed her brothers, she had to sell her own flesh. Holding her youngest brother, her tears fell on his hair. He said, "Sister, I'm hungry!" Sister! Sister was a piece of meat, to be given to her brothers to eat!

Huniu not only didn't comfort Xiaofuzi but was even willing to help her: Huniu was willing to put up some capital, help her dress up nicely, and then she could repay the money she earned. Huniu was willing to lend her a place because her own room was too dirty, and Huniu at least had Xiangzi, and two rooms, so everyone had space to move around. Since Xiangzi wouldn't be back during the day, Huniu was happy to help a friend, and she could observe more, understand more, about what she lacked and couldn't do herself. Every time Xiaofuzi used the room, Huniu set a condition: she had to give her two mao. Friends were friends, business was business. For Xiaofuzi's sake, she had to clean the room well, which required labor and more money. Didn't buying brooms and dustpans cost money? Two mao was definitely not much, and it was only because they were friends that such consideration was shown.

Xiaofuzi showed some teeth, her tears falling inward.

Xiangzi knew nothing, but he couldn't sleep well again. Huniu "helped" Xiaofuzi, and also wanted to find her lost youth in Xiangzi.

[1]

 "过话" means to converse, to chat.

[2]

 "人" here refers to a man. This term is limited to informal relationships between men and women.

Chapter Eighteen

By June, the large courtyard was almost silent during the day. Children would rush out early with broken baskets to pick up whatever they could find; by nine o'clock, the scorching sun would already be cracking their thin backs, so they had to bring back what they had collected and eat whatever food the adults could give them. Then, the older ones, if they could find the smallest bit of capital, would go buy and pick, gathering ice cores to sell. If they couldn't find this capital, they would go out of the city in groups to swim in the moat, and casually steal a few pieces of coal at the station, or catch dragonflies and cicadas to sell to the children of wealthy families. The younger ones, not daring to run far, would go to places with trees outside the gate to pick locust tree worms, dig for "King Kong" [1]

 and play. With all the children gone, and all the men gone, the women would be bare-backed indoors, no one willing to come out; not because they were afraid of looking bad, but because the ground in the courtyard was already scorching hot.

Not until the sun was about to set did the men and children return one after another. By then, there were shadows on the walls of the courtyard and some cool breeze, while the heat trapped in the rooms all day was like a furnace; everyone sat in the courtyard, waiting for the women to cook. At this moment, the courtyard was very lively, like a market without goods. Everyone had endured the heat all day, their eyes red, and in no good temper; hungry, they were all anxious and pale-faced. If a word was out of place, some would hit their children, some would hit their wives; even if they didn't fight, they would curse to their heart's content. This commotion continued until everyone had eaten. Some children lay down in the courtyard and fell asleep, others went to the street to play boisterously [2]

. After the adults had eaten, their tempers mellowed considerably, and those who liked to talk would gather in groups of three or five, recounting the day's hardships. Those who couldn't find food, having nowhere to pawn or sell—even if they had something to pawn or sell—because it was already dark. The men, regardless of how hot it was indoors, would flop onto the kang, not saying a word, or perhaps cursing loudly. The women, with tears in their eyes, would go around to borrow money, perhaps facing many rejections before borrowing a tattered twenty-coin paper note. Clenching this precious note, she would go out to get some mixed flour and cook a pot of porridge for everyone to eat.

Huniu and Xiaofuzi were not part of this daily routine. Huniu was pregnant, and this time it was real. Xiangzi would go out early in the morning, and she would usually not get up until eight or nine o'clock; the traditional misconception that pregnancy is not suitable for much exercise, Huniu believed this, and also used it to show off her status: everyone else had to get up early to work, but she could leisurely lie in bed as long as she wanted. In the evening, she would take a small stool and sit outside the main gate where there was a breeze, not coming in until almost everyone in the courtyard had gone to sleep. She disdained idle chatter with everyone.

Xiaofuzi also got up late, but she had another reason. She was afraid of the men in the courtyard looking at her askance, so she waited until they had all left before daring to step out of her room. During the day, she would either visit Huniu or go for a walk, because her advertisement was herself. In the evening, to avoid the attention of the people in the courtyard, she would go out and wander the streets, estimating that everyone had gone to bed before she would sneak back in.

Among the men, Xiangzi and Erqiangzi were exceptions. Xiangzi was afraid to enter this large courtyard, and even more afraid to go into the rooms. The poor talk of the people in the courtyard made him anxious, and he preferred to find a quiet place to sit alone. Inside the room, he increasingly felt that Huniu was like a tigress. The small room was so hot and stuffy, and with that tigress added, he felt as if he couldn't breathe as soon as he entered. A few days ago, he had no choice but to come back early, to avoid Huniu's nagging and quarrels. Recently, with Xiaofuzi as a companion, she didn't control him much, so he came back later.

As for Erqiangzi, he hardly came home recently. He knew about his daughter's business and had no face to enter that street gate. But he couldn't stop her; he knew he had no power to support his children. He could only stop coming back, to avoid seeing and being bothered. Sometimes he hated his daughter, if Xiaofuzi had been a boy, he guaranteed she wouldn't have to suffer such disgrace; since she was a girl, why was she born to him! Sometimes he pitied his daughter, his daughter was selling herself to support her two younger brothers! Whether he hated or pitied, he had no choice. When he drank alcohol and had no money, he no longer hated or pitied. He would come back and ask her for money. At such times, he saw his daughter as a money-making thing, and as a father, it was right and proper for him to ask her for money. At this time, he also thought of his dignity: didn't everyone look down on Xiaofuzi? Her father didn't spare her either, he forced her to give him money, and cursed and grumbled, seemingly for everyone to hear—Erqiangzi was not wrong, Xiaofuzi was naturally shameless.

He argued, Xiaofuzi didn't even breathe heavily. It was Huniu, half scolding and half persuading, who dealt with him, of course, he had to take some money with him. This money only allowed him to go drinking again, because if he saw it sober, he would jump into the river or hang himself.

On June 15th, the heat was maddening. As soon as the sun came out, the ground was already like fire. Some hazy, cloud-like, fog-like gray air floated low in the sky, making people feel suffocated. There was no wind at all. Xiangzi looked at the reddish-gray sky in the courtyard and planned to pull a rickshaw late—going out after four in the afternoon; if he couldn't earn money, he could pull until dawn: nighttime was always better than daytime.

Huniu urged him to go out, afraid he would be in the way at home, in case Xiaofuzi brought a client. "Do you think it's comfortable at home? By noon, even the walls in the room are scorching hot!"

He didn't say a word, drank a dipper of cold water, and walked out.

The willow trees on the street, like sick patients, had leaves covered in a layer of dust, curling on the branches; the branches were too lazy to move, drooping listlessly. There wasn't a single drop of water on the road, which was dry and emitted a white glare. Dust flew high on the sidewalk, connecting with the gray air in the sky, forming a poisonous gray sandstorm that scorched the faces of pedestrians. Everywhere was dry, everywhere was hot to the touch, everywhere was stifling; the entire old city was like a burnt brick kiln, making it hard to breathe. Dogs lay on the ground, their red tongues hanging out, mules and horses' nostrils were unusually wide, vendors dared not shout, the asphalt road melted; even the brass plaques in front of shops seemed to be melting from the sun. The street was unusually quiet, with only the monotonous clanging from the coppersmiths and ironsmiths, which made people irritable. Rickshaw pullers, knowing that if they didn't work, they wouldn't eat, were too lazy to solicit business: some parked their rickshaws in a shady spot, propped up the canopy, and dozed off on the seat; some went into small teahouses to drink tea; some didn't even pull out their rickshaws, but came to the street to see if there was any possibility of getting a fare. Those who did have business, even the most handsome young men, were willing to lose face and dared not run, only walking slowly with their heads down. Every well platform became their savior; no matter how few steps they had just pulled, they would rush to the well as soon as they saw it; if they couldn't get freshly drawn water, they would drink a large gulp from the trough with the donkeys and horses. Others, due to heatstroke or sunstroke, would walk and then suddenly collapse to the ground, never to rise again.

Even Xiangzi felt a bit timid! After pulling the empty rickshaw for a few steps, he felt the heat enveloping him from face to foot, even his hands were sweating. But when he saw a passenger, he still wanted to pull, thinking that perhaps running would create some breeze. He took a fare, pulled the rickshaw, and only then did he realize that the severity of the weather had reached a point where it was impossible for anyone to work. As soon as he ran, he became breathless, and his lips were parched; even though he knew he wasn't thirsty, he wanted to drink water whenever he saw it. If he didn't run, the scorching sun would crack his hands and back. He finally reached the destination with great difficulty, his trousers and jacket completely soaked and clinging to him. He picked up a palm-leaf fan to fan himself, but the wind was hot. He had already drunk several gulps of cold water, but he went to the teahouse again. After drinking two pots of hot tea, his mind calmed down a bit. The tea went in through his mouth, and sweat immediately came out from his body, as if his body was hollow and could no longer store any moisture. He dared not move again.

After sitting for a long time, he felt annoyed. He dared not go out, and had nothing to do, feeling as if the weather was deliberately against him. No, he couldn't give in. He had been pulling rickshaws for more than a day, and this wasn't his first summer; he couldn't just "waste" a day like this. He wanted to go out, but his legs were truly lazy, his body felt very soft, as if he hadn't had a satisfying bath; although he had sweated a lot, he still didn't feel refreshed. After sitting for a while longer, he couldn't sit still anymore. Anyway, sitting also made him sweat, so he might as well go out and try.

As soon as he went out, he realized his mistake. The layer of gray air in the sky had dispersed, and it wasn't as stifling, but the sunlight was much more intense: no one dared to look up to see where the sun was, only feeling that everything was dazzling, the sky, rooftops, walls, and ground were all bright white, with a hint of red in the white; from top to bottom, the whole scene was like an enormous burning mirror, every ray of light like the focal point of a burning mirror, scorching things to the point of ignition. In this white light, every color was glaring, every sound was harsh, every smell was mixed with the fishy stench evaporating from the ground. The street seemed to be empty of people, the road seemed to have suddenly widened a lot, vast and without a trace of coolness, a frightening dazzling white. Xiangzi didn't know what to do, he lowered his head, pulled the rickshaw, moving forward extremely slowly, without a plan, without a purpose, feeling dizzy and muddled, covered in a layer of sticky sweat, emitting a sour smell. After walking for a while, the soles of his feet and his socks stuck together, as if he were stepping on wet mud, which was very uncomfortable. He originally didn't want to drink water anymore, but when he saw a well, he couldn't help but go over and drink a large gulp again, not to quench his thirst, but seemingly just to enjoy the coolness of the well water, from his mouth to his stomach, it suddenly cooled down, his pores suddenly contracted, he shivered, feeling very comfortable. After drinking, he kept burping, the water threatening to come back up!

Walking for a while, sitting for a while, he was always too lazy to solicit business. It wasn't until noon that he felt no hunger. He thought of eating something as usual, but the sight of food made him nauseous. His stomach was almost filled with various kinds of water, and sometimes it would make a soft gurgling sound, like a mule or horse after drinking water, making a hollow gurgling sound in its belly.

Comparing winter and summer, Xiangzi always thought winter was more terrifying. He never expected summer to be so unbearable. He had spent more than one summer in the city, but he didn't remember it ever being this hot. Was the weather hotter than previous years, or was his own body weak? Thinking this, he suddenly wasn't so muddled, and his heart seemed to cool down a bit. His own body, yes, his own body was failing! He was scared, but there was nothing he could do. He couldn't drive Huniu away, he was going to become Erqiangzi, become that tall man he met before, become Little Ma'er's grandfather. Xiangzi was finished!

It was one o'clock in the afternoon when he got another fare. This was the hottest time of the day,It was the hottest day of the summer, but he decided to make the trip. He didn't care how hot it was under the sun: if he finished the trip and felt fine, it would prove his body wasn't broken; if he couldn't complete the job, what more was there to say? He might as well drop dead on the scorching ground!

After just a few steps, he felt a cool breeze, like a breath of cool air entering a very hot room through a crack in the door. He couldn't believe it; he looked at the willow branches by the roadside, and they indeed swayed slightly twice. The street suddenly filled with more people, shopkeepers rushed out, clutching palm-leaf fans to shield their heads, looking around: "A cool breeze! A cool breeze! The cool breeze is here!" Everyone almost jumped up and shouted. The willow trees by the roadside suddenly seemed like angels, conveying news from heaven: "The willow branches are moving! Oh, Lord, please give us more cool air!"

It was still hot, but his heart was much calmer. The cool breeze, even just a little, gave people a lot of hope. After a few gusts of cool air, the sunlight wasn't as strong, alternating between bright and slightly dim, as if a patch of flying sand was floating above. The wind suddenly picked up, and the willow branches, which had been still for half the day, seemed to have received some joyful news, swaying gracefully, their branches appearing to stretch longer. A gust of wind passed, the sky darkened, and all the dust flew into the air. Some dust settled, and ink-like dark clouds appeared on the northern horizon. Xiangzi no longer sweated; he glanced north, stopped his rickshaw, and put up the tarpaulin. He knew that summer rain came suddenly, without warning.

No sooner had he finished putting up the tarpaulin than another gust of wind came, and rolling black clouds had already covered half the sky. The heat from the ground mixed with the cool wind, carrying the pungent smell of dry earth, feeling both cool and hot; the southern half of the sky was bright and clear, while the northern half was dark with ink-like clouds, as if some great disaster was approaching, and everything was in a panic. Rickshaw pullers rushed to put up their tarpaulins, shopkeepers hurried to take down their awnings, vendors frantically packed up their stalls, and pedestrians quickened their pace. Another gust of wind. After the wind passed, the awnings, stalls, and pedestrians on the street seemed to have been swept away by the wind, all gone, leaving only the willow branches dancing wildly with the wind.

Before the clouds had fully covered the sky, the ground was already very dark, as if the extremely bright and hot clear afternoon had suddenly turned into night. The wind carried raindrops, like it was searching for something on the ground, crashing randomly from east to west. A red flash in the distance to the north, as if tearing open a piece of the black cloud, revealing a large patch of blood. The wind lessened, but it was sharp and strong, making people shiver. After such a gust of wind, everything seemed at a loss, even the willow trees waited with apprehension for something. Another flash, directly overhead, and bright white raindrops immediately followed, hitting hard and kicking up a lot of dust, with a faint smell of rain in the earth. Several large raindrops hit Xiangzi's back, and he shivered twice. The raindrops stopped, and black clouds evenly covered the sky. Another gust of wind, more violent than before, willow branches flew horizontally, dust spread in all directions, and rain poured down; wind, dust, and rain mixed together, forming a continuous mass, horizontally and vertically all gray and chilly, everything was enveloped within it, unable to distinguish what was tree, what was ground, what was cloud, all directions were chaotic, noisy, and blurry. The wind passed, leaving only vertical rain streaks, stretching across the sky and earth, falling, not visible as individual lines, but as a single sheet, a single downpour, countless arrows shot up from the ground, and thousands of waterfalls fell from the houses. In a few minutes, heaven and earth were indistinguishable, rivers in the sky fell, rivers on the ground flowed horizontally, becoming a dark, murky, sometimes bright white, watery world.

Xiangzi's clothes were already soaked through, with no dry spot on his entire body; even through his straw hat, his hair was completely wet. The water on the ground was above his ankles, making it very difficult to walk; the rain from above hammered his head and back, swept across his face, and enveloped his crotch. He couldn't lift his head, couldn't open his eyes, couldn't breathe, couldn't take a step. He felt like he was standing still in the water, not knowing where the road was, not knowing what was in front, behind, or to his sides, only feeling the bone-chilling water pouring over every part of his body. He knew nothing else, only a vague warmth in his heart and the sound of rain in his ears. He wanted to put down the rickshaw, but didn't know where. He wanted to run, but the water held his legs. So he half-dead, half-alive, pulled forward step by step with his head down. The passenger seemed to have died in the rickshaw, silently letting the rickshaw puller struggle for his life in the water.

The rain lessened a bit, and Xiangzi slightly straightened his back, letting out a breath: "Sir, let's take shelter and then go!"

"Hurry up! What do you mean by leaving me here?" the passenger shouted, stamping his feet.

Xiangzi really wanted to just put down the rickshaw and find a place to shelter. But looking at himself, water was already streaming down his body, and he knew that if he stopped, he would shiver into a ball. He gritted his teeth, waded through the water, and ran regardless of height or depth. Not long after he started running, the sky darkened for a moment, followed by a flash of light, and the rain again blurred his vision.

He arrived, but the passenger didn't give him even one extra copper coin. Xiangzi said nothing, he was already beyond caring for his life.

The rain stopped for a while, then started again, much lighter than before. Xiangzi ran all the way home. Hugging the fire, he roasted himself for a while, shivering like a leaf in the wind and rain. Huniu made him a bowl of ginger-sugar water, and he drank it all like an idiot. After drinking, he burrowed into the quilt, knowing nothing else, half-asleep, half-awake, with the swishing sound of rain in his ears.

Around four o'clock, the black clouds began to show signs of fatigue, weakly and feebly flashing with not-so-red lightning. A moment later, the western clouds split open, the black cloud peaks edged with golden yellow, and some white mist rushed beneath the clouds; the lightning all moved to the south, dragging a few not-so-loud claps of thunder. After waiting a while longer, sunlight appeared through a gap in the western clouds, illuminating the rain-soaked leaves into a golden green. In the eastern sky hung a seven-colored rainbow, its two ends inserted into the black clouds, its arch supporting a patch of clear blue sky. The rainbow soon dissipated, and there was no longer a single black cloud in the sky, the washed blue sky and everything else, like a new, cool, beautiful world born from the darkness. Even several colorful dragonflies came to the puddles in the large courtyard.

However, apart from the children chasing those dragonflies barefoot, the people in the courtyard didn't bother to appreciate the clear sky after the rain. A section of the back eaves wall of Xiaofuzi's room had collapsed, and the three sisters were busy taking up the kang mats to block the hole. Several sections of the courtyard wall had collapsed, and everyone had no time to deal with them, only focusing on tidying up their own rooms: some steps were too low, and water had already flowed into the rooms, so everyone, with many hands, used dustpans and broken bowls to scoop out the water. Some had collapsed gable walls and tried to fill them. Some roofs leaked like watering cans, soaking everything, so they hurried to move things out, placing them by the stove to dry, or on the windowsill to sun. While it was raining, everyone hid in those rooms that could collapse at any moment and bury them alive, entrusting their lives to heaven; after the rain, they calculated and tidied up their losses; although the heavy rain had passed, a catty of grain might only drop by half a copper coin, but their losses could not be compensated by this. They paid rent, but no one ever came to repair the houses; only when they collapsed beyond habitation would one or two masons come, using plain mud and broken bricks to loosely patch them up—preparing for another collapse. If the rent wasn't paid, the whole family would be evicted, and their belongings confiscated. The houses were dilapidated, they could kill people, and no one cared. Their meager money could only rent such rooms; broken, dangerous, they deserved it!

The biggest loss was falling ill from the rain. Both children and adults spent all day on the streets looking for work, and summer downpours could drench them at any time. They all earned money through hard labor, always covered in sweat, and the northern downpours were so sudden, so cold, sometimes mixed with hail the size of walnuts; the icy raindrops, hitting their open sweat pores, would at least make them lie on the kang, feverish for a day or two. If a child got sick, there was no money for medicine; a rain could make the corn and sorghum in the fields grow taller, but it could also kill many poor children in the city. If adults got sick, it was even worse; after the rain, poets would sing of dewdrops on lotus leaves and double rainbows; for poor families, if an adult got sick, the whole family would go hungry. A rain might also add a few more prostitutes or petty thieves, and send more people to prison; if adults got sick, it was better for their children to become thieves or prostitutes than to starve! Rain falls on the rich and the poor; it falls on the righteous and the unrighteous. In fact, rain is not fair, because it falls on a world without fairness.

Xiangzi fell ill. He was not the only patient in the large courtyard.

[1]

Jingang, refers to the pupa of the locust borer.

[2]

Sahan, originally refers to animals running and playing joyfully, also used to describe children doing such actions.

Nineteen

Xiangzi slept in a daze for two days and two nights, and Huniu became frantic. At the Niangniang Temple, she sought a divine remedy: a bit of incense ash, plus two or three herbs. After forcing it down him, he did open his eyes and look around, but after a while, he fell asleep again, mumbling something unintelligible. Only then did Huniu remember to call a doctor. After two injections and a dose of medicine, he regained consciousness, and as soon as he opened his eyes, he asked, "Is it raining?"

When the second dose of medicine was ready, he refused to take it. He felt both the pain of spending money and the hatred for his own uselessness, to be struck ill by a mere rain. He wouldn't drink that bitter concoction. To prove he didn't need medicine, he wanted to get dressed and go out immediately. But as soon as he sat up, his head felt as if a large stone was weighing it down, his neck went limp, golden stars appeared before his eyes, and he collapsed again. There was nothing more to say; he took the bowl and swallowed the medicine.

He lay there for ten days. The longer he lay, the more anxious he became, sometimes crying silently into his pillow. He knew he couldn't earn money, so all expenses would have to be covered by Huniu; once her money ran out, everything would depend on his rickshaw; with Huniu's love for spending and eating, he couldn't afford it, and she was pregnant too! The more he couldn't get up, the more he indulged in wild thoughts, and the more he thought, the more worried he became, making it harder for his illness to heal. As soon as he recovered a bit, he asked Huniu, "Where's the rickshaw?"

"Don't worry, Ding Si is renting it!"

"Ah!" He was worried about his rickshaw, fearing that Ding Si, or anyone else, might damage it. But since he couldn't get up, of course, he had to rent it out; could it just sit idle? He calculated in his mind: if he pulled it himself, he could earn five or six mao a day, come what may. Rent, coal, rice, firewood, lamp oil, tea, not to mention new clothes, would barely be enough for two people, and they'd still have to be very frugal, not like Huniu who was so carefree. Now, with only a little over one mao in rickshaw rent daily, he was losing four or five mao, not counting the medicine. What if his illness didn't get better? Yes, it wasn't Two-Strong's fault for drinking, nor those poor friends for their misbehavior; pulling a rickshaw was a dead end! No matter how hard you worked, how strong you were, you couldn't get married, get sick, or have any mishaps. Humph! He remembered his first rickshaw, the money he had saved, who did he offend or provoke? Not because of illness, nor for getting married, it was just lost so heartlessly and unreasonably! Neither good nor bad worked; on this path, there was only death, and it could come at any time, without him knowing. Thinking of this, his worry turned into despondency. What did it matter? If he couldn't get up, he'd just lie there; it was all the same anyway! He stopped thinking about anything, lying quietly. Soon, he couldn't bear it anymore; he wanted to get up immediately and go back to his arduous work; the road was dead, but people's hearts were alive, always hoping until they were in their coffins. But he couldn't stand up. He could only idly, pitifully, say a few words to Huniu:

"I said that rickshaw was unlucky, truly unlucky!"

"Take care of your illness! Always talking about the rickshaw, you rickshaw fanatic!"

He said nothing more. Right, he was a rickshaw fanatic! Ever since he started pulling a rickshaw, he believed the rickshaw was everything, how could it be... As soon as his illness eased a bit, he got out of bed. Looking in the mirror, he didn't recognize the person in it: his face was covered in stubble, his temples and cheeks were sunken, his eyes were two deep hollows, and there were many wrinkles on that scar! The room was very stuffy, and he didn't dare go into the courtyard, partly because his legs were as soft as if they had no bones, and partly because he was afraid of being seen. Not just in this courtyard, but at all the rickshaw stands in the east and west cities, who didn't know Xiangzi was a top-notch young man? Xiangzi couldn't be this kind of sick ghost! He refused to go out. Inside the room, he felt stifled. He wished he could instantly become strong and go out to pull his rickshaw. But illness was destructive; its coming and going were entirely up to itself.

After resting for about a month, he didn't care if he was fully recovered or not, and he started pulling his rickshaw. He wore his hat very low so that people wouldn't recognize him, allowing him to run at a slower pace. "Xiangzi" and "fast" were inseparable; he couldn't slowly drag himself along in a grand manner, letting people look down on him.

His body hadn't fully recovered, and he was eager to pull more fares to make up for the losses during his illness. After a few days, his illness returned. This time, dysentery was added. He was so anxious he slapped himself, but it was no use; his stomach seemed to be touching his waist, and he was still having diarrhea. When the dysentery finally stopped, his legs struggled even to squat down and stand up again, let alone run for a while.

He rested for another month! He knew Huniu's money was probably almost gone! By the fifteenth of the eighth lunar month, he decided to go out with his rickshaw. This time, if he got sick again, he swore, he would jump into the river!

During his first illness, Xiaofuzi often came to visit. Xiangzi's mouth was never as sharp as Huniu's, and his heart was so stifled, so sometimes he would talk a few words with Xiaofuzi. This provoked Huniu. When Xiangzi wasn't home, Xiaofuzi was a good friend; when Xiangzi was home, Xiaofuzi was, in Huniu's opinion, "coming to flirt! Shameless!" She then forced Xiaofuzi to pay back the money she owed her, "From now on, don't you dare come in again!"

Xiaofuzi lost her place to entertain guests, and her own room was so dilapidated—the kang mat blocked the back wall—she had no choice but to go to the "Transfer Company" to register. However, the "Transfer Company" didn't need her kind of "goods." They introduced "female students" and "daughters of prominent families," with high connections and large expenses; they didn't want ordinary people like her. She was at her wit's end. She thought of going to a brothel, but since she had no capital, she couldn't run her own business, so she would have to be indentured to a madam. But doing so would mean completely losing her freedom; who would look after her two younger brothers? Death was the simplest and easiest thing; living was already hell. She wasn't afraid of death, but she didn't want to die either, because she wanted to do something braver and greater than death. She wanted to see her two younger brothers earn money, and then she could die with peace of mind. She would die sooner or later, but she had to die to save two! After much thought, she had only one path: cheap prostitution. Those who would enter her small room certainly wouldn't pay a high price. Well, whoever came was fine, as long as they paid. This saved her the cost of clothes and makeup; those who sought her out didn't expect her to be dressed elegantly; they paid for pleasure. She was very young, which was already a bargain.

Huniu's body was becoming quite inconvenient; she was even afraid of slipping when going out to buy things. And Xiangzi was out all day, and Xiaofuzi wouldn't come over, so she felt as lonely as a dog tied up in the house. The lonelier she got, the more she hated, believing that Xiaofuzi's discounted services were deliberately meant to spite her. She couldn't stand this humiliation: sitting in the outer room, with the door open, she waited. When someone walked towards Xiaofuzi's room, she would loudly gossip, making them uncomfortable and making Xiaofuzi unable to bear it. Xiaofuzi's customers decreased, and Huniu was pleased.
