Xiaofuzi knew that if this continued, everyone in the courtyard would gradually side with Huniu and drive her out. She was just afraid, not daring to get angry; people in her situation knew to put facts before anger and tears. She brought her youngest brother over and knelt before Huniu. She said nothing, but her expression conveyed: if this kneeling wasn't enough, she herself wasn't afraid of death, but no one else should expect to live! The greatest sacrifice was enduring humiliation, and the greatest endurance of humiliation was preparing for resistance.

Huniu, however, was at a loss. No matter how she thought about it, it didn't feel right, but with such a big belly, she didn't dare to fight. Since she couldn't resort to force, she had to give herself an out: she was just teasing Xiaofuzi, who knew it would turn serious, Xiaofuzi was too stubborn. With this explanation, they became good friends again, and she continued to support Xiaofuzi in everything.

Since going out with his rickshaw in mid-autumn, Xiangzi had become extra cautious; two bouts of illness had taught him that he wasn't made of iron. His ambition to earn more money hadn't completely vanished, but repeated setbacks had made him realize how weak an individual's strength was; a strong man had to grit his teeth when the time came, but even gritting his teeth could make him spit blood! Although the dysentery had healed, his stomach still ached from time to time. Sometimes his legs and feet were just getting into their stride, and he wanted to try to pick up speed, but his stomach would twist like a rope, and he would slow down, or even suddenly stop, lowering his head, hunching his stomach, enduring for a while. Pulling a single passenger was manageable, but when pulling a group rickshaw, his sudden stop would baffle everyone, and he himself would feel extremely embarrassed. He was only in his twenties, already making such a fool of himself; what would he be like when he was thirty or forty? Thinking this, he broke out in a sweat!

For the sake of his health, he really wanted to go back to pulling chartered rickshaws. After all, it was a steady job with moments to catch his breath; he had to run fast, but the rest periods were also long, always more leisurely than pulling individual fares. But he also knew for sure that Huniu would absolutely not let him go; once married, he lost his freedom, and Huniu was particularly formidable. He resigned himself to his bad luck. For half a year, from autumn to winter, he just muddled through, half coping, half struggling, not daring to be careless, nor daring to be lazy, feeling stifled and depressed, working hard with his head down. With his head down, he no longer dared to be as reckless as before, not caring about anything. As for earning money, he still earned more than the average rickshaw puller. Unless his stomach was cramping with pain, he would never let a business opportunity go, pulling whenever he could; he never developed bad habits. He never learned to deliberately inflate prices, or switch passengers midway, or wait endlessly for good fares. This way, he suffered more hardship, but he consistently earned money every day. He didn't take shortcuts, so he also avoided danger.

However, the money came in too slowly and couldn't be saved. In one hand and out the other, a clean slate every day. He didn't even dare to think about saving money. He knew how to save, but Huniu knew how to spend. Huniu's "confinement month" was in early February of the new year. Since early winter, her pregnancy had become visible, and she liked to deliberately stick out her belly to show her importance. Looking at her own belly, she was too lazy to even get off the kang. All the cooking and meal preparation were entrusted to Xiaofuzi, and naturally, the leftover soup and scraps had to be given to Xiaofuzi for her brothers to eat. This cost a lot. Besides meals, she also had to eat snacks; the more her belly showed, the more she felt she needed to eat good things; she couldn't deprive herself. She not only bought various odds and ends at any time but also instructed Xiangzi to bring some back for her every day. Xiangzi earned as much as she spent; her demands fluctuated with his money. Xiangzi couldn't say anything. When he was sick, he spent her money, so it was a tit-for-tat, and of course, he had to spend for her too. If Xiangzi tightened his purse strings even a little, she would immediately get sick, "Pregnancy is being sick for over nine months, what do you know?" What she said was also true. By the time the New Year came, she had even more ideas. Unable to move herself, she sent Xiaofuzi on countless errands to buy things. She hated that she couldn't go out, yet she pampered herself and refused to go out, and not going out made her feel stifled, so she just bought more things to feel more comfortable looking at them. She kept saying she wasn't buying for herself but felt sorry for Xiangzi: "You've worked hard all year, aren't you going to eat a bite? Since you got sick, you haven't fully recovered; if you don't eat by the end of the year, you'll starve like a shriveled bedbug!" Xiangzi couldn't argue, nor would he argue; when the food was prepared, she would eat two or three large bowls. After eating, with no exercise, she would feel bloated, holding her stomach and insisting it was "fetal qi"!

After the New Year, she absolutely forbade Xiangzi from going out in the evening; she could give birth at any time, and she was afraid. It was then that she remembered her true age, though she still wouldn't say it outright, but she no longer told him, "I'm only 'a little' older than you." With her making such a fuss, Xiangzi was bewildered. The continuation of life was merely having children, and Xiangzi couldn't help but feel a little joy in his heart; even if he didn't need a child at all, that simple yet profound word "Dad" that would soon be bestowed upon him made even the most hard-hearted person close their eyes and ponder. No matter how he thought about it, this word always touched his heart. Xiangzi, clumsy and awkward, couldn't think of any good qualities or anything to be proud of in himself; but thinking of this wonderful word, he suddenly felt his own dignity, as if nothing else mattered, as long as he had a child, life wouldn't be empty. At the same time, he wanted to provide and serve Huniu to the best of his ability; she was no longer "one" person now; even if she was very annoying, she had a hundred percent credit in this matter. However, no matter how great her credit, her fussiness was truly unbearable. She had a new idea every moment, making a chaotic fuss as if seeing gods and ghosts, while Xiangzi had to go out to earn money and needed rest; even if money could be spent recklessly, he still needed a good night's sleep to go back to his arduous work tomorrow. She wouldn't let him go out at night, nor would she let him sleep well; he had no idea what to do, feeling dizzy and confused all day, not knowing what was best. Sometimes joyful, sometimes anxious, sometimes vexed, sometimes ashamed for being joyful, sometimes comforting himself for being anxious, sometimes joyful for being vexed; emotions circled in his heart, making the simplest person lose his bearings. Once, he even pulled a passenger past their destination, forgetting where they had hired him to go!

Around the Lantern Festival, Huniu decided to have Xiangzi go invite the midwife,She could no longer hold on. The midwife arrived, told her it wasn't time yet, and described some signs of impending labor. She endured for two days, then started to fuss again. The midwife was called back, but it still wasn't time. Crying and screaming, she threatened to kill herself, unable to bear the torment any longer. Xiangzi was helpless; to show his devotion, he could only agree to her request and temporarily stop pulling his rickshaw.

The commotion continued until the end of the month, and even Xiangzi could see that it was truly time; she no longer looked human. The midwife returned and subtly hinted to Xiangzi that it might be a difficult birth. Huniu's age, this being her first pregnancy, her lack of exercise, and the large size of the fetus due to her overeating fatty foods during pregnancy—these factors combined meant that a smooth delivery was unlikely. Moreover, she had never been examined by a doctor, so the baby's position hadn't been corrected. The midwife couldn't perform such an operation but could say, "I'm afraid it might be a transverse or breech birth!"

In this courtyard, the birth of a child and the death of a mother were habitually spoken of in the same breath. But Huniu faced more danger than others; other women remained active until the day of delivery and ate sparingly, so their babies weren't too large, making delivery easier. Their danger lay in postpartum complications, but Huniu was the opposite. Her advantages were her misfortunes.

Xiangzi, Xiaofuzi, and the midwife stayed with her for three days and three nights. She called upon all the gods and Buddhas, making countless vows, but to no avail. Finally, her voice hoarse, she could only whisper, "Mama! Mama!" The midwife was helpless, everyone was helpless. It was Huniu herself who suggested that Xiangzi go to Deshengmen outside the city to invite Granny Chen Er—who channeled a toad immortal. Granny Chen Er wouldn't come for less than five yuan. Huniu took out her last seven or eight yuan: "Good Xiangzi, hurry! Money doesn't matter! When I'm better, I'll live a good life with you! Go quickly!"

Granny Chen Er arrived with her "disciple"—a sallow-faced man of about forty—just as dusk was falling. She was about fifty, wearing a blue silk jacket, with red pomegranate flowers in her hair and a full set of gilded jewelry. Her eyes were fixed and staring. Upon entering, she first washed her hands, then lit incense. She kowtowed herself, then sat behind the incense table, staring blankly at the incense sticks. Suddenly, her whole body shook, she shivered violently, lowered her head, closed her eyes, and remained motionless for a long time. The house was so quiet you could hear a pin drop, and Huniu bit her lip, not daring to make a sound. Slowly, Granny Chen Er raised her head, nodded, and looked at everyone. The "disciple" tugged at Xiangzi, telling him to quickly kowtow. Xiangzi didn't know if he believed in gods or not, but he felt that kowtowing couldn't be wrong. Dazed, he didn't know how many times he kowtowed. Standing up, he looked at the pair of staring "divine" eyes and the brightly burning red incense sticks, smelling the incense smoke. In his heart, he vaguely hoped that this ritual would bring some good, and he stood there blankly, cold sweat on his palms.

The toad immortal spoke in an old, croaky voice, with a slight stutter: "No, no, no problem! Draw a催,催,催生符 (birth-hastening talisman)!"

The "disciple" quickly handed over a piece of yellow cotton paper. The immortal grabbed some ash from the incense sticks, then dipped her finger in saliva and drew on the paper.

After drawing the talisman, she stammered a few more words: the general meaning was that Huniu owed this child a debt in a previous life, and therefore had to suffer some torment. Xiangzi was dazed and didn't understand much, but he was a little scared.

Granny Chen Er let out a long yawn, closed her eyes and stared for a while, then opened them as if waking from a deep dream. The "disciple" quickly reported the immortal's words. She seemed very pleased: "The immortal is happy today, she likes to talk!" Then she instructed Xiangzi on how to make Huniu drink the divine talisman and gave her a pill to take with it.

Granny Chen Er eagerly waited to see the talisman's effect, so Xiangzi had to prepare some food for her. Xiangzi entrusted this task to Xiaofuzi. Xiaofuzi bought hot sesame paste flatbreads and braised pork hock; Granny Chen Er even complained that there was no wine.

Huniu took the talisman, Granny Chen Er and the "disciple" finished eating, but Huniu continued to writhe and fuss. After more than an hour, her eyes slowly rolled upwards. Granny Chen Er still had an idea; unhurriedly, she told Xiangzi to kneel and light a tall stick of incense. Xiangzi's faith in Granny Chen Er was already dwindling. But since he had already spent five yuan, he might as well try all her methods; since he couldn't beat her, he would follow her advice, just in case it worked!

Kneeling stiffly in front of the tall incense stick, he didn't know which god he was praying to, but he wanted to be devout. Watching the flickering incense, he pretended to see some shapes in the flame, and then he prayed. As the incense burned shorter and shorter, black lines appeared in the middle of the flame. He lowered his head, his hands on the ground, feeling drowsy and confused; he hadn't slept well for two or three days. His neck suddenly went limp, startling him. He looked again, and there was not much incense left. He didn't care if it was time to stand up or not; he propped himself up with his hands on the ground and slowly stood, his legs already a little numb.

Granny Chen Er and the "disciple" had already secretly slipped away.

Xiangzi didn't have time to hate her; he rushed to Huniu, knowing that things had reached a very difficult point. Huniu was only gasping for breath, unable to make a sound. The midwife told him to find a way to go to the hospital; her methods were exhausted.

Xiangzi's heart seemed to suddenly crack; he opened his mouth wide and cried. Xiaofuzi also shed tears, but being in a position to help, she was still a little clearer-headed. "Brother Xiang! Don't cry yet! I'll go to the hospital and ask?"

Regardless of whether Xiangzi heard her, she wiped her tears and ran out.

She was gone for about an hour. When she ran back, she was so out of breath she couldn't speak. Leaning on the table, she coughed dryly for a long time before saying: "It costs ten yuan for a doctor to come, just to look, not to deliver. Delivery is twenty yuan. If it's a difficult birth, you have to go to the hospital, and that will cost dozens of yuan. Brother Xiang! What do you think we should do?!" Xiangzi was helpless; he could only wait for the doomed to die!

Stupidity and cruelty are some phenomena here; the reasons for this stupidity and cruelty are, however, different.

Huniu passed away at midnight, taking a dead child with her.

[1]

"Back pull" means average.

[2]

"Head to head" means first-class.

Twenty

Xiangzi's rickshaw was sold!

Money flowed like water, his hands couldn't stop it; the dead had to be carried out, and even posting the funeral notice cost money.

Xiangzi looked like an idiot, watching everyone busy, he just kept pulling out money. His eyes were terribly red, with yellow-white eye boogers piled in the corners; his ears were deaf, and he stumbled around with everyone, not knowing what he was doing.

Following Huniu's coffin out of the city, he became a little clearer, but his mind couldn't yet think about anything. No one attended the funeral except Xiangzi and Xiaofuzi's two younger brothers, each holding a thin stack of paper money, scattering it along the way for the road-blocking ghosts.

Stumbling, Xiangzi watched the pallbearers bury the coffin; he didn't cry. His mind was like a raging fire, burning away his tears, making him unable to cry even if he wanted to. Staring blankly, he barely knew what was happening. It wasn't until the "leader" came to give instructions that he remembered to go home.

The room had been tidied by Xiaofuzi. Back home, he collapsed onto the kang, too tired to move. His dry eyes couldn't close; he stared blankly at the ceiling with some water stains. Unable to sleep, he sat up. He glanced at the room, then dared not look again. He didn't know what to do. He went out and bought a pack of "Yellow Lion" cigarettes. Sitting on the edge of the kang, he lit a cigarette; he didn't really want to smoke. Staring blankly at the blue smoke at the tip of the cigarette, tears suddenly streamed down, not only thinking of Huniu but of everything. Several years had passed since he came to the city, and this was the result of his efforts—just like this, just like this! He couldn't even cry out loud! The rickshaw, the rickshaw, the rickshaw was his livelihood. He bought it, lost it; bought it again, sold it; three ups and three downs, like a ghost, always out of reach, only suffering hardship and grievances. It was all gone, everything was gone, even a wife! Although Huniu was fierce, how could he form a family without her? Looking at the things in the room, they were all hers, but she herself was buried outside the city! The more he thought, the more he hated; his tears were blocked by anger. He fiercely puffed on the cigarette, the more he didn't want to smoke, the more he forced himself to. After finishing the cigarette, he held his head in his hands, his mouth and heart burning, wanting to scream wildly, to spit out all the blood in his heart to feel relieved.

He didn't know when, but Xiaofuzi came in, standing in front of the vegetable chopping board in the outer room, staring blankly at him.

He suddenly looked up and saw her, and tears quickly flowed again. At this moment, even if he saw a dog, he would cry; his heart full of grievances, he wanted to vent when he met a living thing; he wanted to talk to her, to get some sympathy. But there were too many words, and his mouth couldn't open.

"Brother Xiang!" She moved closer, "I've tidied everything up."

He nodded, not bothering to thank her; politeness in sorrow was hypocrisy. "What do you plan to do?"

"Ah?" He seemed not to understand, but then he quickly understood and shook his head—he couldn't think of a solution.

She took two more steps forward, her face suddenly flushed, revealing a few white teeth, but she couldn't speak. Her life had forced her to forget shame, but when it came to serious matters, she was still a sincere woman: a woman's heart largely operates on shame. "I think..." She only managed to say that much. She had a lot to say in her heart; her face flushed, and all her words suddenly scattered, unable to be recalled.

There are not many true words in the world, and a woman's blush is worth more than a lot of words; even Xiangzi understood her meaning. In his eyes, she was the most beautiful woman, beautiful to the bone, even if her whole body were covered in sores, and her flesh rotted away, she would still be beautiful in his heart. She was beautiful, she was young, she was strong, she was diligent and thrifty. If Xiangzi wanted to marry again, she was an ideal person. He didn't want to remarry immediately; he couldn't think about anything. But since she was willing, and because the pressure of life forced her to bring it up immediately, he seemed to have no way to refuse. She herself was so good, and had helped him so much, he could only nod. He really wanted to go over and hug her, cry his heart out, cry away all his grievances, and then work hard with her, united in heart, to continue their arduous struggle. In her, he saw the comfort a man could and should receive from a woman. He wasn't much of a talker, but when he saw her, he was willing to talk freely; with her listening, his words wouldn't be wasted; her nod or smile was the most perfect answer, making him feel truly "at home."

Just then, Xiaofuzi's second younger brother came in: "Sister! Dad's here!"

She frowned. As soon as she pushed open the door, Erqiangzi had already walked into the courtyard. "What were you doing in Xiangzi's room?" Erqiangzi's eyes widened, his feet stumbling like garlic, swaying from side to side as he lunged forward: "Haven't you sold yourself enough, now you have to let Xiangzi play with you for free? You shameless thing!"

Xiangzi heard his name and rushed out, standing behind Xiaofuzi. "I say, Xiangzi," Erqiangzi tried to puff out his chest, swaying unsteadily, "I say, Xiangzi, are you still a human? You can take advantage of anyone, but only her? What kind of person are you!"

Xiangzi didn't want to bully a drunkard, but the pent-up frustration in his heart made him unable to control his anger. He stepped forward. Four red eyes met, as if sparks would fly in the air. Xiangzi grabbed Erqiangzi's shoulder, like lifting a child, and threw him far away.

The pangs of conscience, fueled by a little alcohol, turned into rage: Erqiangzi's drunkenness was somewhat feigned. After this fall, he sobered up halfway. He wanted to counterattack, but he knew he was no match for Xiangzi. To leave so obediently was also very unsatisfying. He sat on the ground, unwilling to get up, and it was inconvenient to just sit there. His mind was in a mess, so he just blurted out: "What does my disciplining my children have to do with you? Beat me? Your grandma! You're not worthy!"

Xiangzi didn't want to retort, just quietly waited for him to counterattack.

Xiaofuzi, with tears in her eyes, didn't know what to do. Persuading her father was useless, and watching Xiangzi beat him also made her uneasy. She searched all over herself and scraped together a dozen coppers, which she handed to her brother. Her brother usually never dared to approach his father, but today, seeing his father beaten on the ground, he was a little bolder. "Here, go!"

Erqiangzi, with glaring eyes, took the money, and as he stood up, he grumbled, "You bunch of bitches! You've angered your grandpa, damn it, I'll get a knife and slaughter all of you!" As he neared the street gate, he shouted, "Xiangzi! We'll settle this score [1] outside!"

After Erqiangzi left, Xiangzi and Xiaofuzi went into the room together.

"I have no choice!" she muttered to herself, a sentence that summarized all her difficulties and contained infinite hope—if Xiangzi was willing to marry her, she would have a way.

Xiangzi, after this incident, saw many dark shadows in her.He still liked her, but he couldn't bear the responsibility of supporting her two younger brothers and a drunken father! He didn't dare to think that if Huniu died, he would be free; Huniu also had her good points, at least she helped him a lot financially. He didn't dare to think that if Xiaofuzi were to cling to him, it was also true that her whole family couldn't earn a living. Love or not, the poor had to decide based on money; "romantics" only existed in wealthy families.

He began to pack his things.

"Are you moving out?" Xiaofuzi's lips were completely white.

"Moving out!" He hardened his heart. In an unjust world, the poor relied on a hardened heart to maintain their personal freedom, that very, very small bit of freedom.

She glanced at him, then walked out with her head down. She wasn't resentful, nor angry, just despairing.

Huniu's jewelry and better clothes were all taken into the coffin. All that remained were some old clothes, a few wooden items, and some pots, bowls, and spoons. Xiangzi picked out a few better pieces of clothing from among them and set them aside; the rest, including clothes and utensils, he sold. He called a "drummer" (peddler) and sold everything for a dozen or so yuan at a fixed price. He was eager to move, eager to get rid of these things, so he didn't bother to find more people to slowly bargain with. The "drummer" packed up the things and left. In the room, only his bedding and the few selected clothes remained, lying on the matless kang. The room was completely empty, and he felt a little relieved, as if he had shed many entanglements and could now go far away. But, after a short while, he thought of those things again. The table had been moved, but its legs had left some traces—piles of fine dust, forming small square patches along the wall. Looking at these marks, he thought of the things, thought of the people, all gone as if in a dream. Regardless of whether the things were good or bad, or the people good or bad, without them, his heart had nowhere to rest. He sat on the edge of the kang and pulled out another "Yellow Lion" cigarette.

With the cigarette, he pulled out a crumpled banknote. Intentionally or unintentionally, he pulled out all his money; for the past two days, he hadn't bothered to count his accounts. He pulled out a pile: silver dollars, banknotes, copper coins, what have you. The pile wasn't small; he counted it, and it was less than twenty yuan. Adding the dozen or so yuan from selling things, his total assets were only a little over thirty yuan.

He placed the money on the kang bricks and stared at it, unsure whether to cry or laugh. There was no one in the room, no things, only himself and this pile of old, moldy, dirty money. What was this for?

He sighed deeply, helplessly put the money in his bosom, then picked up his bedding and the few clothes, and went to find Xiaofuzi.

"Keep these clothes to wear! Store the bedding here for a while, I'll go find a good rickshaw depot first, then come back for it." Not daring to look at Xiaofuzi, he finished saying all this in one breath with his head down.

She said nothing, only assented twice.

Xiangzi found a rickshaw depot, came back for his bedding, and saw that her eyes were swollen from crying. He couldn't say anything, but he tried every method to come up with these two sentences: "Wait! When I'm doing well, I'll come! I'll definitely come!"

She nodded, saying nothing.

Xiangzi only rested for one day, then went back to pulling his rickshaw as usual. He no longer pulled fares with the same fiery enthusiasm as before, but he also didn't deliberately slack off; he just muddled through day by day, indifferently but not厌倦. After muddling through for about a month, he felt very peaceful in his heart. His face had filled out a bit, but it was no longer as rosy as before; his complexion was yellowish, not appearing robust, nor showing signs of weakness. His eyes were very bright, but without expression, always shining as if full of spirit, yet also as if seeing nothing. His demeanor was much like a tree after a storm, standing quietly in the sunlight, not daring to move again. He had never been fond of talking, and now he spoke even less. The weather was already very warm, and the willow branches were covered with tender leaves. Sometimes he would park his rickshaw facing the sun, muttering to himself with slightly moving lips, and sometimes he would tilt his head back to receive the sunlight, taking a short nap; unless absolutely necessary, he hardly spoke to anyone.

But he had become addicted to cigarettes. As soon as he sat on the rickshaw, his large hand would reach under the chest pad. Lighting a cigarette, he would inhale and exhale very slowly, his eyes following the smoke rings upwards, staring blankly, then nodding, as if he had understood something.

When pulling the rickshaw, he was still more agile than the average rickshaw puller, but he no longer ran desperately. He was especially careful when turning corners and going up and down slopes. Almost excessively careful. If someone wanted to race him, no matter how they teased or provoked him, he would keep his head down and say nothing, continuing to run at an unhurried pace. He seemed to have seen through what rickshaw pulling was all about, no longer seeking any glory or praise from it.

At the depot, however, he made friends; although he wasn't very talkative, silent geese also liked to fly in flocks. If he didn't make friends, his loneliness would probably be unbearable for him. As soon as he pulled out his cigarette case, he would pass it around to everyone. Sometimes, when people saw only one cigarette left in his case, they would be too embarrassed to take it, and he would simply say, "Buy more!" When everyone gambled, he no longer stood aside as before, but would come over to watch, and sometimes even place a bet, not caring about winning or losing, seemingly just to show everyone that he was sociable and understood that after a few days of hard work, everyone should have some fun. When they drank, he would join them; he didn't drink much, but he would pay for some drinks and food for everyone. Things he used to look down upon now seemed somewhat interesting to him—since his own path was blocked, he had no choice but to admit that others were doing things right. If there was a wedding or funeral among his friends, he used to not understand social etiquette, but now he would contribute forty copper coins, or follow a "public agreement" [2]. Not only did he contribute money, but he also personally attended the mourning or celebration, because he understood that these things were not just about wasting money, but about fulfilling necessary human obligations. Here, people cried or laughed genuinely, not just making a fuss.

He dared not touch those thirty-odd yuan. He got a piece of white cloth, and with his clumsy hands, he used a large needle to sew the money inside, always keeping it close to his body. He didn't want to spend it, nor did he want to buy another rickshaw, but just kept it with him as a precaution—who knew what calamities might strike in the future! Illness, unexpected misfortunes, could come upon him at any time, so he had to be prepared. People were not made of iron, he now understood.

As the beginning of autumn approached, he took on another monthly contract. This time, it was more leisurely than any previous job in a mansion; if it weren't, he wouldn't have accepted it. He now knew how to choose jobs, only taking suitable monthly contracts; otherwise, pulling individual fares was fine, unlike before when he eagerly sought jobs in mansions. He understood that he should take care of his body; a rickshaw puller who wanted to work himself to death—like he used to—would only lose his life without gaining any benefit. Experience taught people how to be a bit more cunning, because one only had one life!

This time, his workplace was near Yonghegong. The master, surnamed Xia, was over fifty years old, educated and refined; his family included a wife and twelve children. Recently, he had taken a concubine, and not daring to let his family know, he deliberately chose a secluded place to set up a small separate household. In this small household near Yonghegong, there were only Mr. Xia and his new concubine; besides them, there was a maid, and a rickshaw puller—who was Xiangzi. Xiangzi liked this job very much. First, let's talk about the courtyard. There were only six rooms in total in the courtyard. Mr. Xia occupied three rooms, the kitchen took one, and the remaining two served as servants' quarters. The courtyard was very small, with a half-grown small jujube tree leaning against the south wall, its tip adorned with a dozen or so half-red jujubes. When Xiangzi swept the courtyard, it took almost two or three sweeps to go from one end to the other, which was very easy. There were no flowers or plants to water. He really wanted to tidy up that jujube tree, but he knew how willful jujube trees were, crooked and unruly, so he didn't bother to touch it.

Other work was not much either. Mr. Xia went to the yamen to work in the morning and didn't return until five in the afternoon. Xiangzi only needed to drop him off and pick him up; once home, Mr. Xia would not go out again, as if seeking refuge. Mrs. Xia, however, often went out, but always returned around four o'clock, so that Xiangzi could pick up Mr. Xia—once he was picked up, Xiangzi's day's work was considered done. Moreover, the places Mrs. Xia went were nothing more than Dong'an Market and Zhongshan Park, and after dropping her off, there was plenty of time to rest. Xiangzi did these tasks as if playing.

Mr. Xia was very tight-fisted, unwilling to part with even a small coin easily; coming and going, he looked straight ahead, as if there were no people or things on the street. His wife, however, was generous, going out to buy things every few days; if it was food, and it wasn't tasty, she would give it to the servants; if it was an item, when it was time to buy a new one, she would first give the old one to the servants, so she could negotiate with Mr. Xia for money. Mr. Xia's life mission seemed to be to dedicate all his energy and money to his concubine with utmost devotion; beyond that, he had no life or enjoyment. His money had to go out through his concubine's hands; he himself wouldn't spend it, let alone give it to others—it was said that his original wife and twelve children lived in Baoding, sometimes not receiving a single coin from him for four or five months.

Xiangzi disliked this Mr. Xia: all day long, he would bend his back, shrink his neck, coming and going like a thief, eyes fixed on his toes, always silent, never spending money, never smiling, even sitting in the rickshaw he looked like a skinny monkey; but occasionally, when he said a word or two, he would say it in a way that was extremely unpopular, as if everyone else was a scoundrel and only he himself was a learned and refined gentleman. Xiangzi didn't like such people. But he saw "the matter" as "the matter"; as long as he earned money monthly, what else mattered?! Moreover, the wife was quite open-minded, often giving him food and necessities; never mind, just treat him as pulling an unfeeling monkey. As for the wife, Xiangzi only regarded her as a woman who would give him some small change, and didn't particularly like her. She was much prettier than Xiaofuzi, and steeped in perfume and cosmetics, wrapped in silk and satin, far beyond what Xiaofuzi could compare to. However, although she was beautiful and dressed elegantly, for some reason, whenever he saw her, he would think of Huniu; there was always something about her that resembled Huniu, not the clothes, nor her appearance, but some attitude or spirit that Xiangzi couldn't find the right words to describe. He just felt that she and Huniu were, in the words he could think of, "of the same ilk." She was very young, at most twenty-two or twenty-three, but her demeanor was very mature, not at all like a newly married woman, just like Huniu who never had the shyness and gentleness of a young girl. She had permed hair, wore high heels, and her clothes were tailored just right to help her twist her body with sharp angles. Even Xiangzi could see that although she dressed so fashionably, she lacked the poise that ordinary ladies possessed. But she also didn't seem to have come from a prostitute background. Xiangzi couldn't figure out what she was about. He just felt that she was somewhat terrifying, as terrifying as Huniu. However, Huniu wasn't as young as her, nor as beautiful; so Xiangzi feared her even more, as if she carried all the formidable and malicious aspects of women he had experienced. He simply dared not look at her directly.

After spending some days here, he feared her even more. When pulling Mr. Xia out, Xiangzi had never seen him spend any money; however, Mr. Xia would sometimes go shopping—to a large pharmacy to buy medicine. Xiangzi didn't know what kind of medicine he bought; but whenever he bought medicine, the couple seemed particularly happy, and even the usually quiet Mr. Xia appeared especially energetic. After being energetic for two or three days, Mr. Xia would become quiet again, and his back would be even more bent, much like a live fish bought from the street, momentarily lively when first put in water, but soon becoming docile again. Whenever Xiangzi saw Mr. Xia sitting in the rickshaw like a ghost, he knew it was time to go to the pharmacy again. He disliked Mr. Xia, but every time he went to the pharmacy, he couldn't help but feel sorry for this old skinny monkey. When Mr. Xia returned home with the medicine package, Xiangzi would think of Huniu, and an indescribable sadness would fill his heart. He didn't want to harbor resentment towards the deceased, but looking at himself, looking at Mr. Xia, he couldn't help but resent her; no matter what, his body was no longer as strong as before, and Huniu should bear most of the responsibility.

He really wanted to quit his job. But to quit for such an irrelevant reason seemed inappropriate; smoking a "Yellow Lion," he muttered to himself, "Why meddle in other people's business?!"

[1]

 To put this matter aside, meaning it won't be settled for now, to be discussed later.

[2]

 Public agreement, a jointly agreed-upon gift. Twenty-one

When chrysanthemums were out of season, Mrs. Xia bought four pots of flowers, and one was broken by the maid, Yang Ma, leading to an argument between them. Yang Ma, from the countryside, fundamentally believed that flowers and plants were not important things; however, since she had broken someone else's property, no matter how unimportant it was, it was always due to her own carelessness, so she dared not say a word. But when Mrs. Xia kept nagging endlessly, cursing wildly and rudely, Yang Ma's temper could no longer be suppressed, and she retorted. When country folk get angry, they don't speak with restraint; she blurted out the crudest insults. Mrs. Xia stomped her feet and cursed for a while,"""Tell Aunt Yang to pack her bags and get out immediately.

Xiangzi never came over to mediate. His mouth wasn't good at breaking up fights, much less mediating a quarrel between two women. When he heard Aunt Yang curse Mrs. Xia, calling her a prostitute, a stinking whore ridden by thousands and touched by tens of thousands, he knew Aunt Yang's goose was cooked. At the same time, he realized that if Aunt Yang was out, he would be out too; Mrs. Xia probably wouldn't keep a servant who knew her history. After Aunt Yang left, he waited to be dismissed, calculating that his time to pack up would likely come when the new maid arrived. But he wasn't worried about this; experience had taught him to calmly take and leave jobs, without getting emotional.

However, after Aunt Yang left, Mrs. Xia became very polite to Xiangzi. Without a maid, she had to go to the kitchen herself to cook. She gave Xiangzi money and told him to go buy groceries. When he brought them back, she instructed him on what needed peeling and what needed washing. While he peeled and washed vegetables, she chopped meat and cooked rice, chatting with him as she worked. She wore a pink sanitary gown, with a pair of green trousers underneath, and white satin embroidered slippers on her feet. Xiangzi worked clumsily with his head down, not daring to look at her, yet wanting to. Her perfume often wafted strongly into his nostrils, seemingly telling him he had to look at her, luring him like fragrant flowers entice bees and butterflies.

Xiangzi knew the power of women, and also their benefits; one Huniu was enough to make anyone fear women, yet be unable to do without them. Moreover, Mrs. Xia was far beyond what Huniu could compare to. Xiangzi couldn't help but glance at her twice. If she was as terrifying as Huniu, she had many more admirable qualities than Huniu.

Two years ago, Xiangzi would never have dared to look at her like that. Now, he didn't care much anymore: firstly, having been tempted by women, he couldn't control himself anymore. Secondly, he had gradually fallen into the "rickshaw puller" rut: what ordinary rickshaw pullers considered right, he now also saw as right; since his own efforts and self-restraint had failed, everyone else's behavior must be reasonable, and he had to be a "rickshaw puller," whether he liked it or not; being different wouldn't work. So, taking advantage of an opportunity was considered legitimate by ordinary poor people, why shouldn't Xiangzi seize an opportunity when he saw one? He looked at this woman twice, yes, she was just a woman! If she was willing, Xiangzi couldn't refuse. He couldn't believe she would be so cheap, but what if? If she didn't move, Xiangzi certainly wouldn't; if she showed some interest first, he'd be at a loss. Had she already shown some interest? Otherwise, why dismiss Aunt Yang and not immediately hire someone else, only asking Xiangzi to help cook? Why wear so much perfume when going to the kitchen? Xiangzi dared not decide anything, dared not hope for anything, yet deep down he vaguely wanted to decide something, to have some hope. He felt like he was having an unreal good dream, knowing it was a dream, yet willing to continue dreaming. Life had a heat that forced him to admit his worthlessness, and in this worthlessness lay the greatest joy—or perhaps the greatest sorrow, who cared!

A glimmer of hope stirred some courage; some courage ignited a great heat; fire burned in his heart. There was nothing cheap here, neither he nor she was cheap, lust was equal!

A touch of fear awakened reason; a touch of reason extinguished the fire in his heart; he almost wanted to flee immediately. There was only sorrow here, taking this path would surely lead to ridicule!

Suddenly hopeful, suddenly fearful, his heart felt like it was suffering from malaria. This was harder to bear than when he met Huniu; back then, he knew nothing, like a young bee venturing out for the first time and falling into a spiderweb; now, he knew how to be careful, and also how to be bold, he inexplicably wanted to slide down, yet clearly feared falling.

He didn't look down on this concubine, this streetwalker, this beauty; she was everything, and nothing. If he could find some self-justification, he thought, it was that old skinny monkey-like Mr. Xia who was hateful, and deserved some retribution. With a husband like him, whatever she did was not wrong. With a master like him, he—Xiangzi—whatever he did didn't matter. His courage grew.

However, she didn't notice whether he looked at her or not. After cooking, she ate alone in the kitchen; when she finished, she called out to Xiangzi: "You eat. After you finish, you must wash the dishes. When you go to pick up Mr. in the afternoon, buy the vegetables for dinner on your way, so you don't have to go out again. Tomorrow is Sunday, Mr. will be home, I'm going out to find a maid. Do you know anyone, can you recommend one? Maids are so hard to find! All right, go eat first, don't let it get cold!"

She spoke very generously, naturally. That pink sanitary gown suddenly—in Xiangzi's eyes—seemed much plainer. He felt somewhat disappointed, and from disappointment, he felt ashamed, realizing that he was no longer a strong-willed person, not only not strong-willed, but a bad person! He muddled through two bowls of rice, feeling very bored. After washing the dishes, he sat down in his room and chain-smoked who knows how many "Yellow Lion" cigarettes!

When he went to pick up Mr. Xia in the afternoon, he hated this old skinny monkey for some reason. He really wanted to pull the rickshaw fast, then let go and throw the old fellow half to death. Only then did he understand that when he used to pull a rickshaw in a mansion, the master's third concubine and the eldest young master were not very discreet, and after the master found out, how the eldest young master almost poisoned the master; he had previously thought the eldest young master was too young and ignorant, but now he understood why that master deserved to die. However, he didn't want to kill anyone, he just found Mr. Xia annoying and hateful, but had no way to punish him. He deliberately jolted the rickshaw handles up and down, shaking this old monkey a few times. The old monkey didn't say anything, but Xiangzi felt a bit uneasy. He had never done such a thing, and even if he had a reason to do it occasionally, he couldn't forgive himself. Regret made him somewhat indifferent to everything; why deliberately seek discomfort? No matter what, he was a rickshaw puller, doing his job well for others was enough, what was the use of thinking about other things?

His heart calmed down, and he forgot about this fruitless matter; occasionally, when he remembered it, he found it a bit amusing.

The next day, Mrs. Xia went out to look for a maid. She came back with a trial maid after a short while. Xiangzi gave up hope, but deep down, he felt uneasy.

After lunch on Monday, Mrs. Xia dismissed the trial maid, finding her too unclean. Then, she told Xiangzi to buy a pound of chestnuts.

After buying a pound of cooked chestnuts and returning, Xiangzi called out from outside the door.

"Bring them in," she said from inside the room.

Xiangzi entered. She was powdering her face in front of the mirror, still wearing the pink sanitary gown, but had changed into a light green lower garment. Seeing Xiangzi enter in the mirror, she quickly turned around and smiled at him. Xiangzi suddenly saw Huniu in this smile, a young and beautiful Huniu. He froze there. His courage, hope, fear, and caution were all gone, leaving only a breath of hot air, which could be large or small, supporting his entire being. This breath made him advance if he advanced, retreat if he retreated; he no longer had a will of his own.

The next evening, he pulled his bedding and returned to the factory.

What he usually feared and found most shameful, he now revealed to everyone with a laugh—he couldn't urinate!

Everyone scrambled to tell him what medicine to buy or which doctor to see. No one found it shameful; they all sympathetically offered advice, and with a slight blush, proudly recounted their own similar experiences. Several young men had bought this disease with money, several middle-aged men had acquired this condition for free, several who had been on monthly contracts had similar experiences of varying degrees, and several who had been on monthly contracts hadn't personally experienced this, but had other stories about their masters that were quite worth telling. Xiangzi's illness opened their hearts, and they spoke to him intimately. He himself forgot shame, but he didn't take pride in it either; he simply endured this illness calmly, much like catching a cold or suffering from heatstroke. When the pain came, he felt a slight regret; when he felt comfortable for a while, he remembered that sweetness again. No matter what, he wasn't anxious; life's experiences had taught him to take life lightly, what was the use of being anxious?

A little medicine, a folk remedy, cost him over ten yuan; the illness was not cured at the root. Carelessly, he thought he was better and stopped taking medicine. When it was cloudy or the seasons changed, his joints would ache, and he would temporarily take some medicine or just tough it out, not taking it seriously at all. Since his life was bitter to the end, what did his body matter? Once he figured this out, even a fly could find pleasure in a cesspool, let alone a living person as big as him.

After the illness passed, he almost became a different person. He was still as tall, but that upright spirit was gone. His shoulders deliberately slumped forward, his mouth drooped, and a cigarette dangled from his lips. Sometimes he would clip a half-smoked cigarette behind his ear, not for convenience, but purely for show [1]. He still didn't talk much, but when he did open his mouth, he would force out some witty remarks, even if they weren't perfectly smooth or sharp, at least they had that spirit. With his mind relaxed, his posture and demeanor became sloppy.

However, compared to ordinary rickshaw pullers, he couldn't be considered very bad. When he sat alone, thinking of his former self, he still wanted to be strong, unwilling to just slide down like this. Although being strong was useless, destroying himself wasn't necessarily clever. At such times, he thought of buying a rickshaw again. His thirty-odd yuan, more than ten of which had been spent on treating his illness, was a waste! But with twenty-odd yuan as a base, he still had more hope than others who were completely shooting blanks. Thinking this, he really wanted to throw away the half-smoked pack of "Yellow Lion" cigarettes and quit smoking and drinking from then on, gritting his teeth to save money. From saving money, he thought of buying a rickshaw, and from buying a rickshaw, he thought of Xiaofuzi. He felt a little sorry for her; since leaving the crowded courtyard, he hadn't visited her, and not only had he not improved, but he had also contracted a nasty disease!

When he met his friends, he still smoked as usual, and drank when he had the chance, completely forgetting Xiaofuzi. With his friends, he didn't take the lead in doing anything, but if others wanted to do something, he couldn't help but join in. The day's hard work and a belly full of grievances could only be temporarily forgotten by chatting and joking with them. Immediate comfort drove away noble aspirations; he wanted to be happy for a while, and then sleep soundly until dark; who wouldn't like that, since life was so boring, painful, and hopeless! Life's festering sores could only be numbed for a while by the poison of tobacco, alcohol, and women, fighting poison with poison. The poison would eventually return to the heart one day, who didn't know this, yet who could have a better idea to replace it?!

The less he was willing to strive, the more he pitied himself. Before, he feared nothing, but now he sought ease and comfort: he wouldn't go out with his rickshaw in wind or rain; if he had a slight ache, he would rest for two or three days. Self-pity led to selfishness; he wouldn't lend a single coin of his money to others, saving it specifically for his own use on windy or rainy days. He could share cigarettes and alcohol, but money couldn't be lent out; he was more delicate and pitiful than anyone else. The more idle he was, the lazier he became, and with nothing to do, he felt bored, so he constantly needed some entertainment or a good meal. When he thought that he shouldn't waste time and money like this, he always had a ready-made phrase in his heart, a phrase forged by countless experiences: "Didn't I used to be strong-willed? Did it do me any good?" No one could refute this last sentence, no one could explain it away; so, who could stop Xiangzi from sinking lower?!

Laziness can make one ill-tempered. Xiangzi now knew how to glare at people. At rickshaw seats, at patrolmen, at anyone, he decided he would no longer politely humor them. When he worked hard and diligently, he never received justice. Now, he knew how precious his sweat was, and he would shed as little as possible; if someone wanted to take advantage of him, they could forget it. He would casually put down his rickshaw, too lazy to move it again, regardless of whether it was the right place to park. If a patrolman came to interfere, he would talk but not move, delaying as long as possible. When he saw that he absolutely had to move the rickshaw, his mouth would be even busier; he would curse. If the patrolman didn't want to be cursed at, then a fight was no big deal. Fortunately, Xiangzi knew he was strong; he would beat the patrolman first, and then going to jail wouldn't be a loss. During a fight, he would again feel his strength and skill, unleashing all his power onto others' flesh. He saw the light, and the sun seemed to shine especially brightly. Saving his strength to prepare for a fight, something he had never even thought of before, had now become a reality, and it was something that could make him feel good for a while; thinking about it, how amusing!

Not to mention an unarmed patrolman, even the cars that swarmed the streets, he wasn't afraid of them. When a car came head-on, kicking up all the dust on the ground, Xiangzi wouldn't dodge, no matter how loud the car's horn blared, no matter how anxious the passengers were. The car had no choice but to slow down. When it slowed, Xiangzi would also dodge, avoiding a lot of dust. If a car came from behind, he would use the same trick. He had figured it out: cars wouldn't dare to hurt people anyway, so why dodge so early? """How could it kick up all that dust? Patrolmen are specifically there to clear the way for cars, fearing they won't go fast enough or kick up enough dust. Xiangzi isn't a patrolman, so he won't let cars run rampant. In the eyes of the patrolmen, Xiangzi is a first-class "troublemaker," but they don't dare provoke a "troublemaker." The laziness of the suffering is the natural result of effort that has come to nothing; the defiance of the suffering contains some justice.

He was absolutely impolite with the passengers. He would pull them exactly where they said, not a single step more. If they said "to the entrance" of an alley, and then told him to pull them "inside" the alley, no way! The passenger would glare, and Xiangzi would glare back even harder. He knew how much those gentlemen in Western suits feared getting their clothes dirty, and he also knew how arrogant and stingy most of those gentlemen in Western suits were. Fine, he was already prepared; if things went south, he'd grab them by the sleeve of their fifty or sixty dollar Western suit, at least leaving a big black handprint! Even with such a handprint, they'd still have to pay, for they knew how much strength that big hand possessed, and that grip had already squeezed their slender arms painfully.

He didn't run slowly, but he wouldn't speed up for nothing. If a passenger urged him, his big foot would scrape the ground: "Faster? How much more?" There was no politeness; he was selling his sweat and blood. He no longer hoped for extra tips from their kindness; it was a fair price for a fair service. He had to make it clear first before exerting his strength.

He no longer cherished the rickshaw as much. Since his desire to buy a rickshaw had cooled, he was indifferent to other people's rickshaws. A rickshaw was just a rickshaw; pulling it meant he could earn enough for food and the rental fee, and that was that. If he didn't pull it, he wouldn't have to pay the rental fee, so as long as he had enough money for a day's food, there was no need to pull it out. The relationship between man and rickshaw was just that. Of course, he still wouldn't intentionally damage someone else's rickshaw, but he also wouldn't go out of his way to protect it. Sometimes, if another rickshaw puller accidentally damaged a part, he wouldn't jump up and down and argue with them. Instead, he would calmly pull it back to the shop, and if it cost fifty cents to repair, he'd pay twenty cents, and that was the end of it. If the owner didn't agree, that was easy to handle; the final solution always involved a fight. If the owner wanted to fight, Xiangzi would oblige!

Experience is the fertilizer of life; what kind of experience one has determines what kind of person one becomes. You can't grow peonies in the desert. Xiangzi had completely fallen into the rut; he was no better than other rickshaw pullers, nor was he worse, just a rickshaw puller like any other. In this way, he felt more comfortable than before, and others also found him more agreeable; all crows are black, and he didn't wish to be the only white one.

Winter arrived again, and the yellow wind blowing from the desert could freeze many people to death overnight. Listening to the wind, Xiangzi buried his head in the quilt, not daring to get up. Only when the wind stopped its wolf-howling, ghost-shrieking noise did he reluctantly get up, undecided whether to go out or take a day off. He was too lazy to grab the icy rickshaw handles, fearing the nauseating, choking wind. The gale feared the sunset; it wasn't until after four o'clock that the wind completely died down, and a faint reddish glow of the setting sun appeared in the dim yellow sky. He forced himself to pull out the rickshaw. With his hands tucked in his sleeves, his chest leaning against the rickshaw handles, he slowly and listlessly swayed, a half-smoked cigarette dangling from his mouth. Soon, it was dark, and he wanted to quickly get a couple of fares so he could finish early. He was too lazy to light the lamps until the patrolmen along the road urged him four or five times before he finally lit them.

In front of the Drum Tower, he snatched a passenger under the lamplight, pulling them towards the East City. He didn't even take off his thick cotton padded gown, just trotted along in a daze. He knew it wasn't proper, but if it wasn't proper, then so be it; who would give him more money for being proper? This wasn't pulling a rickshaw, it was just getting by; even with sweat on his head, he still wouldn't take off his long clothes, making do if he could. Entering a small alley, a dog, perhaps finding the long-clothed rickshaw puller displeasing, followed him, barking. He stopped the rickshaw, reversed his grip on the cloth duster, and desperately chased the dog, beating it. After chasing the dog out of sight, he waited a while longer to see if it dared to come back. The dog didn't dare to return, and Xiangzi felt a bit satisfied: "Damn it! You think I'm scared of you!" "What kind of rickshaw pulling is this? Let me ask you!" The person in the rickshaw asked impatiently.

Xiangzi's heart skipped a beat; the voice sounded familiar. The alley was very dark, and although the rickshaw lamps were bright, the light was all directed downwards, so he couldn't see who was in the rickshaw. The person in the rickshaw wore a large hood, with their mouth and nose wrapped within a large scarf, only their two eyes visible. Xiangzi was guessing. The person in the rickshaw spoke again: "Aren't you Xiangzi?"

Xiangzi understood; it was Liu Siye in the rickshaw! He felt a sudden rush of heat throughout his body, not knowing what to do.

"Where's my daughter?"

"Dead!" Xiangzi stood there blankly, not knowing if it was himself or someone else who had uttered those two words.

"What? Dead?"

"Dead!"

"Fell into your damn hands, how could she not die?!"

Xiangzi suddenly found himself: "Get down! Get down! You're too old, you can't take a beating from me; get down!"

Liu Siye's hands trembled as he got down. "Where is she buried? I'm asking you!" "None of your business!" Xiangzi pulled the rickshaw and walked away.

He walked a long distance, then looked back. The old man—like a large dark shadow—was still standing there.

[1]

 To show off, to be flashy.

Twenty-two

Xiangzi forgot where he was going. He held his head high, gripped the handlebars tightly with both hands, his eyes shining, and strode forward; he only cared about walking, regardless of direction or destination. His heart was joyful, his body light, as if all the bad luck he had experienced since marrying Huniu had been poured onto Liu Siye. Forgetting the cold, forgetting to arrange business, he just wanted to walk forward, as if by walking somewhere he could find his original self, that unburdened, pure, strong-willed, and always striving Xiangzi. Remembering the dark figure standing in the alley, that old man, it seemed there was nothing more to say; defeating Liu Si was like defeating everything. Although he hadn't punched or kicked the old man, the old man had lost his only kin, while Xiangzi was free and easy; who could say this wasn't retribution! Even if the old man didn't die of anger, he must be close to death! Old Man Liu had everything, Xiangzi had nothing; but now, Xiangzi could still happily pull his rickshaw, while the old man couldn't even find his daughter's grave! Well, let the old man have piles of foreign money and a huge temper, he couldn't control this poor wretch who lived hand-to-mouth!

The more he thought, the happier he became. He really wanted to sing a few lines loudly, so that everyone in the world could hear this triumphal song—Xiangzi was alive again, Xiangzi had won! The evening chill bit at his face, but he didn't feel cold, only exhilarated. The streetlights cast a cold glow, but Xiangzi felt a warm comfort in his heart, everywhere was light, illuminating his future. He hadn't smoked for a long time, and didn't want to anymore; from now on, no smoking or drinking, Xiangzi wanted to start anew, to strive and improve himself as before. Today he had defeated Liu Si, and he would defeat Liu Si forever; Liu Si's curses were enough to make Xiangzi even more successful, more hopeful. With a sigh of relief, Xiangzi would forever breathe fresh air. Looking at his hands and feet, wasn't Xiangzi still very young? Xiangzi would be forever young, let Huniu die, Liu Si die, but Xiangzi would live, happily, strongly, alive—all evil people would suffer retribution, would die, the soldiers who stole his rickshaw, Mrs. Yang who didn't feed her servants, Huniu who deceived and oppressed him, Liu Si who looked down on him, Detective Sun who swindled his money, Second Grandma Chen who fooled him, Mrs. Xia who seduced him... all would die, only the loyal Xiangzi would live, live forever! "But, Xiangzi, you must work hard from now on!" he admonished himself. "Why shouldn't I work hard? I have ambition, strength, and I'm young!" He defended himself: "With a joyful heart, who can stop Xiangzi from starting a family and career? If you put what happened a few days ago on anyone else, who would be happy, who wouldn't decline? All that is past, tomorrow you will see a new Xiangzi, better than before, much better!"

Muttering to himself, he put more effort into his steps, as if to bear witness to his words—it wasn't nonsense, I really do have a strong body. Although I've been sick, and suffered from the inability to face people, what does it matter? Once the heart changes, the body immediately strengthens, no problem! He broke into a sweat, felt thirsty, and wanted a drink of water. Only then did he realize he had reached the back gate. Not bothering to go to a teahouse, he parked his rickshaw at the "parking spot" west of the city gate, called over the tea vendor's child carrying a large clay pot and a yellow sand bowl, and drank two bowls of tea that tasted like dishwater; it was extremely unpalatable, but he told himself that from now on he would have to drink this, and could no longer spend all his money on good tea and good food. Having made this decision, he might as well eat something else—something hard to swallow—as the beginning of a new life of diligence and endurance. He bought ten fried buns, which were all cabbage stems inside, and "tough" [1] and "gritty" [2] outside. No matter how bad they tasted, he swallowed them all. After eating, he wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. Where to go now?

There were only two people he could turn to, rely on, in his heart. Determined to strive for self-improvement, he had to find these two—Xiaofuzi and Mr. Cao. Mr. Cao was a "sage," and would surely forgive him, help him, and give him good advice. Following Mr. Cao's advice, and then with Xiaofuzi's help; he would work outside, she would manage inside, they would surely succeed, surely succeed, there was no doubt!

Who knew if Mr. Cao had returned? It didn't matter, he would go to Beichang Street tomorrow to inquire; if he couldn't find him there, he would go to the Zuo residence to ask. As long as he found Mr. Cao, everything would be easy. All right, he would pull rickshaws for one night today, and look for Mr. Cao tomorrow; once he found him, he would go see Xiaofuzi and tell her the good news: Xiangzi hadn't done well, but he was determined to do well, let's work together and strive forward!

With this plan made, his eyes shone like an eagle's, scanning all around with a glow. Seeing a passenger, he ran over like a bird, and took off his thick cotton jacket before even settling on a price. When he ran, his legs were indeed not as they used to be, but a surge of warmth supported his whole body, and he ran with all his might! Xiangzi was Xiangzi after all, and even when Xiangzi ran with all his might, there was still no share for others. He saw a rickshaw, he pulled a rickshaw, as if he had gone mad. Sweat flowed freely. After one trip, he felt much lighter, his legs had that elasticity again, and he wanted to run more, like a famous horse that hadn't run enough, still kicking its hooves after stopping. He didn't stop pulling rickshaws until one o'clock in the morning. Back at the rickshaw depot, besides the rickshaw rental fee, he still had over ninety cents left.

He slept until dawn; he turned over, and when he opened his eyes again, the sun was already high. The rest after fatigue was the sweetest enjoyment. He got up and stretched, his joints cracking lightly, his stomach felt completely empty, and he desperately wanted something to eat. After eating something, he smiled and told the depot owner: "I'm taking a day off, I have something to do." He calculated in his mind: take a day off, get everything done, and start a new life tomorrow. He immediately rushed to Beichang Street, to see if by any chance Mr. Cao had returned. As he walked, he prayed in his heart: Mr. Cao, please, please be back, don't let me make a wasted trip! If the first thing doesn't go well, then nothing will go well! Xiangzi has changed, won't heaven protect him?

Outside the Cao residence, his hand trembled as he pressed the doorbell. Waiting for someone to open the door, his heart was pounding. He didn't bother to think about everything in the past regarding this familiar door, he only hoped that when the door opened, he would see a familiar face. He waited, he suspected there might be no one in the courtyard, otherwise why was it so quiet, almost frighteningly quiet. Suddenly there was a slight sound from inside the door, and he was startled. The door opened, and amidst the sound of the door, there was a most precious, most intimate and lovely "Oh!" It was Gao Ma! "Xiangzi? How rare! You've gotten so thin?" Gao Ma, however, had gained some weight. "Is the master home?" Xiangzi didn't bother to say anything else.

"He's home. You're really something, you only know the master, as if we don't know each other! You don't even ask how I am! You're really something, always a 'shy carpenter—one saw (sentence)'! Come in! You've been doing well, haven't you?" She asked as she walked inside.

"Hmph! Not well!" Xiangzi smiled.

"Well, Master," Gao Ma called from outside the study, "Xiangzi is here!"

Mr. Cao was moving the narcissus in the sunlight in his room: "Come in!" "Oh, you go in, we'll talk later; I'll go tell Madam; we often talk about you! A simple person has simple luck, don't you see!" Gao Ma grumbled as she walked in.

Xiangzi entered the study: "Master, I'm here!" He wanted to say hello, but the words didn't come out.

"Ah, Xiangzi!" Mr. Cao stood in the study, wearing a short jacket, with a strangely kind and clean smile on his face. "Sit down! Well—" He thought for a moment: "We came back a long time ago, Old Cheng said you were at—yes, Renhe Depot. Gao Ma even went to look for you once, but couldn't find you. Sit down! How are you? Are things going well?"

Xiangzi's tears were about to fall. He couldn't confide in others, because his words were made of blood, hidden deep in his heart. After calming down for a long time, he wanted to let those simple words, formed from blood, flow out. Everything was in his memory, and as soon as he thought, it all came back. He had to slowly arrange and organize them. He wanted to tell a living history, although he didn't know its meaning, but that string of grievances was real and clear.

Mr. Cao saw that he was thinking, and sat down quietly, waiting for him to speak.

Xiangzi lowered his head and was in a daze for a long time, then suddenly looked up at Mr. Cao, as if if he couldn't find someone to listen to him, it would be better not to speak.

"Speak!" Mr. Cao nodded.

Xiangzi began to talk about the past, starting from how he came from the countryside to the city. He originally didn't want to talk about these useless things, but if he didn't, his heart couldn't be relieved, and things would seem incomplete. His memory was built of sweat, blood, and pain, and he couldn't just talk about it casually; once he started, he didn't want to cut off the beginning or the end. Every drop of sweat, every drop of blood, flowed from his life, so every event had a value worth telling.

He talked about how he worked hard after coming to the city, and then how he changed to pulling rickshaws. How he saved money to buy a rickshaw, how he lost it... all the way to his current situation. Even he himself felt strange why he could talk for so long, and so freely. Things, one after another, all wanted to leap out of his heart. The events themselves seemed to find the right words, one sentence after another, each sentence real, lovely, and tragic. His heart couldn't stop those things from coming out, and his words couldn't stop either. Without any hesitation or confusion, he seemed to want to pour out his whole heart in one breath. The more he spoke, the more relieved he felt, forgetting himself, because he was already wrapped up in those words, every word contained him, the strong-willed, wronged, hardworking, fallen him. After speaking, his head was sweating, his heart was empty, comfortably empty, like the empty comfort of fainting and breaking into a cold sweat.

"Now you want me to give you advice?" Mr. Cao asked.

Xiangzi nodded; his words were finished, and he seemed unwilling to open his mouth again. "Still pulling rickshaws?"

Xiangzi nodded again. He couldn't do anything else.

"Since you still have to pull rickshaws," Mr. Cao said slowly, "then there are only two paths. One is to save money to buy a rickshaw, and the other is to temporarily rent a rickshaw, right? Since you have no savings, borrowing money to buy a rickshaw would incur interest, isn't it the same? It's better to rent a rickshaw first. It's better to pull for a monthly fee, the work is steady, and food and lodging are also provided. I think it's best if you come back here; I sold my rickshaw to Mr. Zuo, if you want to come, you'll have to rent one; how about it?"

"That would be great!" Xiangzi stood up. "Master doesn't remember that incident?" "Which incident?"

"That time, when Master and Madam both ran to the Zuo residence!"

"Oh!" Mr. Cao laughed. "Who remembers that! That time, I was a bit too flustered. I stayed in Shanghai with Madam for a few months, but actually it wasn't necessary at all, Mr. Zuo had already arranged everything, and that Ruan Ming is now an official and treats me quite well. Well, you probably don't know this; never mind, I don't remember it at all. Let's talk about us: what about that Xiaofuzi you just mentioned?"

"I have no idea!"

"Let me think about it for you: if you marry her and rent a room outside, it's still not worth it; rent, coal, lamps, and charcoal are all money, not enough. If she goes to work with you, how can it be such a coincidence that you pull a rickshaw and she works as a maid, it's not easy to find! This is difficult to handle!" Mr. Cao shook his head. "Don't be too sensitive, is she reliable after all?" Xiangzi's face turned red, and he choked for a long time before saying: "She had no choice but to do that, I'll stake my head on it, she's very good! She..." His heart was in turmoil: many different emotions condensed into a ball, and then suddenly wanted to burst open, all wanting to rush out; he was speechless.

"If that's the case," Mr. Cao said hesitantly, "unless I can accommodate you here. You occupy one room, and the two of you also occupy one room; living arrangements won't be a problem. I don't know if she can wash and do things, but if she can do some work, let her help Gao Ma; Madam will be having a baby soon, and Gao Ma alone is a bit too busy. As for her, she'll eat my food for free, but I won't pay her wages, what do you think?"

"That would be great!" Xiangzi smiled innocently.

"However, I can't make the final decision on this, I have to discuss it with Madam!"

"No problem! If Madam is worried, I'll bring her over for Madam to see!" "That's good too," Mr. Cao also smiled, not expecting Xiangzi to have such a thought. "How about this, I'll mention it to Madam first, and you bring her over another day; if Madam agrees, then we'll consider it a success!"

"Then, Master, I'll go?" Xiangzi was eager to find Xiaofuzi and report this good news that he hadn't even dared to hope for.

Xiangzi left the Cao residence, it was probably around eleven o'clock, the most pleasant time of a winter day. This day was particularly clear and beautiful, with not a single cloud in the blue sky, and sunlight streaming down through the dry, cool air, giving a refreshing warmth. The crowing of roosters, the barking of dogs, and the shouts of vendors could all be heard from afar, and across the street, clear and crisp sounds could be heard, like the cries of cranes falling from the sky. The rickshaws all had their canvas tops open, and the brass fittings on the rickshaws gleamed yellow. Camels walked slowly and steadily on the sidewalk,Cars and electric trams sped through the streets, people and horses bustled on the ground, and white doves flew in the sky. The entire old city was full of movement and stillness, chaotic yet enjoyable, quiet yet enjoyable. A symphony of sounds, a myriad of lives, all beneath the clear blue sky, with trees standing silently everywhere.

Xiangzi's heart was about to leap out, flying into the sky to circle with the white doves! Everything was settled: the job, the wages, Xiaofuzi, all beautifully resolved in a few words, something he had never even imagined! Look at the sky, how clear and dry, just like the straightforward and frank people of the North. When one encounters good news, even the weather improves. He seemed to have never seen such a lovely winter day. To more practically express his joy, he bought a frozen persimmon. With one bite, his mouth was full of ice shards! The cold bit into his teeth, slowly spreading from his mouth to his chest, making him shiver. He finished it in a few bites, his tongue a little numb, but his heart felt comfortable. He strode off to find Xiaofuzi. In his mind, he already saw that courtyard, that small room, and his beloved; all he needed was a pair of wings to take him there instantly. As long as he saw her, everything before could be wiped clean, and a new world would open up. His urgency now surpassed that of going to see Mr. Cao. Mr. Cao and he had a relationship of friends and master-servant, exchanging good for good. She was not just a friend; she would entrust her entire life to him. Two people from hell would wipe away their tears and walk hand in hand with smiles. Mr. Cao's words could move him, but Xiaofuzi could move him without saying a word. He had told Mr. Cao the truth, and he would tell Xiaofuzi even more intimate things, things he couldn't tell anyone else. She, now, was his life; without her, nothing would matter. He couldn't just work for his own food and drink; he had to rescue her from that small room and then live with him in a clean, warm room, as happy, decent, and affectionate as a pair of little birds! She could ignore Erqiangzi, and she could ignore her two younger brothers; she had to come and help Xiangzi. Erqiangzi could earn his own living, and the two younger brothers could manage to pull a cart together or do other things; but Xiangzi couldn't do without her. His body, spirit, and work, there was no part that didn't need her. And she also needed a man like him.

The more he thought, the more eager and happy he became; there were many women in the world, but none as good and suitable as Xiaofuzi! He had been married, had stolen; he had encountered the beautiful and the ugly, the old and the young; but none of them could capture his heart, they were just women, not companions. True, she wasn't the pure and innocent girl he had envisioned, but precisely because of this, she was more pitiable and could help him more. That foolish country girl might be very innocent, but she would never have Xiaofuzi's ability and insight. Moreover, what about himself? He also had many dark spots in his heart! So, he and she were a perfect match, neither high nor low, like a pair of cracked but water-holding jars, perfectly placed together.

No matter how he thought about it, this was the most suitable thing. After thinking about these things, he began to think about practical matters: first, get a month's wages from Mr. Cao, buy her a cotton robe, tidy up her shoes and feet, and then take her to see Mrs. Cao. Dressed in new, plain long cotton robes, clean from head to toe, with her looks, age, and demeanor, she would surely be presentable and win Mrs. Cao's favor. No doubt!

When he arrived, he was covered in sweat. Seeing that broken gate was like seeing his old home, which he hadn't returned to for many years: the broken gate, the broken walls, the few dry yellow grasses on the gate tower, all were very endearing. He entered the gate and went straight to Xiaofuzi's room. Without bothering to knock or call out, he pulled open the door. As soon as he pulled it open, he instinctively recoiled. A middle-aged woman was sitting on the kang, wrapped in a very tattered quilt because there was no fire in the room. Xiangzi stood stunned outside the door, and a voice came from inside: "What's wrong! Bringing bad news? Why don't you say something and just walk into someone's room?! Who are you looking for?" Xiangzi didn't want to speak. All the sweat on his body suddenly receded, his hand resting on the broken door, yet he dared not abandon all hope: "I'm looking for Xiaofuzi!" "Don't know! Next time you look for someone, ask first before pulling the door! What Xiaofuzi or Dafuzi!"

Sitting at the main gate, he was stunned for a long time, his mind empty, forgetting what he was doing. Slowly, a small thought came to him, a thought as small as Xiaofuzi. Xiaofuzi walked back and forth in his mind, like a paper figure on a revolving lantern, constantly moving back and forth, without any effect. He seemed to have forgotten his relationship with her. Slowly, Xiaofuzi's figure shrank a bit, and his heart became a little more active. Only then did he realize his sadness.

When one is unsure of the outcome of things, one always thinks of the best first. Xiangzi guessed that perhaps Xiaofuzi had moved, and there was no major change. He himself was at fault, why didn't he visit her more often? Shame spurred him to action, to make up for his mistakes. It would be best to inquire first. He entered the large courtyard again and found an old neighbor to ask. He didn't get any accurate news. Still unwilling to give up hope, he didn't even bother to eat, thinking of looking for Erqiangzi; finding her two younger brothers would also work. These three men were always on the streets, so they shouldn't be hard to find.

He asked everyone he met, at the cart stands, in teahouses, in courtyards, walking all day with all his might, asking all day, but there was no news.

In the evening, he returned to the rickshaw depot, utterly exhausted, but still unwilling to forget the matter. After a day of disappointment, he dared not hope for anything anymore. Poor people die easily, and when poor people die, they are easily forgotten. Could it be that Xiaofuzi was already gone? Thinking a step further, even if she wasn't dead, it was possible that Erqiangzi had sold her again, sold her to a very distant place; this was worse than death!

Smoking and drinking became his friends again. How could he think without smoking? How could he stop thinking without getting drunk?

[1]

 Skin, not burnt.

[2]

 Gritty, impure flour, feeling like biting sand when eaten.

Twenty-three
