Xiangzi walked the streets like a lost soul, and met Xiaoma'er's grandfather. The old man no longer pulled a rickshaw. His clothes were thinner and more tattered than before. He carried a willow stick, with a large clay pot hanging in front and a broken yuanbao basket hanging behind. In the basket were some sesame cakes, fried dough sticks, and a large brick. He still recognized Xiangzi.

As they talked, Xiangzi learned that Xiaoma'er had died more than half a year ago. The old man had sold the broken rickshaw and now spent his days selling tea and some sesame cakes and fruits at the cart stand. The old man was still as kind and lovable, but his back was much more bent, his eyes teared up in the wind, and his eyelids were always red as if he had just finished crying.

Xiangzi drank a bowl of his tea and briefly told him about his grievances. "You want to make it on your own?" the old man commented on Xiangzi's words: "Who doesn't think that way? But who has made it? In the beginning, I was strong and kind-hearted, and I've been struggling until now, and look at me! Strong body? Even an iron man can't escape our inescapable net. Kind-hearted? What's the use! Good deeds are rewarded with good, evil deeds with evil, there's no such thing! When I was young, I was truly enthusiastic, treating other people's affairs as my own. Was it useful? No! I even saved lives, people who jumped into the river, people who hanged themselves, I saved them all, was there any retribution? No! I'm telling you, I might freeze to death any day. I've understood now, for those who do hard labor, trying to make it on their own is harder than climbing to heaven. What can one person do [1]? Have you seen grasshoppers? A single one can jump quite far, but if a child catches it and ties it with a string, it can't even fly. But when they gather in a swarm, forming a formation, hmph, they can devour an entire field of crops in one go, and no one can stop them! Don't you agree? My heart was kind, but I couldn't even keep my little grandson. He got sick, I had no money to buy him good medicine, and I watched him die in my arms! Don't say anything, don't say anything! —Tea! Who wants a hot bowl?"

Xiangzi truly understood: Liu Si, Mrs. Yang, Detective Sun — they wouldn't suffer bad retribution just because he cursed them; and he himself wouldn't gain anything just because he was strong-willed. Relying solely on himself, just as the old man said, was like a grasshopper tied with a string by a child. What good were wings then?

He had no intention of going to the Cao residence. If he went to the Cao residence, he would have to be strong-willed, but what was the use of being strong-willed? He might as well just drift along carelessly: if he had no food, he would pull the rickshaw; if he had enough for a day, he would rest for a day, and worry about tomorrow when it came. This was not only a method, but the only method. Saving money, buying a rickshaw, all for others to snatch, what was the point? Why not enjoy himself while he could?

Moreover, if he found Xiaofuzi, he should still strive, not for himself, but for her, shouldn't he? Since he couldn't find her, just like this old man whose grandson died, for whom was he struggling? He also told the old man about Xiaofuzi, treating the old man as a true friend.

"Who wants a hot bowl?" the old man first called out, and then thought for Xiangzi: "I guess, there are only two possibilities: either Erqiangzi sold her to someone as a concubine, or she's been taken to the White House. Hmph, most likely she's gone to the White House! How so? Since Xiaofuzi, as you just told me, was married, it's not easy for someone else to want her; people who buy concubines want 'fresh goods'. So, there's about an eighty percent chance she's gone to the White House. I'm almost sixty, and I've seen a lot: if a strong young rickshaw puller doesn't show up at the cart stand for a day or two, go look for him, he's either hired for a month or crawling in the White House; if our rickshaw pullers' daughters or wives suddenly disappear, there's a seven or eight out of ten chance they've gone there too. We sell our sweat, our women sell their flesh, I understand, I know! Go look for her there, don't hope she's really there, but — tea! Who wants a hot bowl?!" Xiangzi ran all the way to outside Xizhimen.

As soon as he left the city gate, he immediately felt the vastness. Lean trees stood by the roadside, with not even a bird on their branches. Gray trees, gray land, gray houses, all stood silently under the gray-yellow sky; looking across this expanse of gray, he saw the desolate Western Hills. North of the railway, a patch of woods, and outside the woods, a few low houses. Xiangzi calculated that this was probably the White House. He looked at the woods, there was no movement; looking further north, he could see some watery land outside the Wanshengyuan, with only a few withered reeds remaining, uneven. There was no one outside the small houses, no movement. It was so quiet everywhere, he doubted if this was the famous White House. He bravely walked towards the houses. The doors were all hung with straw mats, newly hung, yellowish and somewhat shiny. He had heard people say that the women here, in summer, would sit outside with bare backs, calling out to passersby. Those who came to patronize them would even sing "kiln tunes" [2] from afar, showing that they were not outsiders. Why was it so quiet now? Did they not do business here in winter?

As he was wondering, the straw mat of the room closest to the edge moved, revealing a woman's head. Xiangzi was startled. The head, at first glance, looked very much like Huniu's. He thought to himself: "I came to find Xiaofuzi, if I find Huniu, that would truly be seeing a ghost!"

"Come in, you silly darling!" the head spoke, but the voice was not like Huniu's; it was hoarse, very much like the old man selling wild medicine he often heard at Tianqiao, hoarse and seemingly anxious.

There was nothing in the room except the woman and a small kang. There was no mat on the kang, but there was a fire burning inside, giving off a very unpleasant, foul smell. An old quilt was placed on the kang, and the edge of the quilt, like the bricks on the kang, was shiny with grease. The woman was about forty years old, with disheveled hair and an unwashed face. She wore padded trousers below and a small blue cotton jacket above, unbuttoned. Xiangzi had to duck his head to squeeze in. As soon as he entered, she embraced him. The small cotton jacket was unbuttoned, revealing a pair of very long and large breasts.

Xiangzi sat on the edge of the kang, because if he stood, he couldn't straighten his neck. He was very happy to have met her. He often heard people say that there was a "white flour sack" in the White House, and this must be her. The nickname "white flour sack" came from her two large breasts. Xiangzi directly asked her if she had seen Xiaofuzi, but she didn't know. Xiangzi described Xiaofuzi's appearance, and she remembered: "Yes, there was such a person! Not very old, often showing a few white teeth, yes, we all called her 'Little Tender Meat'."

"Which room is she in?" Xiangzi's eyes suddenly widened with a murderous look. "Her? She's long gone!" The "white flour sack" pointed outside, "She hanged herself in the woods!"

"What?"

"Little Tender Meat was very popular after she came here. But she couldn't quite handle it, her body was quite frail. One day, at dusk, I remember it clearly, because I was sitting at the door with two or three other women. Alas, at that very moment, a customer came and went straight to her room; she didn't like sitting at the door with us, and she even got beaten for it when she first arrived,"Later, when she became famous, everyone left her alone in the room, and fortunately, those who came to visit her never looked for anyone else. After about the time it takes to eat a meal, the guest left and went straight to the woods. We didn't see anything, and no one went into the room to check on her. It wasn't until Old Fork came to collect the money from her that they saw a naked man lying in the room, sleeping soundly. He was drunk. Little Tender Meat stripped the guest's clothes, put them on herself, and escaped. She was really clever. If it hadn't been dark, she wouldn't have been able to escape even if her life depended on it. It was dark, and she was disguised as a man, fooling everyone. Old Fork immediately sent people to search everywhere, and sure enough, as soon as they entered the woods, she was hanging there. When they took her down, she had already stopped breathing, but her tongue hadn't protruded much, and her face wasn't unpleasant; she was still likable even in death! For so many months, there had been no trouble in the woods at night; she didn't come out to scare people, how benevolent!..." Xiangzi walked out, swaying, before she could finish. He walked to a cemetery, where some pine trees were planted in a square, with a dozen or so graves among the trees. The sunlight was already very faint, and it was even dimmer in the pine forest. He sat on the ground, where there was some dry grass and pine pollen. There was no sound, only a few magpies in the trees crying mournfully with long calls. This could not possibly be Little Fuzi's grave, he knew, but his tears fell in strings. Nothing was left, not even Little Fuzi, who was now buried! He was strong-willed, Little Fuzi was strong-willed, but he was only left with useless tears, and she had become a hanged ghost! A mat, buried in a mass grave, that was the end of a lifetime of effort!

Back at the rickshaw depot, he slept for two days in a daze. He no longer wanted to go to the Cao residence, and there was no need to send a message; Mr. Cao couldn't save Xiangzi's life. After sleeping for two days, he pulled out his rickshaw, his mind completely blank, no longer thinking about anything, no longer hoping for anything, only coming out to suffer for his stomach; when his stomach was full, he would go to sleep. What was there to think about, what was there to hope for? Seeing a dog so thin that its bones protruded waiting by a sweet potato vendor to eat some peels and roots, he realized that he was just like this dog, his daily actions only for picking up some sweet potato peels and roots to eat. Just getting by was everything; there was no need to think about anything else.

Humans elevated themselves from beasts, but even now, humans still drive their own kind into the realm of beasts. Xiangzi was still in that city of culture, but he had become a beast. It was not his fault at all. He stopped thinking, so even if he killed someone, he would not bear any responsibility. He no longer had hope, just falling vaguely, falling into that bottomless abyss. He ate, he drank, he whored, he gambled, he was lazy, he was cunning, because he had no heart; his heart had been taken away by others. He was left with only that tall frame of flesh, waiting to rot, preparing to go to the mass grave.

Winter passed, and the spring sunshine was nature's clothing for everyone; he rolled up all his padded clothes and sold them. He wanted to eat well, drink well, no need to save winter clothes, and certainly no intention of seeing winter again; let's be happy today, and die tomorrow! What did winter or no winter matter! Unfortunately, if he was still alive when winter came, then they would talk about it again. Originally, when he thought, he would think about things for a lifetime; now, he only cared about the present. Experience told him that tomorrow was just a continuation of today, and tomorrow inherited today's grievances. After selling his padded clothes, he felt extremely relieved. What couldn't he do with the cash? Why keep it for a winter that could choke a person to death with a gust of wind?

Slowly, not only clothes, but he wanted to sell everything; anything temporarily unused was immediately sold. He liked to see his things turn into money and be spent by himself; if he spent it himself, it wouldn't fall into others' hands, which was the safest. Sell things, and then buy them when needed; if there was no money to buy, then simply do without. Not washing his face or brushing his teeth didn't matter much anymore; it not only saved money but also saved trouble. Who was he putting on a show for? Wearing ragged clothes, but eating pancakes rolled with braised meat in his stomach, that was real! With good things in his stomach, even if he died, there would be some fat, not like a starved mouse. Xiangzi, such a respectable Xiangzi, became a thin, dirty, low-class rickshaw puller. His face, body, and clothes, he didn't wash them, and sometimes he didn't shave his hair for over a month.

His rickshaw was no longer particular either; new or old, as long as the rental fee was small, it was fine. If he got a fare and there was a slight advantage, he would ditch it midway. If the passenger disagreed, he would glare, and if a fight broke out, spending two days at the police station was no big deal! Pulling the rickshaw alone, he walked very slowly; he cherished his sweat. When he joined a group of rickshaws, if he was happy, he would even run for a while, specifically to leave others behind. At such times, he was also very good at causing trouble, such as cutting off other rickshaws, deliberately making sharp turns, annoying the rickshaw behind him, or suddenly pushing the rickshaw in front of him. He used to think that pulling a rickshaw was pulling a human life, and a slight carelessness could lead to someone falling to their death. Now, he deliberately wanted to cause trouble; it didn't matter much who fell to their death, everyone was going to die! He had reverted to his silent and taciturn self. Without a word, he ate, he drank, he caused trouble.

Speech is for humans to exchange opinions and convey emotions; he had no opinions, no hope, so why talk? Except for bargaining, he kept his mouth shut all day long; his mouth seemed to be reserved solely for eating, drinking tea, and smoking. Even when drunk, he made no sound; he would sit in a secluded place and cry. Almost every time he got drunk, he would go to the woods where Little Fuzi hanged herself to shed tears; after crying, he would stay in the white house. When he sobered up, his money was gone, and he was sick. He didn't regret it; if he ever regretted anything, he regretted why he was so strong-willed, so cautious, so honest in the first place. All the things to regret were in the past; now there was nothing to regret.

Now, he would do whatever it took to gain an advantage. Smoking an extra cigarette from someone, using a fake copper coin to buy things, eating a few more pickles with his douzhi, exerting less effort while pulling a rickshaw to earn an extra copper coin or two, all made him feel satisfied. He gained an advantage, and others suffered a loss; yes, this was a form of revenge! Slowly expanding this, he also learned to borrow money from friends, and still didn't want to repay it; if pressed, he could act like a rogue. At first, no one doubted him at all; everyone knew he was a respectable and trustworthy person, so he would get the money as soon as he opened his mouth. He used this remnant of his character to borrow everywhere, borrowing as if picking up something for free, and spending it as soon as he got it. When people asked for repayment, he would put on a pitiful act to beg for an extension; if that didn't work, he would borrow another two dimes and repay one dime and five cents, drinking the remaining five cents first. Gradually, he couldn't borrow even a single copper coin, and he started to swindle money. He visited all the mansions he had previously frequented, whether it was the master or the servant, he would make up a lie to get some money; if there was no money, he would beg for some old clothes, and the clothes would immediately turn into money, and the money would immediately turn into cigarettes and alcohol. He lowered his head and pondered, thinking of bad ideas; if he thought of a good idea, he could earn more money than pulling a rickshaw for a day; it saved effort and earned money, which he found very cost-effective. He even went to find Gao Ma at the Cao residence. He waited far away for Gao Ma to come out to buy things, and when he saw her come out, he almost rushed over in one step, calling her "Sister Gao" in a very touching way. "Oh! You scared me to death! Who did I think it was? Xiangzi! How did you get like this?" Gao Ma's eyes widened, as if she had seen a monster.

"Don't even mention it!" Xiangzi lowered his head.

"Didn't you and the master agree on everything? Why did you leave and never come back? I even asked Old Cheng about you, and he said he hadn't seen you. Where did you go after all? The master and madam are both worried!"

"I was seriously ill, almost died! Please tell the master and help me out; I'll come back to work when I'm fully recovered!" Xiangzi said the words he had already prepared, simply and movingly.

"The master isn't home, would you like to come in and see the madam?"

"No need! Not in this state! Please tell her for me!"

Gao Ma took out two yuan for him: "The madam gave this to you, and told you to take some medicine quickly!"

"Alright! Thank you, madam!" Xiangzi took the money, wondering where to spend it. As soon as Gao Ma turned her back, he rushed to Tianqiao and played for a whole day.

Slowly, after visiting all the mansions, he went around a second time, but this time it was not as effective. He realized that this path couldn't be relied on for long; he had to think of another idea, an idea that would earn money more easily than pulling a rickshaw. Previously, his only hope was pulling a rickshaw; now, he hated pulling a rickshaw. Of course, he couldn't completely cut ties with rickshaws for a while, but as long as there was a way to temporarily make ends meet, he wouldn't touch the handlebars. His body was lazy, but his ears were sharp; if there was news, he would rush to the front. Whether it was a citizens' group or a petition group, he would do anything that involved someone paying money. Whether it was three dimes or two dimes, he was happy to wave a flag for a day, wandering with the crowd. He felt that this was better than pulling a rickshaw no matter what; it didn't earn much money, but he didn't have to exert himself. Holding a small flag, he lowered his head, a cigarette dangling from his mouth, walking with the crowd with a half-smile, not saying a word. When it was absolutely necessary to shout a few times, he would open his mouth wide, but make no sound; he cherished his voice. He didn't want to exert himself for anything, because he had exerted himself before and gained no benefit whatsoever. In such flag-waving and shouting situations, if he encountered any danger, he would be the first to run away, and he would run very fast. His life could be destroyed in his own hands, and he would no longer sacrifice anything for anyone. Those who strive for themselves also know how to destroy themselves; these are the two extremes of individualism.

[1]

 "Bèng'er" means ability, skill, or future.

[2]

 "Yáodiào" refers to popular tunes in brothels.

Learning resource sharing twenty-four

It was again time for pilgrimages to the temple, and the weather suddenly became scorching hot.

It seemed that all the paper fan sellers suddenly emerged from somewhere, carrying boxes, the bells on their boxes jingling, attracting attention. By the roadside, green apricots were already being sold in piles, cherries were eye-catchingly red, golden bees swarmed over basins of rose dates, and glass powder lay in layers of milky luster in large porcelain basins. The stalls selling "baga" (steamed millet cake) and "liangfen" (jelly noodles) were neatly arranged, displaying various colored seasonings. People had also changed into light-colored and gaudy single-layer clothes, and the streets suddenly gained many colors, like many rainbows scattered among humans. The street sweepers worked harder, constantly sprinkling water on the roads, but the light dust still flew up, making people irritable. Yet, amidst the light dust, there were also long willow branches and nimble, active swallows, which made people feel refreshed. It was a kind of weather that made people feel inexplicably good; everyone yawned lazily, feeling tired yet invigorated.

Yangko dances, lion dances, road-opening ceremonies, Five Tiger Stick performances, and various other gatherings all went up the mountain one after another. Beating gongs and drums, carrying boxes and baskets, waving apricot-yellow flags, one group followed another, giving the whole city an unusual excitement, giving people vague yet intimate feelings, and leaving sounds and dust in the air. Those attending and those watching all felt some enthusiasm, piety, and excitement. The liveliness of troubled times came from superstition; the only comfort for fools was self-deception. These colors, these sounds, the clear clouds filling the sky, the dust on the street, gave people spirit and things to do: some went up the mountain, some visited temples, some admired flowers... The least fortunate could still watch the excitement by the roadside and recite a few Buddhist prayers.

As soon as the weather turned hot, it seemed to awaken the spring dream of the old capital; there were places to play everywhere, everyone thought of things to do, and the temperature urged flowers, plants, fruits, and human enjoyment to grow together. The green willows and new reeds in the North and South Seas attracted teenagers playing harmonicas; boys and girls put their small boats under the willow shade or drifted among the tender lotus, singing love songs, their eyes and eyebrows even kissing. The peonies and tree peonies in the park invited poets and refined scholars, who strolled leisurely, fanning themselves with expensive paper fans; when they got tired, they would drink a few cups of clear tea, enough to evoke idle melancholy, in front of red walls and under green pines, secretly glancing at the passing young ladies from prominent families and famous beauties from the north and south. Even those usually quiet places were brought visitors by the gentle breeze and sunny days, just as butterflies were brought. The peonies of Chongxiao Temple, the green reeds of Taoranting, the mulberry groves and rice paddies of the Natural History Museum, all attracted human voices and umbrella shadows; even the Temple of Heaven, the Confucius Temple, and the Yonghe Temple had a slight liveliness amidst their solemnity. Those who loved to travel and students went to the Western Hills, to the hot springs, to the Summer Palace, to travel, to wander, to collect, to scribble on rocks. The poor also had places to go; Huguo Temple, Longfu Temple, Baitasi, Tudimiao, and Hua'er Market were all livelier than usual: various wild flowers were brightly displayed by the roadside, and for one or two copper coins, "beauty" could be brought home. At the douzhi stalls, the pickles were as colorful as large flowers, with焦红 (scorched red) chili peppers at their tips."""Chickens are cheap, and the fried egg corners, golden and tender, make people salivate. Tianqiao is even more bustling, with newly built tea sheds, one after another, white tablecloths, and enchanting singing girls, facing the old pines on the walls of the Temple of Heaven. The sound of gongs and drums extends for seven or eight hours, and the dry weather makes the gongs and drums particularly crisp, unsettling people's hearts. Prostitutes are easily dressed up; a single floral print dress can make them look beautiful and clearly reveal the curves of their bodies. Those who prefer quiet places also have their destinations: by the accumulated water, outside Wanshou Temple, the kiln pits in the eastern suburbs, and Baishi Bridge in the western suburbs, where they can fish, and small fish occasionally make the tender reeds sway slightly. After fishing, the pig's head meat, boiled tofu, baijiu, and salted beans at the wild tea houses can also make one drunk and full; then, carrying fishing rods and small fish, along the willow banks, stepping into the sunset, they leisurely enter the ancient city gates.

Everywhere is fun, everywhere is lively, everywhere is vibrant. A sudden heatwave in early summer is like a magic charm, making this old city full of magic. It disregards death, misfortune, and hardship; when the time comes, it exerts its power, hypnotizing millions of hearts, making them sing its praises as if in a dream. It is dirty, it is beautiful, it is old, it is lively, it is chaotic, it is peaceful, it is lovely, it is the great Beiping of early summer.

It is at this time that people long for news that can relieve boredom, news that can be read two or three times without tiring, news that can be personally witnessed after reading the newspaper, for the sky is so long and clear!

Such news arrived! As the tram just left the factory, the newspaper boy was already shouting with his sharp voice, chasing people everywhere: "News of Ruan Ming's execution, news of his parade at nine o'clock!" One copper coin, one copper coin, and another copper coin were all taken by the small black hands. On the tram, in shops, in the hands of pedestrians, every single sheet was about Ruan Ming: Ruan Ming's photo, Ruan Ming's history, Ruan Ming's interviews, large and small print, illustrated captions, entire pages were all about Ruan Ming. Ruan Ming was on the tram, in the eyes of pedestrians, in the mouths of conversationalists; it seemed there was no one else in the old city, only Ruan Ming; Ruan Ming was paraded today, executed today! Valuable news, ideal news, not only speaking of Ruan Ming but also being able to see him in a while. Women rushed to dress up; old people went out early, fearing their legs would be slow and they would fall behind; even schoolchildren wanted to skip half a day of school to witness it. By half past eight, the streets were already full of people, excited, hopeful, crowded, noisy, waiting to see this living news. Rickshaw pullers forgot to solicit business, shops broke their rules, hawkers were too lazy to shout, all awaiting the prisoner's cart and Ruan Ming. A nation whose history included Huang Chao, Zhang Xianzhong, and the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom, would endure killings and also loved to watch killings. Execution by firing squad seemed too simple; they loved to hear of slow slicing, beheading, flaying, live burial, listening to it felt like eating ice cream, a shiver of pleasure. But this time, in addition to the execution, there was a parade, and they almost wanted to thank the person who came up with such an idea, allowing them to see a half-dead person tied to a cart, entertaining their eyes; even if they weren't the execution supervisor, they were close enough. These people had no sense of right or wrong, did not understand good and evil, could not distinguish between right and wrong; they clung tightly to some moral codes, wishing to be called civilized people; yet they loved to watch their own kind be tortured to death, as cruelly and pleasurably as a child slaughtering a puppy. Once power was in their hands, any of them would massacre a city, cutting off women's breasts and feet to pile them into small mountains; this was their quick pleasure. Since they didn't have this authority, they might as well watch more pig and sheep slaughtering and human killings to get a thrill. If they couldn't even watch this, they would curse a child with a thousand cuts, ten thousand cuts, to vent their inner malice.

A clear blue sky, a high red sun in the east, a few gusts of light east wind, the willow branches by the roadside swaying slightly. There was a large shadow on the eastern side road, crowded with people: old and young, men and women, ugly and beautiful, fat and thin, some dressed fashionably, some just wearing short jackets, all chatting, hoping, constantly craning their necks to the south or north. One person craned their neck, and everyone followed, their hearts beating a little faster. Thus, they pushed forward more and more, the crowd gradually squeezing to the roadside, forming a wall of flesh, with only the uneven heads moving chaotically. Patrol police came out in squads to maintain order; they blocked, they shouted, and sometimes they even grabbed a mud-like child and gave him a couple of punches, drawing laughter from everyone. Waiting, patiently waiting, their legs were already sore, but they were unwilling to go back empty-handed; those in front refused to move, and new arrivals from behind pushed forward, causing disputes, with no physical action, only verbal battles, mutual insults, and everyone cheering. Children grew impatient and were slapped by adults; pickpockets succeeded, and those who lost their belongings cursed loudly. Noise, shouting, a cacophony, no one willing to move, the crowd grew, and no one was more unwilling to move, showing a unanimous desire to see the half-dead prisoner.

Suddenly, everyone fell silent, and a squad of armed police approached from afar. "They're here!" someone shouted. Immediately, the crowd became chaotic, the entire mass of people surged forward an inch, then another inch, like a machine. They're here! They're here! Everyone's eyes lit up, and they were all saying something, a cacophony of human voices, the entire street reeking of sweat, the people of a land of propriety eagerly loving to watch killings.

Ruan Ming was a short man, his hands tied behind his back, sitting in the cart like a sick little monkey; his head was bowed, and a two-foot-long white banner was stuck behind his back. The human voices were like ocean waves, the front pushing the back, everyone pouting slightly in criticism, somewhat disappointed: "Is that all? Just this little monkey! So weak and spiritless! Head bowed, face pale, not saying a word!" Some people had an idea, wanting to provoke him: "Brothers, let's cheer for him!" Immediately, shouts of "Bravo!" came from all directions, like cheering for an actress on stage, contemptuous, malicious, annoying. Ruan Ming still made no sound, not even lifting his head. Some people were truly anxious, truly looked down on such a weak prisoner, squeezing to the roadside and spitting at him a few times. Ruan Ming still didn't move, showing no reaction. Everyone grew more and more bored, but also more reluctant to leave; what if he suddenly said, "Twenty years later, I'll be a good man again"? What if he asked the tavern for two pots of baijiu and a plate of braised meat? No one was willing to move, wanting to see what he would do. The cart passed, but they still followed; he showed no reaction now, but who knew if he would regain his composure at Danpailou and sing a few lines of "Si Lang Tan Mu"? Follow him! Some followed all the way to Tianqiao; although he never did anything admirable or satisfying, people watched him get shot, so it could be considered a worthwhile trip after all.

At such a lively time, Xiangzi walked slowly by himself, head bowed, along the base of the Deshengmen city wall. When he reached Jishuitan, he looked around. There was no one, and he slowly, stealthily, went towards the lake. Reaching the lakeside, he found an old tree, leaned his back against the trunk, and stood for a while. Hearing no human sounds around, he gently sat down. The reed leaves rustled slightly, or a small bird suddenly chirped, making him quickly stand up, sweat appearing on his forehead. He listened, he watched, there was no movement around, and he slowly sat down again. After several such instances, he began to get used to the slight movement of the reed leaves and the chirping of birds, deciding not to panic anymore. He stared blankly at the ditch outside the lake, where some small fish, their eyes bright like tiny pearls, gathered and dispersed, came and went; sometimes their heads topped a tender duckweed, sometimes they spat out some bubbles. Near the ditch, some tadpoles that had already grown legs stood upright, wiggling their black, large heads. The water suddenly flowed faster, washing away the small fish and tadpoles, their tails askew as they drifted downstream, but with the water came another group, struggling to stop. A water scorpion scurried past very quickly. The current gradually stabilized, and the small fish formed a team again, opening their small mouths to nibble on a floating green leaf or a piece of grass. Slightly larger fish hid in the depths, occasionally showing their backs, quickly turning to go down, leaving a whirlpool and some ripples on the water surface. Kingfishers darted across the water like arrows, and both small and large fish disappeared, leaving only duckweed on the water. Xiangzi stared blankly at these, seemingly seeing, yet seemingly not seeing, unconsciously picking up a small stone and throwing it into the water, splashing some water and scattering many duckweeds, he was suddenly startled, almost standing up again.

After sitting for a long time, he secretly reached for his waist with his large black hand. He nodded, his hand resting there; after a while, he took out a wad of banknotes, counted them, and then carefully hid them back in their original place.

His heart was entirely driven by that money: how to spend it, how to keep others from knowing, how to enjoy it safely. He was no longer thinking for himself; he had become an appendage of money, everything subject to its control.

The origin of this money had already determined its destination. Such money could not be spent openly and honestly. This money, and the person holding it, dared not see the light of day. People were watching Ruan Ming on the street, while Xiangzi hid by the quiet city wall, trying to find an even quieter, darker place. He dared not walk on the streets anymore, because he had sold Ruan Ming. Even when facing the quiet flowing water alone, leaning against the deserted city wall, he dared not look up, as if a ghost always followed him. Ruan Ming, who fell in a pool of blood at Tianqiao, lived in Xiangzi's heart, and in the banknotes at his waist. He did not regret it, he was just afraid, afraid of that ghost that followed him everywhere, at all times.

After Ruan Ming became an official, he enjoyed many things he had previously considered worthy of overthrow. Money can lead people into a corrupt society, abandoning noble ideals and willingly descending into hell. He wore splendid Western clothes, went whoring, gambling, and even smoked opium. When his conscience awakened, he believed it was this evil society that had entrapped him, and not entirely his own fault; he admitted his actions were wrong, but blamed society's overwhelming temptation, which he could not resist. Gradually, his money ran out, and he remembered those radical ideas again, but not to act on them; he wanted to use ideas to make some money. Turning ideas into money was like trying to get passing grades in school by simply associating with the teacher. The ideas of lazy people cannot stand alongside integrity; anything that can be exchanged for money will eventually be sold. He received a subsidy. Organizations eager to promote revolution could not be extremely careful in selecting fighters; anyone willing to join was considered a comrade. However, those receiving subsidies had to show some results, no matter what means were used to achieve them; the organization wanted reports. Ruan Ming could not just take money without doing anything. He participated in organizing rickshaw pullers. Xiangzi, on the other hand, was an old hand at waving flags and shouting slogans; thus, Ruan Ming got to know Xiangzi.

Ruan Ming sold his ideas for money; Xiangzi accepted ideas for money. Ruan Ming knew that when necessary, he could sacrifice Xiangzi. Xiangzi had never made such a plan, but when the time came, he did it—he sold Ruan Ming. Those who work for money are afraid of encountering more money; loyalty is not based on money. Ruan Ming believed in his own ideas, excusing all his bad behavior with the radicalism of his thoughts. Xiangzi listened to what Ruan Ming said, finding it very reasonable, but also envied Ruan Ming's enjoyment—"If I had more money, I would also be happy for a few days! Just like that Ruan fellow!" Money degraded Ruan Ming's character, and money dazzled Xiangzi's eyes. He sold Ruan Ming for sixty yuan. Ruan Ming wanted the power of the masses; Xiangzi wanted more—enjoyment like Ruan Ming's. Ruan Ming's blood was spilled on the subsidy, and Xiangzi stuffed the banknotes into his waist.

Xiangzi sat until the sun began to set, and the reeds and willows by the lake were tinged with golden-red light, before he stood up and walked west along the city wall. Cheating people out of money was something he was used to; selling a human life was a first. Moreover, what Ruan Ming had said still seemed very reasonable to him! The emptiness of the city wall and its towering height made him more and more afraid as he walked. Occasionally seeing a few old crows on a garbage heap, he wanted to walk around them, fearing he might startle them and receive some inauspicious caws. When he reached the west city wall, he quickened his pace, like a dog that had stolen food, and slipped out of Xizhimen. Having someone to accompany him at night, to numb him, to make him unafraid, was an ideal destination; the White House was such an ideal place.

As autumn set in, Xiangzi's illness no longer allowed him to pull a rickshaw, and his credit was so lost that he couldn't rent one. He became a patron of a small shop. At night, with two copper coins, he could lie down in the shop. During the day, he did work that only allowed him to drink a bowl of porridge. He couldn't beg on the street; with his large build, no one would take pity on him. He wouldn't put on makeup to beg for money at temple fairs, because he hadn't been taught and didn't know how to disguise his sores as moving misfortunes. He also didn't have the skills to be a thief; thieves also had their organizations and connections. Only he himself could earn his own food, without any other reliance or assistance. He worked hard for himself, and he also completed his own death. He waited to draw his last breath; he was a living ghost with breath still in him, and individualism was his soul.This soul will rot in the earth along with his body.

Since Beiping was designated as the ancient capital, its pomp, craftsmanship, food, language, police... have gradually flowed outwards, seeking places with the same prestige and financial resources as the emperor to lend their support. The Westernized Qingdao now has Beiping's instant-boiled mutton; in bustling Tianjin, one can hear the low, mournful cry of "hard-dough steamed buns" in the middle of the night; in Shanghai, Hankou, and Nanjing, there are also police and yamen runners speaking Beijing dialect and eating sesame paste flatbreads; jasmine tea travels from south to north, passing through double-smoking in Beiping before heading south again; even coffin bearers sometimes take trains to Tianjin or Nanjing to carry the coffins of high officials and dignitaries.

Beiping itself, however, is gradually losing its original grandeur. Flower cakes can still be bought in pastry shops after the ninth day of the ninth month, sellers of yuanxiao might be off the market in autumn, and those two- or three-hundred-year-old shops suddenly remember to celebrate anniversaries, using the opportunity to distribute flyers for big sales... Economic pressure forces grandeur to find new avenues, as dignity cannot feed one. However, red and white affairs (weddings and funerals) largely retain their old rituals and splendor; weddings and funerals still seem to be worth noting, and require some pomp. The attendants, musical instruments, wedding sedan chairs, and official canopies for weddings and funerals are still unmatched by any other city. The pine cranes and pine lions used for funerals, the paper-mâché figures, sedan chairs, and horses, and the full set of attendants and twenty-four musical instruments used for weddings, still display an official grandeur on the streets, reminding people of the prosperity and elegance of peaceful times.

Xiangzi's life largely depended on these remaining rituals and rules. When there was a wedding, he would hold the flag and umbrella for others; when there was a funeral, he would carry wreaths and elegiac couplets for others; he neither rejoiced nor wept, he only accompanied people parading through the streets for those dozen coppers. Dressed in the green uniform or blue robe provided by the coffin or wedding sedan chair shop, wearing an ill-fitting black hat, he could temporarily cover his rags and appear somewhat respectable. When a wealthy family held an event, requiring everyone to shave their heads and wear boots, he would have the opportunity to be clean and tidy from head to toe for once. His chronic illness made it difficult for him to walk, so he would just hold a flag or two elegiac couplets, slowly shuffling along the roadside.

However, even for this small task, he was not considered skilled. His golden age was over; having failed to establish a family or career through rickshaw pulling, everything had turned into "just that" as his hopes faded. Despite his large build, he insisted on carrying a small flying tiger flag or a pair of short, narrow elegiac couplets; he refused to touch the heavier red umbrellas or "肅靜" (silence) signs. He would even argue with old people, children, or even women. He refused to suffer any loss.

Holding such a small item, he would lower his head, hunch his back, and clench a cigarette butt he had picked up from the road in his mouth, slowly shuffling along listlessly. When everyone stopped, he might still be walking; when everyone had left, he might still stand for a while; he seemed not to hear the gongs signaling commands. He never looked to see if the front and back distances were even, or if the left and right lines were neat; he walked his own way, head down, as if in a dream, or contemplating some profound truth. The red-clad gong-beater and the attendant holding the silk flag, who urged people on, cursed him with almost every vulgarity: "Grandson! I'm talking to you, Camel! You damn well get in line!" He still seemed not to hear. The gong-beater walked over and gave him a whack with the gong stick; he rolled his eyes, vaguely looking around. Regardless of what the gong-beater said, he carefully searched the ground, looking for any cigarette butts worth picking up. Respectable, strong-willed, dream-filled, self-serving, individualistic, robust, great Xiangzi, who knows how many funerals he accompanied; who knows when and where he himself will be buried, burying this fallen, selfish, unfortunate product of a sick society, this last ghost of individualism!
